### 本测试块考点概述 (Block Overview):
本测试块涵盖吞噬细胞缺陷、补体系统缺陷与其他综合征性免疫缺陷：高IgE综合征（Job syndrome：STAT3常显突变，Th17细胞分化受阻，严重湿疹、复发性“冷”葡萄球菌脓肿无红热肿痛反应、保留双排乳牙、极高IgE）；Wiskott-Aldrich综合征（WAS：X连锁WAS基因突变，肌动蛋白细胞骨架重组障碍，经典三联征WATER——Wiskott-Aldrich、Thrombocytopenia血小板减少伴微小血小板小体积、Eczema严重湿疹、Recurrent infections反复荚膜菌化脓性感染，IgA/IgE升高而IgM降低）；共济失调毛细血管扩张症（ATM基因双链DNA断裂修复缺陷，小脑共济失调、眼球结膜毛细血管扩张、甲胎蛋白AFP显著升高）；白细胞粘附缺陷症（LAD-1：CD18突变）；慢性肉芽肿病（CGD：NADPH氧化酶缺陷，过氧化氢酶阳性菌金葡菌、洋葱伯克霍尔德菌、沙雷菌、诺卡菌、曲霉菌反复肉芽肿感染，DHR二氢罗丹明123流式细胞术绿色荧光缺如或NBT四氮唑蓝还原试验阴性）；Cedo-Higashi综合征（LYST溶酶体转运基因突变，巨大中性粒细胞溶酶体颗粒、局部白化病、外周神经病变）；终末补体MAC复合物缺陷（C5-C9缺陷导致脑膜炎奈瑟菌败血症脑膜炎反复发生）。

<a id='question-101'></a>

### Question 101: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 01 (Deficiency)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of primary immunodeficiencies, specifically focusing on the molecular defect and resulting clinical manifestations of X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA), also known as Bruton's agammaglobulinemia, caused by mutations in the *BTK* gene, leading to a deficiency in B cell development and subsequent lack of immunoglobulins. (本题考察原发性免疫缺陷病，特别是布鲁顿无γ球蛋白血症 (XLA)，由 *BTK* 基因突变引起，导致 B 细胞发育缺陷和免疫球蛋白缺乏。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents to his pediatrician with recurrent sinopulmonary infections, including pneumonia, otitis media, and sinusitis, starting around 6 months of age after maternal antibodies waned. He has not received any live vaccines due to concerns about potential complications. Physical examination reveals a normal-appearing child, but his tonsils and adenoids are notably small. Laboratory investigations show markedly reduced levels of all immunoglobulin isotypes (IgG, IgA, IgM, IgE) and a severe deficiency of peripheral B cells (<1% of lymphocytes). T cell numbers and function appear normal. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes reveals a block in B cell development at the pre-B cell stage in the bone marrow. Genetic testing confirms a mutation in the *BTK* gene on the X chromosome. The child is subsequently treated with intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIG) replacement therapy. Despite IVIG, he develops a severe, persistent enteroviral infection, requiring hospitalization and intensive supportive care.

Which of the following best explains the underlying molecular defect responsible for this patient's immunodeficiency?

(A) Defective recombination of V(D)J segments due to mutations in the *RAG1* or *RAG2* genes.
(B) Impaired T cell maturation due to mutations in the *CD3* signaling complex genes.
(C) Failure of B cell maturation beyond the pre-B cell stage due to mutations in the *BTK* gene.
(D) Defective phagocyte function due to mutations in the *CYBB* gene encoding gp91phox.
(E) Impaired T cell and B cell interaction due to mutations in the *CD40LG* gene.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent sinopulmonary infections starting at 6 months:** Suggests a humoral immunity defect, as maternal IgG protection wanes around this time. Common in B cell deficiencies.
- **Small tonsils/adenoids:** Lymphoid tissues dependent on B cells are underdeveloped due to lack of B cells.
- **Markedly reduced levels of all immunoglobulin isotypes (IgG, IgA, IgM, IgE):** Confirms a severe humoral immunodeficiency.
- **Severe deficiency of peripheral B cells (<1%):** Points to a defect in B cell development or survival.
- **Normal T cell numbers and function:** Rules out combined immunodeficiencies (like SCID) or primary T cell defects.
- **Flow cytometry showing a block in B cell development at the pre-B cell stage:** Pinpoints the specific stage where B cell development fails.
- **Mutation in the *BTK* gene on the X chromosome:** Identifies the specific genetic cause.
- **Treatment with IVIG:** Standard therapy for antibody deficiency, replacing missing antibodies.
- **Severe, persistent enteroviral infection despite IVIG:** Enteroviruses are RNA viruses that typically cause mild or asymptomatic infections. Severe infections, especially in the CNS or heart, are characteristic of patients with defects in *humoral immunity* (specifically antibody production) and *cellular immunity* (T cell response), or both. While IVIG replaces antibodies, it does not provide T cell immunity. The persistence suggests a potential underlying T cell defect or a pathogen that requires both arms of immunity for clearance, or simply overwhelming the passive immunity provided by IVIG. However, the primary defect described is B cell development. The question asks for the *underlying molecular defect*.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA) / Bruton's agammaglobulinemia:** A primary immunodeficiency characterized by a failure of pre-B cells to mature into mature B cells.
- ***BTK* gene:** Encodes Bruton's tyrosine kinase, a cytoplasmic tyrosine kinase crucial for B cell receptor (BCR) signaling.
- **Mechanism:** BTK is essential for the signaling cascade initiated by the pre-BCR (μ heavy chain surrogate) and BCR. This signaling is required for the transition from pre-B cell to immature B cell, including V(D)J recombination completion, allelic exclusion, surface immunoglobulin expression, and survival. Mutations in *BTK* lead to a block at the pre-B cell stage in the bone marrow, resulting in a profound lack of mature B cells and consequently, very low levels of all immunoglobulin isotypes.
- **Clinical Features:** Recurrent bacterial infections (especially encapsulated organisms like *Streptococcus pneumoniae*, *Haemophilus influenzae*) starting after 6 months of age, hypogammaglobulinemia, absent or very low B cells, normal T cells, small or absent tonsils/lymph nodes/adenoids.
- **Enteroviral infections:** While typically mild, severe enteroviral infections (e.g., meningoencephalitis, myocarditis, paralysis) can occur in patients with antibody deficiencies, as antibodies are important for neutralizing these viruses. The persistence despite IVIG might indicate that T cell responses are also crucial for controlling this specific infection, or that the IVIG levels were insufficient, or that the virus has developed resistance, but the core defect remains the lack of B cells/antibodies due to the *BTK* mutation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective recombination of V(D)J segments due to mutations in the *RAG1* or *RAG2* genes:** This describes Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID) due to RAG deficiency. RAG1/RAG2 are essential for V(D)J recombination, which occurs in both T and B cell development. This leads to a lack of both T and B cells (T- B- NK+ SCID). The vignette clearly states normal T cell numbers and function, ruling this out.
- (B) **Impaired T cell maturation due to mutations in the *CD3* signaling complex genes:** Mutations in genes encoding components of the CD3 signaling complex (e.g., *CD3D*, *CD3E*, *CD3G*) cause T cell deficiencies. CD3 is crucial for T cell receptor (TCR) signaling. This would result in low or absent T cells and impaired T cell function, contradicting the vignette's finding of normal T cells.
- (D) **Defective phagocyte function due to mutations in the *CYBB* gene encoding gp91phox:** This describes Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD). CGD affects NADPH oxidase, impairing the ability of phagocytes (neutrophils, macrophages) to produce reactive oxygen species needed to kill certain bacteria and fungi. Patients typically present with recurrent infections by catalase-positive organisms (e.g., *Staphylococcus aureus*, *Aspergillus*) and granuloma formation. This does not fit the clinical picture of absent B cells and hypogammaglobulinemia.
- (E) **Impaired T cell and B cell interaction due to mutations in the *CD40LG* gene:** This describes X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome (HIGM1). CD40 ligand (CD40L or CD154) on activated T cells binds to CD40 on B cells, antigen-presenting cells, and other cells. This interaction is crucial for B cell class switching, somatic hypermutation, and germinal center formation. Patients have normal or elevated IgM levels but deficient IgG, IgA, and IgE. They also have normal B cell numbers but impaired antibody responses. This contradicts the vignette's findings of absent B cells and low levels of all immunoglobulin isotypes.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | XLA (BTK defect)                 | RAG Deficiency (SCID)         | CD3 Deficiency (T- cell defect) | CGD (CYBB defect)           | HIGM1 (CD40L defect)        |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------- | :---------------------------- | :----------------------------- | :-------------------------- | :-------------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | B cell development (pre-B stage) | V(D)J recombination (T & B)   | T cell maturation/signaling    | Phagocyte oxidative burst   | T-B interaction/class switch |
| **Cell Types Affected** | B cells                          | T cells, B cells              | T cells                        | Phagocytes                  | B cells (function)           |
| **B Cell Count**        | Absent/Very Low (<1%)            | Absent/Very Low               | Normal                         | Normal                     | Normal                     |
| **T Cell Count**        | Normal                           | Absent/Very Low               | Absent/Very Low                | Normal                     | Normal                     |
| **Immunoglobulins**     | All low (IgG, IgA, IgM, IgE)      | All low                      | Normal (usually)               | Normal                     | High IgM, Low IgG/IgA/IgE   |
| **Gene Affected**       | *BTK*                             | *RAG1*, *RAG2*                | *CD3D*, *CD3E*, *CD3G*          | *CYBB*                     | *CD40LG*                   |
| **Typical Infections**  | Encapsulated bacteria            | Opportunistic (viral, fungal) | Opportunistic (viral, fungal) | Catalase+ bacteria, fungi | Opportunistic (Pneumocystis) |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA) is caused by mutations in the *BTK* gene, leading to a block in B cell development at the pre-B cell stage, resulting in absent B cells and panhypogammaglobulinemia. Key differentials include other primary immunodeficiencies affecting B cells (like HIGM1, which has normal B cell numbers but impaired function) or combined immunodeficiencies (like SCID, which affects both T and B cells). Remember the specific gene (*BTK*) and the stage of B cell development blocked (pre-B cell).

### Question 102: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 02 (Bruton)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: X-linked agammaglobulinemia (Bruton's agammaglobulinemia) is characterized by a defect in B-cell development due to mutations in the *BTK* gene, leading to a profound deficiency of mature B cells and immunoglobulins, resulting in recurrent bacterial infections, particularly with encapsulated organisms. (X-linked 丙球蛋白缺乏症（布鲁顿氏病）的特点是 *BTK* 基因突变导致 B 细胞发育缺陷，从而导致成熟 B 细胞和免疫球蛋白的严重缺乏，进而引起反复的细菌感染，特别是与荚膜细菌有关。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents to the pediatrician with recurrent otitis media, sinusitis, and pneumonia, starting around 6 months of age after maternal antibodies waned. He has not received any live vaccines due to concerns about potential complications. Physical examination reveals normal growth and development, but no palpable lymph nodes or tonsils. Laboratory studies show markedly reduced serum levels of all immunoglobulin isotypes (IgG, IgA, IgM, IgE) and a complete absence of peripheral blood B cells (CD19+ cells) as determined by flow cytometry. T-cell numbers (CD3+ cells) and function are normal. His older brother died at age 5 from complications of sepsis following a severe respiratory infection. The patient's mother reports that her father (the patient's maternal grandfather) also had recurrent infections as a child.

Which of the following cellular components is most likely deficient in this patient?

(A) CD4+ T helper cells
(B) CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes
(C) B-cell receptor (BCR) signaling molecules
(D) Natural killer (NK) cells
(E) Complement component C3

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 3-year-old boy with recurrent bacterial infections (otitis media, sinusitis, pneumonia) starting after 6 months (when maternal IgG wanes). This pattern is classic for antibody deficiencies.
- **Family History:** Older brother died young from sepsis, and maternal grandfather had recurrent infections. This suggests an X-linked inheritance pattern, as the disease is passed from mother to son.
- **Physical Exam:** Absence of palpable lymph nodes and tonsils suggests lymphoid hypoplasia, often seen in severe B-cell deficiencies.
- **Laboratory Findings:**
    - Markedly reduced serum immunoglobulins (IgG, IgA, IgM, IgE): Indicates a severe defect in antibody production.
    - Absence of peripheral blood B cells (CD19+): Points to a block in B-cell development.
    - Normal T-cell numbers (CD3+) and function: Rules out combined immunodeficiencies (like SCID) or primary T-cell defects.
- **Diagnosis:** The clinical presentation (recurrent bacterial infections, onset after 6 months), family history (X-linked pattern), physical findings (lymphoid hypoplasia), and laboratory results (panhypogammaglobulinemia, absent B cells, normal T cells) are pathognomonic for X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA), also known as Bruton's agammaglobulinemia.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the deficient cellular component. XLA is caused by a mutation in the *BTK* (Bruton's tyrosine kinase) gene, which is crucial for B-cell receptor (BCR) signaling during B-cell development in the bone marrow. This defect prevents pre-B cells from maturing into immature B cells and subsequently into mature B cells. Therefore, the deficient component is related to BCR signaling.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **X-linked Agammaglobulinemia (XLA):**
    - **Genetics:** Caused by mutations in the *BTK* gene located on the X chromosome.
    - **Pathophysiology:** BTK is a cytoplasmic tyrosine kinase essential for the signaling pathway downstream of the pre-BCR and BCR. It phosphorylates downstream targets like Syk, which is critical for B-cell development beyond the pre-B cell stage. Without functional BTK, B-cell development arrests at the pre-B cell stage in the bone marrow, leading to a profound lack of mature B cells in the periphery.
    - **Immunological Consequences:** Absence of mature B cells results in the inability to produce antibodies (immunoglobulins). This leads to panhypogammaglobulinemia (low levels of all Ig isotypes). T-cell immunity remains intact.
    - **Clinical Manifestations:** Recurrent infections, primarily with encapsulated bacteria (e.g., *Streptococcus pneumoniae*, *Haemophilus influenzae*, *Staphylococcus aureus*), starting after maternal antibodies decline (around 6 months). Common sites include the respiratory tract (sinusitis, otitis media, pneumonia), skin, and bloodstream (sepsis). Patients are also susceptible to certain viruses (enteroviruses) and protozoa (e.g., *Giardia lamblia*). Lymphoid tissues (lymph nodes, tonsils, spleen) are often underdeveloped due to the lack of B cells.
    - **Diagnosis:** Low/absent serum immunoglobulins, absent peripheral B cells (CD19+), normal T-cell numbers and function, and *BTK* gene mutation confirmation.
    - **Treatment:** Intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIG) replacement therapy to provide passive immunity and prevent infections. Hematopoietic stem cell transplantation can be curative.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) CD4+ T helper cells: Deficiency in CD4+ T cells is characteristic of DiGeorge syndrome (22q11.2 deletion) or severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID), leading to impaired cell-mediated and humoral immunity. This patient has normal T-cell numbers and function. Incorrect.
- (B) CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes: Deficiency in CD8+ T cells is less common as an isolated primary immunodeficiency but can occur in certain forms of SCID or specific T-cell defects. This patient has normal T-cell numbers and function. Incorrect.
- (D) Natural killer (NK) cells: Deficiency in NK cells is seen in specific primary immunodeficiencies (e.g., familial hemophagocytic lymphohistiocytosis type 2) or secondary conditions. This patient's presentation is classic for an antibody deficiency, not an NK cell defect. Incorrect.
- (E) Complement component C3: Deficiency in C3 (or other early complement components like C1q, C2, C4) leads to increased susceptibility to infections with encapsulated bacteria (similar to antibody deficiencies) and autoimmune diseases (like SLE). However, complement deficiency does not cause a lack of B cells or panhypogammaglobulinemia. Patients with complement deficiency typically have normal B-cell numbers and immunoglobulin levels. Incorrect.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | X-linked Agammaglobulinemia (XLA) | Common Variable Immunodeficiency (CVID) | DiGeorge Syndrome | Complement Deficiency (e.g., C3) |
|-------------------------|-----------------------------------|----------------------------------------|-------------------|-----------------------------------|
| **Genetic Basis**       | *BTK* gene (X-linked)             | Variable (often unknown)               | 22q11.2 deletion  | Variable (autosomal)              |
| **B-cell Count**        | Absent/Very Low                   | Normal or Low                          | Normal            | Normal                            |
| **Immunoglobulins**     | Very Low/Absent (all isotypes)    | Low (at least one isotype)             | Normal            | Normal                            |
| **T-cell Count/Function**| Normal                            | Normal                                 | Low/Absent (Thymic hypoplasia) | Normal                            |
| **Onset**               | ~6 months                          | Variable (often later childhood/adulthood) | Birth             | Variable                          |
| **Key Defect**          | B-cell development arrest          | Impaired B-cell differentiation/survival | T-cell development | Complement cascade defect         |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
XLA is a primary immunodeficiency caused by a defect in B-cell development due to *BTK* mutations, leading to absent B cells and panhypogammaglobulinemia. Key differentials include CVID (normal B cells, impaired function), DiGeorge syndrome (T-cell defect), and complement deficiencies (normal B cells, normal immunoglobulins). Remember the X-linked inheritance pattern and the specific defect in BCR signaling.

<a id='question-103'></a>

### Question 103: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 03 (tyrosine)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of primary immunodeficiencies, specifically focusing on defects in tyrosine kinase signaling pathways, particularly Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK) deficiency (X-linked agammaglobulinemia, XLA), and its impact on B cell development and function, leading to recurrent bacterial infections and impaired antibody responses. (本题考察原发性免疫缺陷病，特别是酪氨酸激酶信号通路缺陷，如 Bruton 酪氨酸激酶 (BTK) 缺乏症 (X 连锁丙种球蛋白缺乏症, XLA)，及其对 B 细胞发育和功能的影响，导致反复细菌感染和抗体反应受损。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with recurrent sinopulmonary infections, including pneumonia, otitis media, and sinusitis, starting around 6 months of age after maternal antibody protection waned. He has a history of multiple hospitalizations for severe bacterial infections, including *Streptococcus pneumoniae* and *Haemophilus influenzae*. Physical examination reveals a small tonsillar shadow and absence of palpable lymph nodes. Laboratory investigations show markedly reduced serum levels of all immunoglobulin isotypes (IgG, IgA, IgM, IgE) and a severely decreased number of circulating B lymphocytes (<1% of total lymphocytes). Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes reveals a block in B cell development at the pre-B cell stage in the bone marrow. Genetic testing confirms a mutation in the *BTK* gene located on the X chromosome. The patient is being considered for hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (HSCT). Which of the following cellular processes is most directly impaired in this patient due to the underlying genetic defect?

(A) T cell receptor (TCR) signaling required for T cell activation and cytokine production.
(B) B cell receptor (BCR) signaling required for B cell maturation, proliferation, and differentiation.
(C) Complement component C3 activation, leading to impaired opsonization and lysis of encapsulated bacteria.
(D) Phagocytosis by neutrophils, leading to decreased clearance of extracellular bacteria.
(E) Natural killer (NK) cell cytotoxicity, leading to impaired defense against viral infections and tumor cells.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent sinopulmonary infections:** Suggests a defect in humoral immunity, as these infections are typically cleared by antibodies.
- **Onset around 6 months:** Consistent with the waning of maternal IgG antibodies, exposing the infant's own immune deficiency.
- **Specific pathogens (*S. pneumoniae*, *H. influenzae*):** Encapsulated bacteria, commonly targeted by opsonizing antibodies (IgG, IgM) and complement.
- **Small tonsils/absent lymph nodes:** Indicates lymphoid hypoplasia, characteristic of B cell deficiencies.
- **Markedly reduced serum immunoglobulins (all isotypes):** Points to a severe defect in B cell development or function, preventing antibody production.
- **Decreased circulating B lymphocytes (<1%):** Confirms a profound B cell deficiency.
- **Flow cytometry block at pre-B cell stage:** Pinpoints the developmental arrest in the bone marrow.
- ***BTK* gene mutation on X chromosome:** Identifies the specific genetic defect causing X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA).
- **Question asks for the most directly impaired cellular process:** Requires linking the *BTK* defect to its downstream consequences in B cell biology.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Bruton's Tyrosine Kinase (BTK):** A non-receptor tyrosine kinase crucial for B cell development and signaling. It is expressed primarily in hematopoietic cells, especially B lymphocytes.
- **Role of BTK in B cell development:** BTK is essential for the transition from pro-B cells to pre-B cells and subsequent maturation through immature B cells to mature B cells. It phosphorylates downstream signaling molecules in the B cell receptor (BCR) pathway and other signaling cascades necessary for survival, proliferation, and differentiation.
- **BCR Signaling:** The BCR complex includes immunoglobulin molecules (surface IgM/IgD) and associated signaling molecules (Igα/Igβ heterodimers). Upon antigen binding, the BCR undergoes clustering and activation of intracellular signaling pathways. BTK is a key component of this pathway. It phosphorylates Igα/Igβ, leading to the recruitment and activation of spleen tyrosine kinase (Syk), which then phosphorylates downstream targets like Bruton's agammaglobulinemia tyrosine kinase substrate 1 (Baf3), leading to activation of phospholipase Cγ2 (PLCγ2) and subsequent calcium mobilization and NF-κB activation, promoting B cell survival, proliferation, and differentiation.
- **XLA Pathophysiology:** Mutations in the *BTK* gene lead to a non-functional or absent BTK protein. This disrupts BCR signaling at an early stage, preventing the successful transition through the pre-B cell stage in the bone marrow. Consequently, mature B cells fail to develop, leading to absent or severely reduced B cell numbers in the periphery and a profound deficiency of all immunoglobulin isotypes (agammaglobulinemia).
- **T cell development and function:** BTK is not essential for T cell development or TCR signaling. T cells develop normally in XLA patients.
- **Complement system:** The complement system is largely independent of BTK function, although B cells play a role in the alternative pathway. The primary defect in XLA is B cell development, not complement activation.
- **Phagocytosis:** Neutrophil phagocytosis is generally intact in XLA, although the lack of opsonizing antibodies impairs their ability to clear encapsulated bacteria efficiently. The primary defect is not in the phagocytic cells themselves.
- **NK cell function:** NK cell development and cytotoxicity are independent of BTK.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **TCR signaling:** This is crucial for T cell activation. While T cells are important for adaptive immunity, the primary defect in XLA affects B cells, not T cells. BTK is not directly involved in TCR signaling. Therefore, this option is incorrect.
- (C): **Complement component C3 activation:** C3 activation is central to the complement cascade. While complement deficiency can cause recurrent infections, the defect in XLA is specifically in B cell development and antibody production, not the complement system itself. Therefore, this option is incorrect.
- (D): **Phagocytosis by neutrophils:** Neutrophils are phagocytes essential for clearing bacteria. While patients with XLA are susceptible to bacterial infections partly due to impaired opsonization (lack of antibodies), the fundamental defect is not in the phagocytic ability of neutrophils themselves, but rather in the lack of B cells and antibodies. Therefore, this option is incorrect.
- (E): **Natural killer (NK) cell cytotoxicity:** NK cells are part of the innate immune system, important for killing virus-infected cells and tumor cells. NK cell function is independent of BTK and B cell development. Therefore, this option is incorrect.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | XLA (BTK Deficiency)                                  | T Cell Defects (e.g., DiGeorge)                     | Complement Deficiency (e.g., C3)                  | Phagocyte Defects (e.g., CGD)                     |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------ |
| **Primary Cell Affected** | B lymphocytes                                         | T lymphocytes                                       | Complement proteins                               | Phagocytes (Neutrophils, Macrophages)            |
| **Key Defect**          | B cell development block, lack of antibodies          | Impaired T cell development/function                | Deficient complement cascade activation            | Impaired microbial killing by phagocytes          |
| **Immunoglobulin Levels** | Markedly reduced (all isotypes)                       | Normal or elevated IgM, decreased IgG/IgA/IgE       | Normal                                            | Normal                                            |
| **B Cell Count**        | Severely decreased (<1%)                               | Normal                                             | Normal                                            | Normal                                            |
| **T Cell Count**        | Normal                                                | Decreased (depending on specific defect)            | Normal                                            | Normal                                            |
| **Typical Infections**   | Encapsulated bacteria (e.g., *S. pneumoniae*, *H. flu*) | Opportunistic infections (fungi, viruses, PJP)      | Encapsulated bacteria (similar to XLA)             | Catalase-positive bacteria/fungi (e.g., *Staph*, *Aspergillus*) |
| **Underlying Mechanism** | Defect in BCR signaling (BTK mutation)                 | Defect in T cell development (e.g., thymic hypoplasia) | Defect in complement protein synthesis/function | Defect in NADPH oxidase or phagosome fusion      |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA) is caused by mutations in the *BTK* gene, leading to a block in B cell development at the pre-B cell stage. This results in a profound deficiency of mature B cells and all immunoglobulin isotypes, predisposing patients to recurrent infections with encapsulated bacteria. The core defect lies in the inability of B cells to signal properly through the B cell receptor (BCR) due to the lack of functional BTK, a crucial tyrosine kinase in the BCR signaling pathway. Differentiating XLA from other primary immunodeficiencies requires understanding the specific cell lineage affected and the nature of the defect (e.g., T cell defects like DiGeorge syndrome, complement deficiencies, phagocyte defects like Chronic Granulomatous Disease).

<a id='question-104'></a>

### Question 104: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 04 (kinase)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of primary immunodeficiencies, specifically focusing on defects in intracellular signaling pathways, particularly involving kinases, and their clinical manifestations, including susceptibility to specific infections and impaired T-cell function. (本题考察原发性免疫缺陷，特别是细胞内信号通路缺陷，尤其是激酶相关缺陷，及其临床表现，包括对特定感染的易感性和T细胞功能障碍。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 5-year-old boy presents with recurrent sinopulmonary infections, including pneumonia caused by *Staphylococcus aureus* and *Pseudomonas aeruginosa*, starting in infancy. He also has chronic diarrhea and failure to thrive. His vaccination history is incomplete due to frequent illnesses. Physical examination reveals lymphadenopathy and hepatosplenomegaly. Laboratory investigations show markedly reduced T-cell numbers (CD3+ T cells: 150/µL), normal B-cell numbers (CD19+ B cells: 800/µL), and normal NK cell numbers (CD16+/CD56+ NK cells: 300/µL). Flow cytometry reveals absent expression of CD3ζ (zeta) chain on T cells. T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling assays demonstrate a complete inability of T cells to proliferate in response to mitogenic stimulation (e.g., PHA) or antigen-specific stimulation. The patient's mother has a history of similar, though milder, infections.

(A) Defective Janus kinase (JAK) signaling
(B) Defective Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK)
(C) Defective CD3ζ (zeta) chain signaling
(D) Defective inducible costimulator (ICOS) signaling
(E) Defective adenosine deaminase (ADA) activity

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent sinopulmonary infections (S. aureus, P. aeruginosa):** Suggests a T-cell defect, as T-cells are crucial for controlling intracellular pathogens and encapsulated bacteria. B-cell defects typically present with encapsulated bacteria but less commonly with *P. aeruginosa*.
- **Chronic diarrhea and failure to thrive:** Common features in severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) or other severe T-cell defects.
- **Lymphadenopathy and hepatosplenomegaly:** Can be seen in various immunodeficiencies, including those with T-cell involvement, potentially due to chronic infections or dysregulated immune responses.
- **Markedly reduced T-cell numbers (CD3+):** Points towards a severe T-cell deficiency, potentially SCID or a T-cell specific defect.
- **Normal B-cell numbers (CD19+):** Rules out defects primarily affecting B-cell development, like X-linked agammaglobulinemia (BTK deficiency).
- **Normal NK cell numbers:** Rules out defects affecting NK cell development, such as defects in the common gamma chain (γc) which affects T, B, and NK cells.
- **Absent CD3ζ (zeta) chain expression:** This is the critical clue. The CD3ζ chain is a crucial component of the T-cell receptor (TCR) complex. It contains immunoreceptor tyrosine-based activation motifs (ITAMs) essential for intracellular signaling upon TCR engagement.
- **Impaired T-cell proliferation:** Confirms the functional defect in T-cell activation, consistent with a signaling defect downstream of the TCR.
- **Maternal history of similar, milder infections:** Suggests a possible autosomal recessive inheritance pattern, common for many primary immunodeficiencies.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **TCR Signaling:** The TCR complex consists of the TCRαβ or γδ heterodimer, the CD3 complex (γ, δ, ε, ζ chains), and the CD3ζ homodimer. Upon antigen recognition, the TCR complex associates with the CD3ζ chain, which contains ITAMs. Tyrosine kinases, primarily Lck and Fyn, phosphorylate ITAMs on CD3ζ. This recruits ZAP-70 (zeta-chain-associated protein kinase 70), which then phosphorylates downstream signaling molecules like LAT (linker for activation of T cells), leading to activation of PLCγ, calcium influx, and ultimately T-cell activation, proliferation, and cytokine production.
- **CD3ζ Deficiency:** Absence or dysfunction of the CD3ζ chain prevents the proper assembly and signaling of the TCR complex. Without functional ITAMs, tyrosine kinases cannot initiate the downstream signaling cascade, leading to a complete block in T-cell activation and proliferation, despite normal T-cell development (as evidenced by normal T-cell numbers initially, though they may decline over time). This results in a phenotype resembling SCID, with severe susceptibility to infections.
- **Differential Diagnosis of T-cell defects:**
    - **SCID:** Various genetic defects can cause SCID, affecting T-cell development (e.g., IL-2 receptor gamma chain deficiency, JAK3 deficiency, RAG1/2 deficiency) or function (e.g., CD3ζ deficiency).
    - **JAK3 Deficiency:** Affects signaling downstream of cytokine receptors (IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, IL-21), crucial for T-cell and NK-cell development and function. Leads to absent T and NK cells, but normal B cells.
    - **BTK Deficiency (XLA):** Affects B-cell development, leading to absent B cells and immunoglobulins. T-cell function is normal.
    - **ADA Deficiency:** Causes accumulation of toxic metabolites (deoxyadenosine) leading to lymphopenia (T, B, and NK cells) and SCID.
    - **ICOS Deficiency:** Affects T-cell help for B-cell antibody production and T-cell effector function. Patients have normal T-cell numbers but impaired antibody responses, particularly to polysaccharide antigens, and susceptibility to intracellular pathogens.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective Janus kinase (JAK) signaling:** JAKs (specifically JAK3) are crucial for signaling downstream of cytokine receptors, particularly IL-7, which is essential for T-cell development and survival. JAK3 deficiency causes SCID with absent T and NK cells but normal B cells. This patient has normal B and NK cells but severely reduced T cells with a specific signaling defect (CD3ζ), not a general cytokine receptor defect.
- (B) **Defective Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK):** BTK is essential for B-cell development. Deficiency causes X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA), characterized by absent B cells and immunoglobulins, leading to recurrent bacterial infections (especially encapsulated organisms). T-cell numbers and function are normal. This patient has normal B-cell numbers but severely impaired T-cell function.
- (D) **Defective inducible costimulator (ICOS) signaling:** ICOS is a costimulatory molecule expressed on activated T cells that interacts with ICOS ligand (ICOSL) on antigen-presenting cells. It is crucial for T-cell help to B cells and for T-cell effector function. Deficiency leads to hyper-IgM syndrome (impaired class switching) and susceptibility to intracellular pathogens, but T-cell numbers are typically normal, and the primary defect is in T-cell help, not the initial TCR signaling cascade. This patient has a defect in the initial TCR signaling.
- (E) **Defective adenosine deaminase (ADA) activity:** ADA is an enzyme involved in purine metabolism. Deficiency leads to the accumulation of toxic metabolites (deoxyadenosine, deoxyATP) that are particularly toxic to lymphocytes, causing severe lymphopenia affecting T, B, and NK cells, resulting in SCID. While it causes severe T-cell deficiency, the underlying mechanism is lymphocyte toxicity, not a specific defect in the TCR signaling pathway like CD3ζ.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | CD3ζ Deficiency (Correct) | JAK3 Deficiency (A) | BTK Deficiency (B) | ICOS Deficiency (D) | ADA Deficiency (E) |
|-------------------------|---------------------------|----------------------|---------------------|----------------------|--------------------|
| **Primary Defect**      | TCR Signaling (CD3ζ)     | Cytokine Signaling   | B-cell Development  | T-cell Help         | Lymphocyte Toxicity|
| **T-cell Numbers**      | Reduced/Normal initially | Absent               | Normal              | Normal               | Absent             |
| **B-cell Numbers**      | Normal                    | Normal               | Absent              | Normal               | Absent             |
| **NK-cell Numbers**     | Normal                    | Absent               | Normal              | Normal               | Absent             |
| **T-cell Function**     | Absent (Signaling block)  | Absent (Development) | Normal              | Impaired (Help)      | Absent             |
| **Key Infections**      | Broad (SCID-like)         | Broad (SCID-like)    | Encapsulated bacteria| Intracellular pathogens| Broad (SCID-like)  |
| **CD3ζ Expression**     | Absent                    | Normal               | Normal              | Normal               | Normal             |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Defects in the CD3ζ chain disrupt the initial signaling cascade of the T-cell receptor, leading to a severe T-cell immunodeficiency resembling SCID. This contrasts with defects in cytokine signaling (JAK3), B-cell development (BTK), T-cell help (ICOS), or lymphocyte survival (ADA), which have distinct clinical and immunological profiles. Understanding the specific components of the TCR complex and their roles in signaling is crucial for diagnosing primary immunodeficiencies affecting T-cell function.

<a id='question-105'></a>

### Question 105: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 05 (promotes)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the immunological defect in Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome (WAS), specifically the role of the WASp protein in actin cytoskeleton reorganization and its impact on T-cell function, platelet size, and eczema. (本题考察Wiskott-Aldrich综合征（WAS）的免疫缺陷，特别是WASp蛋白在actin细胞骨架重组中的作用，以及它对T细胞功能、血小板大小和湿疹的影响。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with recurrent severe infections (otitis media, pneumonia), eczema, and thrombocytopenia (small platelets). His mother reports a history of bleeding easily from minor cuts and bruises. Family history reveals that his maternal uncle died at age 10 from lymphoma. Immunological workup shows decreased T-cell numbers, impaired T-cell proliferation in response to mitogens, and low levels of IgM. Flow cytometry reveals absent expression of the WASp protein in his lymphocytes. Which of the following molecular defects is most likely responsible for this patient's clinical presentation?

(A) Defective CD40 ligand (CD40L) expression on T cells, leading to impaired B-cell class switching and T-cell-dependent antibody responses.
(B) Defective Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK) activity, resulting in a block in B-cell development and maturation.
(C) Defective WASp protein function, impairing actin polymerization and cytoskeletal reorganization in hematopoietic cells.
(D) Defective Janus kinase 3 (JAK3) activity, leading to impaired signaling downstream of cytokine receptors, particularly the common gamma chain (γc).
(E) Defective DiGeorge syndrome (22q11.2 deletion), resulting in thymic hypoplasia, impaired T-cell development, and hypocalcemia.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 3-year-old boy. This age is typical for the presentation of primary immunodeficiencies.
- **Clinical Triad:** Recurrent infections (otitis media, pneumonia), eczema, and thrombocytopenia (small platelets). This triad is highly characteristic of Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome (WAS).
- **Bleeding History:** Easy bruising and bleeding suggest a platelet disorder, specifically thrombocytopenia with small platelet size, which is a hallmark of WAS.
- **Family History:** Maternal uncle died young from lymphoma. WAS patients have an increased risk of autoimmunity and malignancy, including lymphoma. The maternal inheritance pattern is consistent with an X-linked recessive disorder, which WAS is.
- **Immunological Findings:** Decreased T-cell numbers, impaired T-cell proliferation, low IgM. These findings indicate a combined immunodeficiency affecting both T-cell and B-cell (specifically IgM production) function.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Absent WASp protein expression in lymphocytes. This is the definitive diagnostic finding for WAS.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular defect responsible for the clinical presentation.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Wiskott-Aldrich Syndrome (WAS):** An X-linked recessive primary immunodeficiency caused by mutations in the *WAS* gene, which encodes the Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome protein (WASp).
- **WASp Function:** WASp is a cytoplasmic protein crucial for actin cytoskeleton organization in hematopoietic cells. It interacts with actin filaments, Arp2/3 complex, and other proteins to regulate actin polymerization, branching, and cell motility.
- **Impact on Hematopoietic Cells:**
    - **T cells:** WASp is essential for T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling, T-cell activation, migration, and synapse formation. Defective WASp leads to impaired T-cell proliferation, cytokine production, and cytotoxic function.
    - **B cells:** WASp contributes to B-cell receptor (BCR) signaling and B-cell development, although B-cell numbers are usually normal. Impaired T-cell help contributes to the low IgM levels.
    - **Platelets:** WASp is required for normal platelet formation and function. Defective WASp results in thrombocytopenia and characteristically small platelets (microthrombocytopenia) due to impaired proplatelet formation.
- **Clinical Manifestations:** The classic triad of eczema, thrombocytopenia (with small platelets), and recurrent infections (due to combined T-cell and B-cell dysfunction) is characteristic. Increased risk of autoimmunity and malignancy (especially lymphoma) is also common.
- **Molecular Basis:** The defect is in the WASp protein itself, leading to impaired actin cytoskeleton dynamics in lymphocytes and platelets.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective CD40 ligand (CD40L) expression:** This describes X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome. Patients have normal T-cell numbers but impaired T-cell-dependent B-cell activation, leading to high IgM and low IgG, IgA, IgE. They do not typically have eczema or thrombocytopenia. The defect is in CD40L (encoded by *CD40LG* gene) on activated T cells, which binds to CD40 on B cells, macrophages, and dendritic cells.
- (B) **Defective Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK) activity:** This describes X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA). Patients have a profound deficiency of mature B cells and all immunoglobulin isotypes due to a block in B-cell development in the bone marrow. They present with recurrent bacterial infections, especially encapsulated organisms, starting after maternal antibodies wane. They do not have eczema, thrombocytopenia, or T-cell defects. The defect is in BTK (encoded by *BTK* gene), crucial for B-cell receptor signaling.
- (D) **Defective Janus kinase 3 (JAK3) activity:** This describes X-linked severe combined immunodeficiency (X-SCID). Patients have a profound deficiency of T cells and NK cells, and often B cells as well. They present early in infancy with severe, opportunistic infections. The defect is in JAK3 (encoded by *JAK3* gene), which is essential for signaling downstream of cytokine receptors that use the common gamma chain (γc), including IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, and IL-21. They do not typically have eczema or thrombocytopenia.
- (E) **Defective DiGeorge syndrome (22q11.2 deletion):** This is a syndrome caused by a microdeletion on chromosome 22, leading to developmental defects including thymic hypoplasia/aplasia, parathyroid hypoplasia, and congenital heart defects. Immunologically, it results in T-cell deficiency (variable severity) due to impaired thymic development. Patients may have recurrent infections, but the classic triad of WAS (eczema, thrombocytopenia, infections) is not present. Hypocalcemia due to parathyroid hypoplasia is a common feature.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Wiskott-Aldrich Syndrome (WAS) | X-linked Hyper-IgM Syndrome | X-linked Agammaglobulinemia (XLA) | X-linked SCID (JAK3 deficiency) | DiGeorge Syndrome |
|----------------------|---------------------------------|------------------------------|-----------------------------------|---------------------------------|-------------------|
| **Gene Defect**      | *WAS* (WASp)                    | *CD40LG* (CD40L)             | *BTK*                             | *JAK3*                          | 22q11.2 deletion  |
| **Inheritance**      | X-linked Recessive              | X-linked Recessive           | X-linked Recessive                | X-linked Recessive              | Autosomal Dominant/De Novo |
| **T Cells**          | Decreased, dysfunctional        | Normal numbers, dysfunctional| Normal numbers                   | Severely decreased/absent      | Decreased/Absent (thymic) |
| **B Cells**          | Normal numbers, dysfunctional   | Normal numbers               | Absent                            | Often absent                   | Normal numbers    |
| **Immunoglobulins**  | Low IgM, variable IgG/IgA/IgE   | High IgM, low IgG/IgA/IgE    | Absent all isotypes               | Absent all isotypes             | Variable          |
| **Eczema**           | Yes                             | No                           | No                                | No                              | No                |
| **Thrombocytopenia** | Yes (small platelets)           | No                           | No                                | No                              | No                |
| **Key Features**     | Triad: Eczema, Thrombocytopenia, Infections; Lymphoma risk | Recurrent infections, High IgM     | Recurrent bacterial infections   | Severe infections early in life | Thymic hypoplasia, Hypocalcemia, Heart defects |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome is characterized by the triad of eczema, thrombocytopenia (with small platelets), and recurrent infections, along with an increased risk of autoimmunity and malignancy. The underlying defect is in the WASp protein, crucial for actin cytoskeleton organization. Differentiating WAS from other X-linked immunodeficiencies like X-linked Hyper-IgM (CD40L defect, high IgM), XLA (BTK defect, absent B cells), and X-SCID (JAK3 defect, severe T-cell deficiency) relies on recognizing the specific clinical triad and immunological findings.

<a id='question-106'></a>

### Question 106: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 06 (expansion;)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Differentiate between X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA) and Common Variable Immunodeficiency (CVID) based on clinical presentation, B-cell development, and immunoglobulin levels. (区分X连锁丙种球蛋白缺乏症(XLA)和共同可变免疫缺陷(CVID)的临床表现、B细胞发育和免疫球蛋白水平。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 5-year-old boy presents with recurrent sinopulmonary infections, including pneumonia and otitis media, starting around 6 months of age after maternal antibodies waned. He has not received any live vaccines. Physical examination reveals normal growth and development, but no palpable lymph nodes or tonsils. Laboratory studies show panhypogammaglobulinemia (IgG < 200 mg/dL, IgA < 50 mg/dL, IgM < 50 mg/dL) and absent B cells (CD19+ cells < 1%) in peripheral blood flow cytometry. His older brother died at age 3 from complications of a severe bacterial infection. His mother is a carrier for an X-linked recessive disorder. A bone marrow biopsy shows markedly reduced B-cell precursors.

(A) Deficiency in Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK) leading to impaired pre-B cell development.
(B) Deficiency in CD40 ligand (CD40L) leading to impaired T-cell dependent B-cell activation and isotype switching.
(C) Deficiency in inducible costimulator (ICOS) leading to impaired germinal center formation and memory B-cell development.
(D) Deficiency in Janus kinase 3 (JAK3) leading to impaired T-cell and NK-cell development, but normal B-cell numbers.
(E) Deficiency in adenosine deaminase (ADA) leading to accumulation of toxic metabolites affecting lymphocyte development, particularly T-cells and B-cells.

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 5-year-old boy with recurrent sinopulmonary infections starting after 6 months (waning maternal IgG). This points towards a humoral immunodeficiency.
- **Family History:** Older brother died young from infection; mother is a carrier of an X-linked recessive disorder. This strongly suggests an X-linked primary immunodeficiency affecting males.
- **Physical Exam:** Absence of palpable lymph nodes and tonsils suggests lymphoid hypoplasia, particularly B-cell tissues.
- **Laboratory Findings:**
    - Panhypogammaglobulinemia (very low IgG, IgA, IgM): Indicates a severe defect in antibody production.
    - Absent B cells (CD19+ < 1%): Points to a block in B-cell development.
    - Bone marrow biopsy: Reduced B-cell precursors confirms the developmental block.
- **Vaccination History:** No live vaccines received, consistent with severe immunodeficiency.
- **Differential Diagnosis:** The combination of X-linked inheritance, early-onset recurrent bacterial infections, panhypogammaglobulinemia, absent B cells, and lymphoid hypoplasia is classic for X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA).

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **X-linked Agammaglobulinemia (XLA):** Caused by mutations in the *BTK* gene on the X chromosome. BTK is a non-receptor tyrosine kinase crucial for B-cell development.
- **Pathogenesis:** BTK is essential for signaling downstream of the pre-B cell receptor (pre-BCR) and B-cell receptor (BCR). A defect in BTK blocks B-cell development at the pre-B cell stage in the bone marrow. This leads to a profound deficiency of mature B cells (CD19+) and plasma cells, resulting in panhypogammaglobulinemia. T-cell immunity is normal.
- **Clinical Features:** Recurrent bacterial infections (especially encapsulated organisms like *Streptococcus pneumoniae*, *Haemophilus influenzae*) starting after maternal antibodies decline (around 6 months). Patients typically lack tonsils, adenoids, and lymph nodes due to the absence of B cells.
- **Diagnosis:** Low/absent serum immunoglobulins (IgG, IgA, IgM), absent B cells (<1-2% of lymphocytes), normal T-cell numbers and function, markedly reduced B-cell precursors in bone marrow.
- **Comparison with other options:**
    - **(B) CD40L deficiency (Hyper-IgM syndrome):** X-linked or autosomal recessive. Affects T-cell dependent B-cell activation and isotype switching. Patients have normal or elevated IgM but low IgG, IgA, IgE. B-cell numbers are usually normal.
    - **(C) ICOS deficiency:** Autosomal recessive. Affects germinal center formation and memory B-cell development. Patients have normal B-cell numbers but impaired antibody responses, particularly to T-dependent antigens. Often presents with recurrent sinopulmonary infections and susceptibility to intracellular pathogens.
    - **(D) JAK3 deficiency:** X-linked. JAK3 is involved in signaling for multiple cytokine receptors (e.g., IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, IL-21). Deficiency affects T-cell and NK-cell development and function, leading to severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID). B-cell numbers are typically normal, but antibody production is impaired due to lack of T-cell help.
    - **(E) ADA deficiency:** Autosomal recessive. Adenosine deaminase is involved in purine metabolism. Deficiency leads to accumulation of toxic metabolites (deoxyadenosine triphosphate) that are particularly toxic to lymphocytes, causing SCID affecting T cells, B cells, and NK cells.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Correct.** This option accurately describes the underlying defect in XLA: BTK deficiency leading to impaired pre-B cell development, explaining the absent B cells and panhypogammaglobulinemia seen in the patient. The X-linked inheritance pattern and clinical presentation fit perfectly.
- (B): **Incorrect.** CD40L deficiency causes Hyper-IgM syndrome. Patients have normal B-cell numbers but cannot undergo isotype switching, resulting in high IgM and low IgG/IgA/IgE. This patient has absent B cells, not normal numbers.
- (C): **Incorrect.** ICOS deficiency affects germinal center formation and memory B cells. Patients typically have normal B-cell numbers but impaired antibody responses, especially to T-dependent antigens. This patient has absent B cells.
- (D): **Incorrect.** JAK3 deficiency causes SCID, affecting T cells and NK cells primarily, although B cells can also be affected secondarily due to lack of T-cell help. B-cell numbers are usually normal in JAK3 deficiency, unlike this patient who has absent B cells.
- (E): **Incorrect.** ADA deficiency causes SCID, affecting T cells, B cells, and NK cells due to toxic metabolite accumulation. While it causes severe immunodeficiency, it typically presents earlier and more severely than XLA, and the primary defect is not specifically in B-cell development leading to absent B cells, but rather a broader lymphocyte toxicity.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | XLA (BTK deficiency) | Hyper-IgM (CD40L def.) | ICOS deficiency | JAK3 deficiency (SCID) | ADA deficiency (SCID) |
|----------------------|----------------------|------------------------|----------------|------------------------|-----------------------|
| Inheritance         | X-linked             | X-linked/AR            | AR             | X-linked               | AR                   |
| Primary Cell Type   | B cells              | T cells (CD4+)          | B cells        | T cells, NK cells      | T, B, NK cells        |
| B-cell Numbers      | Absent (<1%)         | Normal                 | Normal         | Normal                 | Low/Absent            |
| Immunoglobulins     | Panhypo-γ           | High IgM, Low IgG/IgA/IgE | Variable       | Low IgG/IgA/IgE        | Low all Ig           |
| T-cell Numbers      | Normal               | Normal                 | Normal         | Low/Absent             | Low/Absent            |
| NK-cell Numbers     | Normal               | Normal                 | Normal         | Low/Absent             | Low/Absent            |
| Key Defect          | Pre-B cell development | Isotype switching      | Germinal centers| T/NK cell development  | Lymphocyte toxicity   |
| Clinical Onset      | ~6 months            | Variable               | Variable       | Early infancy          | Early infancy         |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
XLA is characterized by absent B cells and panhypogammaglobulinemia due to a defect in BTK, an X-linked recessive disorder. Differentiate it from other primary immunodeficiencies like Hyper-IgM syndrome (normal B cells, high IgM), ICOS deficiency (normal B cells, impaired memory), JAK3 deficiency (SCID, low T/NK cells), and ADA deficiency (SCID, broad lymphocyte toxicity) based on specific cell populations, immunoglobulin levels, and inheritance patterns.

<a id='question-107'></a>

### Question 107: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 07 (faulty)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the molecular defect and resulting immunological phenotype in Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome (WAS), a primary immunodeficiency characterized by eczema, thrombocytopenia, and recurrent infections, specifically focusing on the defective WASp protein and its role in actin cytoskeleton rearrangement in hematopoietic cells. (本题考察Wiskott-Aldrich综合征（WAS）的分子缺陷及其导致的免疫学表型，该综合征是一种原发性免疫缺陷病，特征为湿疹、血小板减少症和复发性感染，重点关注缺陷的WASp蛋白及其在造血细胞中肌动蛋白细胞骨架重排中的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with severe eczema, petechiae, and recurrent sinopulmonary infections since infancy. His mother reports frequent bleeding episodes, including nosebleeds and easy bruising. Physical examination reveals widespread eczematous lesions, multiple petechiae on the lower extremities, and mild splenomegaly. Laboratory findings show thrombocytopenia (platelet count 25,000/µL) with small platelet size, low serum IgM levels, and normal or elevated serum IgA and IgE levels. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes reveals absent expression of the WASp protein. Which of the following molecular defects is most likely responsible for this patient's clinical presentation?

(A) Defective CD40 ligand (CD40L) expression on activated T cells, impairing T cell-dependent B cell activation and isotype switching.
(B) Defective Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK) leading to impaired B cell development and maturation, resulting in agammaglobulinemia.
(C) Defective WASp protein leading to impaired actin cytoskeleton rearrangement in hematopoietic cells, affecting T cell function, platelet formation, and B cell function.
(D) Defective Janus kinase 3 (JAK3) leading to impaired signaling downstream of cytokine receptors, affecting T cell, B cell, and NK cell development and function.
(E) Defective DiGeorge syndrome critical region 4 (DCR4) gene leading to impaired V(D)J recombination, resulting in T cell receptor excision circle (TREC) accumulation and absent T cells.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 3-year-old boy.
- **Key Symptoms:** Severe eczema, petechiae (suggesting thrombocytopenia/platelet dysfunction), recurrent sinopulmonary infections (suggesting immunodeficiency).
- **Physical Exam:** Eczematous lesions, petechiae, mild splenomegaly.
- **Lab Findings:** Thrombocytopenia (low platelet count) with *small* platelet size, low IgM, normal/high IgA/IgE.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Absent WASp protein expression.
- **Question:** Identify the molecular defect responsible for this presentation.

The combination of eczema, thrombocytopenia (especially with small platelets), recurrent infections, low IgM, and absent WASp protein is pathognomonic for Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome (WAS). The question asks for the underlying molecular defect.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Wiskott-Aldrich Syndrome (WAS):** An X-linked recessive primary immunodeficiency caused by mutations in the *WAS* gene located on the X chromosome (Xp11.23).
- **WASp Protein:** The *WAS* gene encodes the Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome protein (WASp). WASp is a cytoplasmic protein expressed in hematopoietic cells (T cells, B cells, platelets, monocytes, dendritic cells).
- **Function of WASp:** WASp is crucial for actin cytoskeleton organization and dynamics. It interacts with various proteins involved in actin polymerization and signaling pathways, including WAVE/Scar proteins, Arp2/3 complex, and N-WASP. This interaction is essential for processes like:
    - **T cell activation:** WASp is required for T cell receptor (TCR) signaling, T cell migration, and synapse formation. Defective WASp impairs T cell proliferation and cytokine production.
    - **B cell function:** WASp influences B cell development, migration, and interaction with T cells. Defective WASp contributes to impaired antibody responses, particularly IgM.
    - **Platelet formation and function:** WASp is essential for platelet biogenesis (leading to small platelets) and function. Defective WASp causes thrombocytopenia and impaired platelet aggregation.
- **Clinical Triad:** The classic triad of WAS is eczema, thrombocytopenia (with small platelets), and recurrent infections (due to combined T and B cell defects).
- **Immunological Phenotype:** Patients typically have low IgM, normal or elevated IgA and IgE, and impaired T cell responses (especially delayed-type hypersensitivity). They are susceptible to opportunistic infections, autoimmune disorders, and malignancies.
- **Molecular Defect:** The underlying defect is the absence or dysfunction of the WASp protein due to mutations in the *WAS* gene.

Now, let's evaluate the options based on this understanding:

- **(A) Defective CD40 ligand (CD40L):** This causes X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome. Patients have normal T and B cell numbers but impaired T cell-dependent B cell activation, leading to high IgM and low IgG, IgA, IgE. They lack eczema and thrombocytopenia.
- **(B) Defective Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK):** This causes X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA). Patients have absent B cells and panhypogammaglobulinemia (low levels of all immunoglobulin isotypes). They lack eczema and thrombocytopenia.
- **(C) Defective WASp protein:** This directly matches the clinical presentation (eczema, thrombocytopenia with small platelets, recurrent infections, low IgM) and the specific finding of absent WASp protein expression. This is the correct answer.
- **(D) Defective Janus kinase 3 (JAK3):** This causes X-linked severe combined immunodeficiency (X-SCID). Patients have absent T cells and NK cells, and B cells are present but non-functional. They present early in life with severe, life-threatening infections. They lack eczema and thrombocytopenia.
- **(E) Defective DiGeorge syndrome critical region 4 (DCR4):** This refers to mutations in the *RAG1* or *RAG2* genes, causing Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID) due to impaired V(D)J recombination. Patients have absent T cells and B cells (T-B- SCID). They lack eczema and thrombocytopenia.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- **(A) Defective CD40 ligand (CD40L):** Incorrect. This causes Hyper-IgM syndrome, characterized by high IgM and low IgG/IgA/IgE due to impaired T cell help for B cell isotype switching. Patients lack the eczema and thrombocytopenia seen in WAS. The key immunological defect is in T cell-B cell interaction, not WASp.
- **(B) Defective Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK):** Incorrect. This causes X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA), characterized by a lack of mature B cells and panhypogammaglobulinemia. Patients lack the eczema and thrombocytopenia seen in WAS. The key defect is in B cell development.
- **(D) Defective Janus kinase 3 (JAK3):** Incorrect. This causes X-SCID, characterized by a lack of T cells and NK cells. Patients lack the eczema and thrombocytopenia seen in WAS. The key defect is in cytokine signaling affecting lymphocyte development.
- **(E) Defective DiGeorge syndrome critical region 4 (DCR4):** Incorrect. This refers to RAG deficiency, causing SCID due to impaired V(D)J recombination, leading to a lack of T and B cells. Patients lack the eczema and thrombocytopenia seen in WAS. The key defect is in lymphocyte receptor gene rearrangement.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Wiskott-Aldrich Syndrome (WAS) | X-linked Hyper-IgM Syndrome | X-linked Agammaglobulinemia (XLA) | X-linked SCID (JAK3 Deficient) | RAG Deficiency SCID |
|----------------------|---------------------------------|------------------------------|-----------------------------------|---------------------------------|----------------------|
| **Gene Defect**      | *WAS* (WASp)                    | *CD40LG* (CD40L)             | *BTK*                             | *JAK3*                          | *RAG1/RAG2*          |
| **Inheritance**      | X-linked Recessive              | X-linked Recessive           | X-linked Recessive                | X-linked Recessive              | Autosomal Recessive  |
| **Key Clinical Features** | Eczema, Thrombocytopenia (small platelets), Recurrent Infections | Recurrent Infections, Opportunistic Infections | Recurrent Infections (bacterial) | Severe Recurrent Infections (early) | Severe Recurrent Infections (early) |
| **Immunoglobulin Levels** | Low IgM, Normal/High IgA/IgE | High IgM, Low IgG/IgA/IgE    | Low All (Panhypogammaglobulinemia) | Normal (B cells present but non-functional) | Low All (Absent B cells) |
| **Lymphocyte Counts** | Normal T/B cells                | Normal T/B cells             | Absent B cells                    | Absent T cells, NK cells         | Absent T/B cells     |
| **Platelets**        | Low, Small                      | Normal                       | Normal                            | Normal                          | Normal               |
| **WASp Expression**  | Absent                          | Normal                       | Normal                            | Normal                          | Normal               |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome is characterized by the triad of eczema, thrombocytopenia (with small platelets), and recurrent infections, resulting from defective WASp protein function, which disrupts actin cytoskeleton dynamics in hematopoietic cells. Differentiating WAS from other primary immunodeficiencies like XLA (no B cells, panhypogammaglobulinemia), Hyper-IgM syndrome (high IgM, low other Ig, normal platelets), and SCID (severe infections, absent T cells) relies on recognizing this specific clinical triad and the associated laboratory findings (low IgM, small platelets, absent WASp).

<a id='question-108'></a>

### Question 108: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 08 (development)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the molecular basis and clinical manifestations of defects in T-cell development, specifically focusing on the role of CD3 signaling and its impact on T-cell receptor (TCR) expression and function, leading to severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID). (理解T细胞发育缺陷的分子基础和临床表现，特别是CD3信号传导缺陷对T细胞受体(TCR)表达和功能的影响，导致重症联合免疫缺陷症(SCID)。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-month-old male infant presents with recurrent severe bacterial, viral, and fungal infections, including *Pneumocystis jirovecii* pneumonia and persistent oral thrush. He has failed to thrive despite adequate nutrition. Family history reveals that his older brother died at 1 year of age from complications of a severe, unspecified immunodeficiency. Flow cytometry analysis of the infant's peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a markedly reduced number of T cells (CD3+ cells) and normal numbers of B cells (CD19+ cells) and NK cells (CD56+ cells). Further analysis reveals that the few T cells present express very low levels of the T-cell receptor (TCR) complex on their surface. Genetic testing identifies a homozygous mutation in the *CD3D* gene, which encodes the CD3 delta chain.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the observed T-cell deficiency in this patient?

(A) Impaired V(D)J recombination due to a defect in the RAG1/RAG2 complex.
(B) Defective positive selection of thymocytes in the thymus due to a lack of MHC class I presentation.
(C) Failure of T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling due to the absence of a functional CD3 complex.
(D) Impaired development of the common gamma chain (γc) required for signaling through multiple cytokine receptors.
(E) Defective expression of CD4 and CD8 co-receptors on the surface of developing thymocytes.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 6-month-old male with recurrent severe infections (bacterial, viral, fungal, *Pneumocystis jirovecii*, thrush), failure to thrive. This pattern is highly suggestive of a severe immunodeficiency, particularly affecting T-cell function.
- **Family History:** Older brother died from a similar immunodeficiency, suggesting a genetic basis, likely autosomal recessive or X-linked.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Markedly reduced T cells (CD3+), normal B cells (CD19+), normal NK cells (CD56+). This points specifically to a T-cell defect, potentially a form of Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID) since T cells are crucial for B-cell help and cell-mediated immunity.
- **TCR Expression:** Low levels of TCR complex on the few T cells present. This indicates a problem with TCR assembly or signaling.
- **Genetic Testing:** Homozygous mutation in the *CD3D* gene. The CD3 complex (composed of CD3γ, CD3δ, CD3ε, and CD3ζ chains) is essential for transducing signals from the TCR upon antigen recognition. A defect in CD3D directly impacts the formation and function of the CD3 complex.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the T-cell deficiency given the *CD3D* mutation.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **T-cell Development:** T-cell precursors migrate from the bone marrow to the thymus. In the thymus, they undergo V(D)J recombination to generate diverse T-cell receptors (TCRs).
- **TCR Complex:** The functional TCR is a heterodimer (αβ or γδ) associated non-covalently with the CD3 complex (γδεζ chains). The CD3 complex is crucial for signal transduction. When the TCR binds to peptide-MHC, the CD3 complex initiates intracellular signaling cascades (e.g., involving ZAP-70, LAT, PLCγ) leading to T-cell activation.
- **CD3D Mutation:** The CD3D gene encodes the CD3 delta chain. A mutation here disrupts the assembly or stability of the CD3 complex.
- **Consequences of CD3D Mutation:** Without a functional CD3 complex, TCR signaling is severely impaired or absent, even if the TCR itself is expressed. This leads to:
    - **Impaired Positive Selection:** Thymocytes whose TCRs weakly recognize self-MHC undergo positive selection to survive. Defective CD3 signaling prevents this process, leading to apoptosis of many developing T cells.
    - **Impaired Negative Selection:** Thymocytes that strongly recognize self-antigens presented on MHC undergo negative selection (apoptosis) to prevent autoimmunity. While this process might be less affected than positive selection, overall T-cell development is severely compromised.
    - **Peripheral T-cell Dysfunction:** The few T cells that escape thymic selection and enter the periphery are non-functional due to the inability to signal upon TCR engagement.
- **Clinical Manifestation:** This results in a severe T-cell deficiency (T- B+ NK+ SCID), characterized by susceptibility to opportunistic infections (like *Pneumocystis jirovecii*), viral infections, and failure to thrive. The normal B-cell numbers are misleading, as B cells require T-cell help for optimal antibody production and class switching, leading to functional B-cell defects as well.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Impaired V(D)J recombination due to a defect in the RAG1/RAG2 complex.** RAG1/RAG2 enzymes are essential for V(D)J recombination, the process that generates TCR and immunoglobulin diversity. Defects in RAG1 or RAG2 cause Omenn syndrome or SCID with absent T and B cells (T- B- NK+ SCID). This patient has normal B cells and some T cells, making RAG deficiency unlikely. The problem is downstream of V(D)J recombination (TCR assembly/signaling).
- (B) **Defective positive selection of thymocytes in the thymus due to a lack of MHC class I presentation.** Lack of MHC class I presentation primarily affects CD8+ T-cell development. While CD3 signaling is involved in positive selection, the primary defect here is the CD3 complex itself, not MHC presentation. MHC class I/II defects lead to Bare Lymphocyte Syndrome (BLS), characterized by a lack of CD4+ or CD8+ T cells, respectively, but the underlying mechanism is different.
- (D) **Impaired development of the common gamma chain (γc) required for signaling through multiple cytokine receptors.** The common gamma chain (encoded by *IL2RG*) is a component of receptors for IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, and IL-21. Mutations in *IL2RG* cause X-linked SCID (T- B+ NK+ SCID), the most common form of SCID. While this also results in T- B+ NK+ SCID, the defect is in cytokine signaling, not TCR signaling via the CD3 complex. The genetic defect identified is in *CD3D*, not *IL2RG*.
- (E) **Defective expression of CD4 and CD8 co-receptors on the surface of developing thymocytes.** CD4 and CD8 are co-receptors that stabilize the TCR-MHC interaction and are involved in signaling. Defects in CD4 or CD8 expression can occur, but they are not the primary defect described by a *CD3D* mutation. The fundamental problem in this case is the inability of the TCR complex to signal effectively due to the faulty CD3 component.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | CD3D Deficiency (This Case) | RAG1/RAG2 Deficiency | MHC Class I/II Deficiency (BLS) | Common Gamma Chain (γc) Deficiency |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------- | :------------------- | :----------------------------- | :---------------------------------- |
| **Genetic Defect**      | *CD3D*                      | *RAG1*, *RAG2*       | *HLA-A, -B, -C* or *HLA-DR, -DQ, -DP* | *IL2RG*                            |
| **Cell Types Affected** | T cells (CD3+)              | T cells, B cells     | CD8+ T cells (Class I), CD4+ T cells (Class II) | T cells, B cells, NK cells         |
| **Flow Cytometry**      | Low CD3+, Normal CD19+, CD56+ | Absent CD3+, CD19+   | Absent CD8+ (Class I), Absent CD4+ (Class II) | Low CD3+, Normal CD19+, CD56+      |
| **Mechanism**           | Impaired TCR signaling       | Impaired V(D)J Recombination | Impaired T-cell selection     | Impaired cytokine signaling        |
| **Inheritance**         | Autosomal Recessive          | Autosomal Recessive  | Autosomal Recessive            | X-linked                            |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The *CD3D* mutation leads to a defect in the CD3 complex, which is essential for transducing signals from the T-cell receptor (TCR) upon antigen recognition. This impairs T-cell development (positive selection) and function, resulting in T- B+ NK+ SCID. Differentiating this from other SCID forms requires understanding the specific molecular defect (e.g., RAG deficiency affects V(D)J recombination, γc deficiency affects cytokine signaling).
