<a id='question-109'></a>

### Question 109: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 09 (Symptoms/Signs)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the ability to recognize the clinical presentation and immunological defect associated with Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD), specifically the inability of phagocytes to produce reactive oxygen species (ROS) due to defects in the NADPH oxidase complex, leading to recurrent bacterial and fungal infections and granuloma formation. (慢性肉芽肿病 (CGD) 的临床表现和免疫缺陷的识别，即吞噬细胞因 NADPH 氧化酶复合体缺陷而无法产生活性氧 (ROS)，导致反复的细菌和真菌感染以及肉芽肿形成。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 5-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with a 2-week history of fever, cough, and shortness of breath. He has a history of recurrent pneumonia, skin abscesses, and osteomyelitis since infancy, often requiring hospitalization and intravenous antibiotics. His mother reports that he has had difficulty gaining weight despite a good appetite. Physical examination reveals a cachectic boy with multiple draining skin lesions on his arms and legs, and crackles auscultated in both lung bases. A chest X-ray shows bilateral patchy infiltrates and hilar lymphadenopathy. Sputum culture grows *Staphylococcus aureus* and *Aspergillus fumigatus*. A neutrophil function test reveals that the neutrophils fail to produce a respiratory burst upon stimulation with phorbol myristate acetate (PMA).

(A) Deficiency in the complement component C5
(B) Defect in the CD40 ligand (CD40L)
(C) Mutation in the gene encoding the p47phox subunit of NADPH oxidase
(D) Absence of B cells and humoral immunity
(E) Defective T-cell receptor signaling leading to impaired cell-mediated immunity

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 5-year-old boy with recurrent infections since infancy. This points towards a congenital immunodeficiency.
- **Infection Types:** Recurrent pneumonia, skin abscesses, osteomyelitis. These are typical infections caused by catalase-positive organisms (like *Staphylococcus aureus*) and fungi (like *Aspergillus fumigatus*). The pattern suggests a defect in phagocytic killing.
- **Growth Failure:** Cachexia and difficulty gaining weight are common in chronic inflammatory/infectious states, especially immunodeficiencies.
- **Physical Exam:** Draining skin lesions and lung crackles confirm active infection.
- **Radiology:** Chest X-ray findings (infiltrates, lymphadenopathy) are consistent with severe pneumonia and possible granuloma formation.
- **Microbiology:** Isolation of *Staphylococcus aureus* and *Aspergillus fumigatus* from sputum strongly suggests a phagocytic defect, as these organisms are normally cleared by neutrophils and macrophages.
- **Neutrophil Function Test:** Failure of neutrophils to produce a respiratory burst (measured by ROS production) upon PMA stimulation is the definitive diagnostic clue for Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD). PMA activates protein kinase C, which phosphorylates and activates the NADPH oxidase complex.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD):** CGD is a primary immunodeficiency characterized by defects in the NADPH oxidase complex, which is responsible for generating superoxide radicals (O2-) and other reactive oxygen species (ROS) within phagocytes (neutrophils, macrophages).
- **NADPH Oxidase Complex:** This enzyme complex is located in the phagosomal membrane and consists of several subunits, including gp91phox (NOX2), p22phox, p47phox, p67phox, and p40phox.
- **Mechanism of Action:** Upon phagocytosis and activation (e.g., by PMA), the subunits assemble to form the active NADPH oxidase. This enzyme transfers electrons from NADPH in the cytoplasm to molecular oxygen (O2) in the phagosome, producing superoxide (O2-). Superoxide is then converted to other ROS, such as hydrogen peroxide (H2O2) and hypochlorous acid (HOCl), which are highly toxic to ingested microbes.
- **Pathophysiology in CGD:** Mutations in any of the genes encoding the NADPH oxidase subunits (most commonly *CYBB* encoding gp91phox, or *CYBA* encoding p22phox) lead to a non-functional or deficient complex. This results in impaired ROS production, rendering phagocytes unable to effectively kill certain ingested bacteria and fungi, particularly catalase-positive organisms. Catalase breaks down H2O2, so organisms that produce catalase can evade killing by H2O2-dependent mechanisms, but they are still susceptible to other ROS generated by NADPH oxidase.
- **Clinical Manifestations:** Recurrent, severe infections with catalase-positive bacteria (*S. aureus*, *Serratia marcescens*, *Burkholderia cepacia*, *Nocardia*) and fungi (*Aspergillus*, *Candida*). Granuloma formation occurs as the immune system attempts to wall off organisms that cannot be killed, leading to complications like obstruction (e.g., GI tract, urinary tract) and inflammation.
- **Diagnosis:** Dihydrorhodamine (DHR) test or nitroblue tetrazolium (NBT) test measures the respiratory burst activity of neutrophils. Flow cytometry can also assess NADPH oxidase complex assembly and function. Genetic testing confirms the specific mutation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Deficiency in the complement component C5:** C5 deficiency impairs the formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC) and opsonization via C5a. This leads to increased susceptibility to *Neisseria* infections (meningitis, gonorrhea) and recurrent bacterial infections, but not typically the specific pattern of catalase-positive bacterial and fungal infections with granulomas seen in CGD. The neutrophil respiratory burst test would be normal.
- (B) **Defect in the CD40 ligand (CD40L):** CD40L (on T cells) interacts with CD40 (on B cells, macrophages, dendritic cells). Deficiency leads to X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome. Patients have normal or elevated IgM levels but deficient IgG, IgA, and IgE production due to impaired class switching and B cell maturation. They are susceptible to opportunistic infections (like *Pneumocystis jirovecii*) but do not have a primary defect in phagocyte killing or the characteristic CGD infection pattern. The neutrophil respiratory burst test would be normal.
- (D) **Absence of B cells and humoral immunity:** This describes X-linked agammaglobulinemia (Bruton's agammaglobulinemia) due to mutations in the *BTK* gene. Patients lack mature B cells and immunoglobulins, leading to recurrent infections primarily with encapsulated bacteria (like *Streptococcus pneumoniae*, *Haemophilus influenzae*). Phagocyte function (including respiratory burst) is normal.
- (E) **Defective T-cell receptor signaling leading to impaired cell-mediated immunity:** This could describe various severe combined immunodeficiencies (SCID) or other T-cell defects. While T-cell defects lead to susceptibility to opportunistic infections (viral, fungal, intracellular bacteria), they do not directly cause the specific pattern of catalase-positive bacterial and fungal infections with granulomas and impaired phagocyte killing seen in CGD. The neutrophil respiratory burst test would be normal.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD) | C5 Deficiency        | CD40L Deficiency (Hyper-IgM) | XLA (B cell absence) | T-cell Deficiency (e.g., SCID) |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :------------------- | :--------------------------- | :------------------ | :----------------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | NADPH Oxidase Function               | Complement (MAC)     | T cell help for B cells      | B cell development | T cell development/function    |
| **Key Cell Affected**   | Phagocytes (Neutrophils, Macrophages) | Complement cascade  | T cells, B cells             | B cells            | T cells                       |
| **Characteristic Infections** | Catalase+ bacteria, *Aspergillus* | *Neisseria*          | Opportunistic (PCP, CMV)     | Encapsulated bacteria | Opportunistic (viral, fungal) |
| **Granulomas?**         | Yes                                  | No                  | No                          | No                 | No                            |
| **Neutrophil Burst Test** | Absent/Reduced                       | Normal              | Normal                      | Normal             | Normal                        |
| **Typical Gene Mutation** | *CYBB*, *CYBA*, etc.                 | *C5*                 | *CD40LG*                    | *BTK*              | Various (e.g., *IL2RG*, *RAG1/2*) |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
CGD is a phagocyte defect characterized by impaired respiratory burst due to NADPH oxidase dysfunction. Key clinical features include recurrent infections with catalase-positive organisms (*S. aureus*, *Aspergillus*) and granuloma formation. The diagnostic test is the neutrophil respiratory burst assay (DHR or NBT test). Differentiate CGD from other primary immunodeficiencies based on the specific cell type affected, the type of infections, and the presence/absence of granulomas.

<a id='question-110'></a>

### Question 110: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 10 (Increased)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of primary immunodeficiencies, specifically focusing on defects in T-cell function and their consequences, including susceptibility to opportunistic infections, graft rejection, and potential for autoimmune phenomena. It requires differentiating between various T-cell defects based on clinical presentation and immunological findings. (本题考察原发性免疫缺陷，特别是T细胞功能缺陷及其后果，包括对机会性感染的易感性、移植物排斥和潜在的自身免疫现象。需要根据临床表现和免疫学发现区分各种T细胞缺陷。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with recurrent severe infections, including Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia (PJP) and disseminated cytomegalovirus (CMV) infection, starting at 6 months of age. He has failed to thrive and exhibits chronic diarrhea. He received a bone marrow transplant from an HLA-matched unrelated donor at age 2. Despite initial engraftment, he developed severe graft-versus-host disease (GVHD), characterized by skin rash, liver dysfunction, and gastrointestinal ulceration. Immunological workup post-transplant revealed a markedly reduced number of CD4+ T cells and absent CD8+ T cells in the peripheral blood, despite normal B-cell and NK-cell counts. Flow cytometry analysis of the donor cells showed normal T-cell populations. Genetic testing of the patient revealed a homozygous mutation in the *RAG1* gene.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying immunological defect in this patient?

(A) Defective T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling due to mutations in the *ZAP70* gene.
(B) Impaired development of the thymus due to mutations in the *TBX1* gene.
(C) Failure of V(D)J recombination due to mutations in the *RAG1/RAG2* genes.
(D) Defective antigen presentation due to mutations in the *TAP1* gene.
(E) Impaired T-cell proliferation due to mutations in the *IL2RG* gene.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent severe infections (PJP, CMV) starting at 6 months:** Suggests a severe T-cell immunodeficiency, as T cells are crucial for controlling intracellular pathogens like *Pneumocystis* and viruses like CMV. The onset at 6 months points towards a primary immunodeficiency, as maternal antibodies wane around this time.
- **Failure to thrive, chronic diarrhea:** Common manifestations of severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) due to chronic infections and malabsorption.
- **Bone marrow transplant (BMT) with severe GVHD:** GVHD occurs when donor T cells recognize the recipient's tissues as foreign and attack them. The severity of GVHD suggests the recipient's tissues lack normal T-cell regulation or express antigens that are highly immunogenic to the donor T cells. This is often seen in patients with severe T-cell defects where the host lacks functional T cells to regulate the donor cells.
- **Post-transplant immunology: Reduced CD4+ T cells, absent CD8+ T cells:** This finding is crucial. It indicates that the patient's own T-cell development is severely impaired, leading to a lack of both helper (CD4+) and cytotoxic (CD8+) T cells. The donor cells have normal T-cell populations, confirming the defect is in the recipient.
- **Genetic testing: Homozygous mutation in the *RAG1* gene:** This is the definitive clue. *RAG1* and *RAG2* genes encode the Recombination Activating Genes 1 and 2, which are essential for V(D)J recombination, the process by which T-cell receptors (TCRs) and B-cell receptors (BCRs) are generated. Mutations in these genes lead to a failure of lymphocyte development.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **V(D)J Recombination:** This process is fundamental for adaptive immunity. It occurs in developing lymphocytes (T and B cells) in the bone marrow (B cells) and thymus (T cells). RAG1 and RAG2 proteins form a complex that introduces double-strand breaks into the DNA at specific recombination signal sequences (RSSs) flanking the variable (V), diversity (D), and joining (J) gene segments. These breaks are then processed by other enzymes (e.g., Artemis, DNA ligase IV) to join the segments, creating a unique TCR or BCR gene.
- ***RAG1/RAG2* Mutations:** Homozygous mutations in *RAG1* or *RAG2* result in severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) characterized by a profound lack of mature T cells and B cells (T-B-NK+ SCID). The absence of functional TCRs and BCRs prevents the selection and maturation of T and B lymphocytes in the thymus and bone marrow, respectively. NK cells are typically normal.
- **Clinical Manifestations of *RAG1/RAG2* SCID:** Patients present early in infancy with severe, recurrent infections (bacterial, viral, fungal, protozoal), failure to thrive, chronic diarrhea, and opportunistic infections like PJP and CMV.
- **GVHD in *RAG1/RAG2* SCID:** The lack of functional T cells in the recipient means they cannot mount an immune response against the donor's T cells, leading to uncontrolled proliferation of donor T cells and severe GVHD. This is a characteristic feature of SCID patients undergoing BMT.
- **Differential Diagnosis of SCID:** Other genetic defects causing SCID include mutations in *IL2RG* (X-linked SCID, affecting common gamma chain of IL receptors), *JAK3* (also affects common gamma chain signaling), *ADA* (adenosine deaminase deficiency, leading to toxic metabolite accumulation), *CD45* (tyrosine phosphatase deficiency), *CD3D/CD3E/CD3G* (TCR complex defects), *ZAP70* (TCR signaling defect), *TBX1* (DiGeorge syndrome, thymic hypoplasia), and *DCLRE1C* (Artemis deficiency, affecting V(D)J recombination and DNA repair).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling due to mutations in the *ZAP70* gene:** *ZAP70* is a tyrosine kinase crucial for TCR signaling. Mutations cause SCID characterized by absent or severely reduced CD8+ T cells and normal or reduced CD4+ T cells. Patients have susceptibility to infections but typically do not have absent CD8+ T cells and often have some residual CD4+ T cell function. The clinical picture (absent CD8+, reduced CD4+) doesn't perfectly match the vignette, and the genetic finding points to *RAG1*.
- (B) **Impaired development of the thymus due to mutations in the *TBX1* gene:** *TBX1* mutations cause DiGeorge syndrome (22q11.2 deletion syndrome), characterized by thymic hypoplasia/aplasia, leading to T-cell deficiency (variable severity), congenital heart defects, hypoparathyroidism (hypocalcemia), and characteristic facial features. While T-cell deficiency is present, B-cell numbers are usually normal, and the genetic defect is *TBX1*, not *RAG1*. The vignette describes absent T cells, not just reduced numbers due to thymic issues.
- (D) **Defective antigen presentation due to mutations in the *TAP1* gene:** *TAP1* (and *TAP2*) are components of the TAP transporter complex, which transports peptides into the endoplasmic reticulum for loading onto MHC class I molecules. Mutations cause Bare Lymphocyte Syndrome type I, characterized by impaired MHC class I expression and deficient CD8+ T-cell responses. CD4+ T-cell function is relatively normal. Patients are susceptible to viral infections but typically have normal or increased CD4+ T cells and B cells. This doesn't fit the vignette's description of absent CD4+ and CD8+ T cells.
- (E) **Impaired T-cell proliferation due to mutations in the *IL2RG* gene:** *IL2RG* encodes the common gamma chain (γc), a component of receptors for IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, and IL-21. Mutations cause X-linked SCID (the most common form of SCID). This results in a lack of T cells, NK cells, and B cells (T-B-NK- SCID). The vignette specifically mentions normal NK-cell counts, ruling out *IL2RG* deficiency.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | *RAG1/RAG2* Deficiency (SCID) | *ZAP70* Deficiency (SCID) | *TBX1* Deficiency (DiGeorge) | *TAP1* Deficiency (Bare Lymphocyte Syndrome I) | *IL2RG* Deficiency (X-linked SCID) |
| :---------------------- | :--------------------------- | :------------------------ | :--------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------- |
| **Genetic Defect**      | V(D)J Recombination          | TCR Signaling             | Thymic Development           | Antigen Presentation (MHC I)                  | IL Receptor Signaling (γc)        |
| **T-cell Development**  | Absent T & B cells (T-B-NK+) | Absent/Reduced CD8+, Normal/Reduced CD4+ (T-B+NK+) | Variable T-cell reduction (T-B+NK+) | Normal T cells, Defective CD8+ function (T-B+NK+) | Absent T, B, & NK cells (T-B-NK-) |
| **B-cell Development**  | Absent                      | Normal                    | Normal                       | Normal                                         | Absent                            |
| **NK-cell Development** | Normal                      | Normal                    | Normal                       | Normal                                         | Absent                            |
| **Key Infections**      | Broad (PJP, CMV, Bacterial)  | Viral (CMV, EBV)           | Broad (similar to T-cell deficiency) | Viral (CMV, HSV)                               | Broad (PJP, CMV, Bacterial)        |
| **GVHD Risk**           | High                        | High                      | Variable                     | Low (due to normal T cells)                     | High                              |
| **Genetic Inheritance** | Autosomal Recessive          | Autosomal Recessive        | Variable (often de novo)     | Autosomal Recessive                            | X-linked Recessive                |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Mutations in *RAG1* or *RAG2* genes cause SCID by blocking V(D)J recombination, leading to a lack of mature T and B lymphocytes (T-B-NK+ SCID). This results in severe susceptibility to opportunistic infections early in life and a high risk of GVHD following hematopoietic stem cell transplantation.
- **Differentials:** Remember to differentiate *RAG1/RAG2* SCID (T-B-NK+) from X-linked SCID (*IL2RG*, T-B-NK-), DiGeorge syndrome (*TBX1*, variable T-cell deficiency, thymic hypoplasia), *ZAP70* SCID (absent CD8+, reduced CD4+, T-B+NK+), and Bare Lymphocyte Syndrome I (*TAP1*, normal T cells, defective CD8+ function). The specific lymphocyte populations affected (T, B, NK) and the genetic defect are key distinguishing features.

<a id='question-111'></a>

### Question 111: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 11 (susceptibility)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of primary immunodeficiencies, specifically focusing on the clinical manifestations and underlying immunological defects associated with defects in T-cell development and function, particularly DiGeorge syndrome (22q11.2 deletion syndrome), and its impact on susceptibility to specific infections and autoimmune phenomena. (本题考察原发性免疫缺陷，特别是T细胞发育和功能缺陷，如DiGeorge综合征（22号染色体q11.2缺失），以及其对特定感染和自身免疫现象的易感性。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with recurrent sinopulmonary infections, including pneumonia and otitis media, starting in infancy. He also has a history of oral thrush unresponsive to standard antifungal therapy and failure to thrive. Physical examination reveals characteristic facial features (hypertelorism, short philtrum, low-set ears), cardiac abnormalities (conotruncal heart defect), and hypocalcemia leading to tetany. Immunological workup shows markedly reduced numbers of peripheral T lymphocytes (CD3+ and CD4+ cells) and absent T-cell proliferation in response to mitogens. B-cell numbers (CD19+) are normal, but immunoglobulin levels (IgG, IgA, IgM) are low, particularly IgA. He develops severe graft-versus-host disease (GVHD) after receiving a blood transfusion. Which of the following is the most likely underlying immunological defect?

(A) Defective B-cell maturation due to mutations in the BTK gene.
(B) Impaired T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling due to mutations in the ZAP-70 gene.
(C) Defective thymic development and T-cell maturation due to a deletion on chromosome 22q11.2.
(D) Defective phagocyte function due to mutations in the CYBB gene.
(E) Defective complement component C3 synthesis leading to impaired opsonization and membrane attack complex formation.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Recurrent sinopulmonary infections (pneumonia, otitis media) since infancy:** Suggests a defect in cell-mediated immunity (T-cells) or antibody production (B-cells), as these are crucial for defense against encapsulated bacteria and intracellular pathogens common in these sites.
*   **Oral thrush unresponsive to standard therapy:** Indicates impaired T-cell immunity, as T-cells are essential for controlling fungal infections like *Candida*.
*   **Failure to thrive:** Common in severe immunodeficiencies due to chronic infections and malabsorption.
*   **Characteristic facial features (hypertelorism, short philtrum, low-set ears), cardiac abnormalities (conotruncal heart defect), hypocalcemia (tetany):** This constellation of findings is highly suggestive of DiGeorge syndrome (22q11.2 deletion syndrome). Hypocalcemia results from parathyroid gland hypoplasia/aplasia, also linked to the 22q11.2 deletion.
*   **Markedly reduced peripheral T lymphocytes (CD3+, CD4+):** Confirms a severe T-cell deficiency.
*   **Absent T-cell proliferation in response to mitogens:** Indicates a functional defect in T-cells, likely due to impaired development or maturation.
*   **Normal B-cell numbers (CD19+), low immunoglobulin levels (IgG, IgA, IgM):** Suggests that B-cells are present but are not producing sufficient antibodies, likely due to lack of T-cell help (T-dependent antibody responses). Low IgA is particularly common in DiGeorge.
*   **Severe graft-versus-host disease (GVHD) after transfusion:** This is a classic manifestation of severe T-cell deficiency. Donor T-cells in the transfused blood recognize the recipient's tissues as foreign and mount an immune attack because the recipient's T-cells are unable to suppress this reaction.

These clues collectively point towards a primary immunodeficiency affecting T-cell development, consistent with DiGeorge syndrome.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **DiGeorge Syndrome (22q11.2 deletion syndrome):** Caused by a microdeletion on chromosome 22q11.2, affecting the development of the 3rd and 4th pharyngeal pouches. This leads to thymic hypoplasia or aplasia (resulting in T-cell deficiency), parathyroid hypoplasia/aplasia (hypocalcemia), and congenital heart defects (especially conotruncal defects like Tetralogy of Fallot, interrupted aortic arch).
*   **T-cell Deficiency:** The lack of a functional thymus impairs the maturation of T-lymphocytes. This results in low numbers of circulating T-cells (CD3+, CD4+, CD8+) and impaired T-cell function (e.g., inability to proliferate, produce cytokines, or provide help to B-cells).
*   **Clinical Consequences:** Patients are susceptible to infections typically controlled by T-cells, including viral (e.g., varicella), fungal (e.g., *Candida*, *Pneumocystis jirovecii*), and intracellular bacterial infections. They also have impaired antibody responses (especially T-dependent responses), leading to low immunoglobulin levels and susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria. Autoimmunity can also occur.
*   **Graft-versus-Host Disease (GVHD):** In severe T-cell deficiency, the patient lacks the regulatory T-cells needed to suppress allogeneic immune responses. When exposed to foreign lymphocytes (e.g., via blood transfusion or bone marrow transplant), the donor T-cells can proliferate unchecked and attack the recipient's tissues.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Defective B-cell maturation due to mutations in the BTK gene:** This describes X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA). Patients with XLA have normal T-cell numbers and function but lack mature B-cells and immunoglobulins. They are susceptible to encapsulated bacteria but typically do *not* have the characteristic facial features, cardiac defects, hypocalcemia, or severe T-cell deficiency seen in this case. They also do not typically develop severe GVHD after transfusion because their T-cells are normal.
*   **(B) Impaired T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling due to mutations in the ZAP-70 gene:** This describes ZAP-70 deficiency, a form of severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID). Patients have severely impaired T-cell and NK-cell function due to defective TCR/NK cell receptor signaling. While they have T-cell deficiency and susceptibility to similar infections, they do not typically have the characteristic constellation of DiGeorge syndrome features (cardiac defects, hypocalcemia, facial dysmorphism). The underlying defect is in intracellular signaling, not thymic development.
*   **(D) Defective phagocyte function due to mutations in the CYBB gene:** This describes Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD). Patients have impaired oxidative burst in phagocytes, leading to susceptibility to catalase-positive organisms (e.g., *Staphylococcus aureus*, *Aspergillus*). T-cell numbers and function are normal in CGD. The clinical presentation (recurrent bacterial and fungal infections, granuloma formation) and immunological findings are distinct from this case.
*   **(E) Defective complement component C3 synthesis leading to impaired opsonization and membrane attack complex formation:** This describes C3 deficiency, a form of complement deficiency. Patients are susceptible to recurrent infections with encapsulated bacteria (especially *Neisseria* species). T-cell numbers and function are normal. The clinical presentation and immunological findings are distinct from this case.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                     | DiGeorge Syndrome (22q11.2 deletion) | XLA (BTK mutation) | ZAP-70 Deficiency | CGD (CYBB mutation) | C3 Deficiency |
| :-------------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :---------------- | :---------------- | :----------------- | :------------ |
| **Primary Defect**          | Thymic hypoplasia/aplasia            | B-cell maturation | TCR signaling     | Phagocyte oxidase  | C3 synthesis  |
| **T-cell Numbers**          | Low                                  | Normal            | Low               | Normal             | Normal        |
| **B-cell Numbers**          | Normal                               | Absent            | Normal            | Normal             | Normal        |
| **Immunoglobulins**         | Low (esp. IgA)                       | Absent            | Low               | Normal             | Normal        |
| **Facial Features**         | Yes (Characteristic)                 | No                | No                | No                 | No            |
| **Cardiac Defects**         | Yes (Conotruncal)                    | No                | No                | No                 | No            |
| **Hypocalcemia**            | Yes                                  | No                | No                | No                 | No            |
| **GVHD Risk**               | High                                 | Low               | High              | Low                | Low           |
| **Typical Infections**       | Viral, Fungal, Intracellular Bac.    | Encapsulated Bac. | Viral, Fungal, Intracellular Bac. | Catalase+ Bac/Fungi | Encapsulated Bac. |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
DiGeorge syndrome (22q11.2 deletion) is characterized by a constellation of findings including T-cell deficiency (due to thymic hypoplasia), cardiac defects, hypocalcemia, and characteristic facial features. Severe T-cell deficiency predisposes patients to opportunistic infections and severe GVHD. Differentiating it from other primary immunodeficiencies like XLA (B-cell defect), SCID variants (e.g., ZAP-70 deficiency), phagocyte disorders (CGD), and complement deficiencies requires careful consideration of the specific clinical features and immunological findings.

<a id='question-112'></a>

### Question 112: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 12 (encapsulated)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of primary immunodeficiencies, specifically focusing on defects in T-cell development and function, particularly DiGeorge syndrome (22q11.2 deletion syndrome), and its associated immunological consequences, including susceptibility to specific infections and potential autoimmune phenomena. (本题考察原发性免疫缺陷，特别是T细胞发育和功能缺陷，例如DiGeorge综合征（22号染色体11.2区缺失），以及其相关的免疫学后果，包括对特定感染的易感性和潜在的自身免疫现象。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with recurrent sinopulmonary infections, including pneumonia and otitis media, starting in infancy. He also has a history of failure to thrive and developmental delay. Physical examination reveals characteristic facial features (hypertelorism, short philtrum, low-set ears), cardiac abnormalities (conotruncal heart defect), and hypocalcemia leading to tetany. Immunological workup shows markedly reduced numbers of peripheral T lymphocytes (CD3+ cells) and absent delayed-type hypersensitivity (DTH) skin reactions to Candida albicans. Flow cytometry reveals a severely decreased population of CD4+ and CD8+ T cells, but normal numbers of B cells (CD19+) and NK cells (CD56+). He is being treated for a persistent *Pneumocystis jirovecii* pneumonia (PJP) that is refractory to standard trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole (TMP-SMX) prophylaxis. Genetic testing confirms a heterozygous deletion on chromosome 22q11.2. Which of the following immunological defects is MOST likely responsible for his susceptibility to opportunistic infections like PJP and his failure to respond adequately to TMP-SMX prophylaxis?

(A) Defective complement component C3 leading to impaired opsonization and lysis of encapsulated bacteria.
(B) Defective phagocyte NADPH oxidase leading to impaired killing of catalase-positive organisms.
(C) Defective thymic development leading to impaired T-cell maturation and function.
(D) Defective B-cell class switching leading to elevated IgM and susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria.
(E) Defective CD40 ligand (CD40L) expression leading to impaired T-cell help for B-cell antibody production and macrophage activation.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent sinopulmonary infections (pneumonia, otitis media) since infancy**: Suggests an underlying immunodeficiency affecting defense against common respiratory pathogens.
- **Failure to thrive, developmental delay**: Common features associated with chronic illness and genetic syndromes.
- **Characteristic facial features (hypertelorism, short philtrum, low-set ears), cardiac abnormalities (conotruncal heart defect), hypocalcemia/tetany**: Classic triad (or tetrad including hypocalcemia) strongly suggestive of DiGeorge syndrome (22q11.2 deletion syndrome).
- **Markedly reduced peripheral T lymphocytes (CD3+), absent DTH**: Indicates a severe T-cell deficiency. DTH is a T-cell mediated response.
- **Flow cytometry: decreased CD4+ and CD8+ T cells, normal B cells (CD19+) and NK cells (CD56+)**: Confirms a selective T-cell defect, sparing B cells and NK cells.
- **Persistent *Pneumocystis jirovecii* pneumonia (PJP) refractory to TMP-SMX prophylaxis**: PJP is a classic opportunistic infection seen in severe T-cell immunodeficiencies (like AIDS or DiGeorge syndrome). Refractoriness suggests a profound defect in cell-mediated immunity required for controlling this organism.
- **Genetic testing: heterozygous deletion on chromosome 22q11.2**: Confirms the diagnosis of DiGeorge syndrome.

The constellation of clinical features (cardiac defects, facial dysmorphism, hypocalcemia) and immunological findings (severe T-cell deficiency, normal B/NK cells) points directly to DiGeorge syndrome. The underlying cause in DiGeorge syndrome is abnormal development of the 3rd and 4th pharyngeal pouches, leading to thymic hypoplasia or aplasia, parathyroid hypoplasia (causing hypocalcemia), and conotruncal cardiac defects. The thymic defect impairs T-cell maturation, resulting in severe T-cell lymphopenia and functional defects. This explains the susceptibility to opportunistic infections like PJP, which require cell-mediated immunity for clearance.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **DiGeorge Syndrome (22q11.2 deletion syndrome)**: Caused by a microdeletion on chromosome 22q11.2, affecting the *TBX1* gene, which is crucial for the development of the 3rd and 4th pharyngeal pouches.
- **Thymic Hypoplasia/Aplasia**: The thymus is the primary site of T-cell maturation. Impaired thymic development leads to a severe reduction in the number of mature T lymphocytes (CD4+ helper T cells and CD8+ cytotoxic T cells).
- **T-cell Deficiency**: The lack of functional T cells compromises both humoral immunity (T-cell help is required for most antibody responses, especially class switching and affinity maturation) and cell-mediated immunity (essential for controlling intracellular pathogens and fungi like *Pneumocystis jirovecii*).
- ***Pneumocystis jirovecii* Pneumonia (PJP)**: An opportunistic fungal infection primarily affecting immunocompromised individuals, especially those with T-cell defects (e.g., AIDS, DiGeorge syndrome, post-transplant patients on immunosuppression). Cell-mediated immunity (specifically CD4+ T cells) is critical for controlling *P. jirovecii*.
- **TMP-SMX Prophylaxis**: Standard prophylaxis for PJP in high-risk individuals. Failure of prophylaxis in this patient indicates a profound T-cell defect, as TMP-SMX primarily works by inhibiting folate synthesis, which is less effective against organisms like *P. jirovecii* without adequate host immune response. The underlying T-cell defect is the primary reason for susceptibility and lack of response.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective complement component C3**: C3 deficiency impairs opsonization and lysis, primarily leading to susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria (e.g., *Streptococcus pneumoniae*, *Haemophilus influenzae*, *Neisseria meningitidis*). While recurrent bacterial infections occur, the characteristic features of DiGeorge syndrome (cardiac defects, facial features, hypocalcemia, severe T-cell deficiency, PJP) are not explained by C3 deficiency.
- (B) **Defective phagocyte NADPH oxidase**: This defect causes Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD). Patients with CGD have impaired killing of catalase-positive organisms (e.g., *Staphylococcus aureus*, *Aspergillus*, *Serratia*, *Burkholderia cepacia*, *Nocardia*). They typically present with recurrent bacterial and fungal infections, often forming granulomas. This does not fit the clinical picture of DiGeorge syndrome or the specific susceptibility to PJP.
- (D) **Defective B-cell class switching**: This leads to Hyper-IgM syndrome (e.g., due to CD40L deficiency or AID deficiency). Patients have normal or elevated IgM levels but low IgG, IgA, and IgE. They are susceptible to extracellular bacterial infections, similar to C3 deficiency, but typically have normal T-cell numbers and function. This does not explain the T-cell deficiency, cardiac defects, or facial features seen in DiGeorge syndrome.
- (E) **Defective CD40 ligand (CD40L) expression**: CD40L (on T cells) interacts with CD40 (on B cells, macrophages, dendritic cells). Deficiency (X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome) impairs T-cell help for B-cell class switching and antibody production, leading to susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria and opportunistic infections like *P. jirovecii*. However, CD40L deficiency primarily affects T-cell *help* function, not T-cell *development* or numbers. Patients with CD40L deficiency typically have normal T-cell counts and DTH responses, unlike the patient described. While PJP susceptibility is shared, the underlying mechanism (impaired T-cell help vs. impaired T-cell maturation) and the associated clinical features (cardiac, facial, hypocalcemia) are distinct.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | DiGeorge Syndrome (22q11.2 del) | C3 Deficiency | CGD (NADPH Oxidase Defect) | Hyper-IgM Syndrome (CD40L Defect) |
|-------------------------|---------------------------------|---------------|-----------------------------|-----------------------------------|
| **Primary Defect**      | Thymic Development (T-cell)     | Complement    | Phagocyte Killing           | T-cell Help (CD40L-CD40)          |
| **T-cell Numbers**      | Severely Reduced (CD4+, CD8+)   | Normal        | Normal                      | Normal                            |
| **B-cell Numbers**      | Normal                          | Normal        | Normal                      | Normal                            |
| **NK cell Numbers**     | Normal                          | Normal        | Normal                      | Normal                            |
| **Key Infections**      | PJP, Viral, Fungal              | Encapsulated Bacteria | Catalase+ Bacteria, Fungi | PJP, Encapsulated Bacteria        |
| **Other Features**      | Cardiac Defects, Facial Dysmorphism, Hypocalcemia | None specific | Granuloma Formation        | None specific                     |
| **Mechanism of PJP Susceptibility** | Impaired T-cell mediated immunity | Normal T-cell function | Normal T-cell function | Impaired T-cell help (indirectly) |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
DiGeorge syndrome (22q11.2 deletion) is characterized by thymic hypoplasia leading to severe T-cell deficiency, resulting in susceptibility to opportunistic infections like PJP, viral infections, and fungal infections. Key clinical features include cardiac defects, facial dysmorphism, and hypocalcemia. Differentiate it from other primary immunodeficiencies based on the specific cell lineage affected (T-cells vs. B-cells vs. phagocytes vs. complement) and the associated clinical manifestations. Remember that PJP is a hallmark of severe T-cell defects.

<a id='question-113'></a>

### Question 113: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 13 (bacteria)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of primary immunodeficiencies, specifically focusing on the clinical manifestations and underlying immunological defects associated with defects in phagocyte function, particularly Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD), and its susceptibility to specific bacterial and fungal infections. (本题考察原发性免疫缺陷，特别是吞噬细胞功能缺陷，如慢性肉芽肿病（CGD），以及其对特定细菌和真菌感染的易感性。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with a 2-week history of fever, cough, and shortness of breath. He has a history of recurrent pneumonia, skin abscesses, and lymphadenitis since infancy, often requiring hospitalization. His infections are typically caused by *Staphylococcus aureus*, *Serratia marcescens*, *Burkholderia cepacia*, and *Aspergillus* species. Physical examination reveals a cachectic child with multiple draining skin lesions and cervical lymphadenopathy. Chest X-ray shows bilateral infiltrates and cavitation. A neutrophil function test reveals that neutrophils from this patient fail to produce a respiratory burst upon stimulation with phorbol myristate acetate (PMA). Which of the following is the most likely underlying defect in this patient?

(A) Defective synthesis of the Fc receptor gamma chain (CD32), leading to impaired antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC).
(B) Defective synthesis of the NADPH oxidase complex, leading to impaired production of reactive oxygen species (ROS).
(C) Defective synthesis of the CD40 ligand (CD40L), leading to impaired T-cell dependent B-cell activation and macrophage activation.
(D) Defective synthesis of the C5 convertase, leading to impaired formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC) and increased susceptibility to Neisseria infections.
(E) Defective synthesis of the mannose-binding lectin (MBL), leading to impaired activation of the lectin pathway of complement.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 6-year-old boy with recurrent, severe infections since infancy. This points towards a congenital immunodeficiency.
- **Infection Types:** Recurrent pneumonia, skin abscesses, lymphadenitis. These are typical infections seen in phagocyte defects.
- **Specific Pathogens:** *Staphylococcus aureus*, *Serratia marcescens*, *Burkholderia cepacia*, *Aspergillus* species. This constellation of catalase-positive bacteria and fungi is highly characteristic of Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD). These organisms produce superoxide radicals, which are normally detoxified by the phagocyte's respiratory burst.
- **Physical Exam:** Cachexia, draining skin lesions, lymphadenopathy, bilateral infiltrates with cavitation on chest X-ray. These findings are consistent with severe, chronic granulomatous infections.
- **Diagnostic Test:** Neutrophil function test shows failure of respiratory burst upon PMA stimulation. PMA activates protein kinase C, which phosphorylates and activates NADPH oxidase. Failure to produce a respiratory burst confirms a defect in this pathway.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying defect. Based on the clinical picture and the neutrophil function test, the defect is in the respiratory burst mechanism.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD):** CGD is a primary immunodeficiency characterized by defects in the NADPH oxidase complex. This complex is essential for the respiratory burst in phagocytes (neutrophils, macrophages), which generates reactive oxygen species (ROS) like superoxide anion (O2-), hydrogen peroxide (H2O2), and hydroxyl radicals (OH•). These ROS are crucial for killing certain ingested bacteria and fungi, particularly catalase-positive organisms.
- **NADPH Oxidase Complex:** This multi-subunit enzyme complex spans the phagosomal membrane and the plasma membrane. Key components include gp91phox (NOX2, encoded by *CYBB* gene on X chromosome - X-linked CGD), p22phox (encoded by *CYBA* gene), p47phox (encoded by *NCF1* gene), p67phox (encoded by *NCF2* gene), and p40phox (encoded by *NCF4* gene). Upon phagocytosis and activation, these subunits assemble to form the active NADPH oxidase.
- **Respiratory Burst:** The assembled NADPH oxidase transfers electrons from NADPH in the cytoplasm to molecular oxygen (O2) within the phagosome, generating superoxide anion (O2-). Superoxide dismutase converts O2- to hydrogen peroxide (H2O2). Myeloperoxidase (MPO) then converts H2O2 and chloride ions (Cl-) into hypochlorous acid (HOCl, bleach), a potent antimicrobial agent.
- **Pathogens in CGD:** Patients with CGD are susceptible to infections with catalase-positive organisms. Catalase breaks down H2O2, preventing its accumulation and subsequent conversion to HOCl by MPO. Organisms like *S. aureus*, *Serratia*, *Burkholderia*, *Nocardia*, and *Aspergillus* are catalase-positive and thrive in the absence of a functional respiratory burst.
- **Clinical Manifestations:** Recurrent bacterial and fungal infections, including pneumonia, abscesses (skin, liver, lung), lymphadenitis, osteomyelitis, and granuloma formation (hence the name).
- **Diagnosis:** Dihydrorhodamine (DHR) test or nitroblue tetrazolium (NBT) test measures the production of ROS by neutrophils. Flow cytometry can assess the expression of NADPH oxidase subunits. Genetic testing identifies mutations in the relevant genes.

##### 3. Mechanism of Action of PMA:
- Phorbol myristate acetate (PMA) is a protein kinase C (PKC) activator. PKC activation leads to the phosphorylation of several proteins, including components of the NADPH oxidase complex (specifically p47phox). This phosphorylation is required for the assembly and activation of the NADPH oxidase complex, initiating the respiratory burst. Failure to produce ROS upon PMA stimulation directly indicates a defect in the NADPH oxidase pathway.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Defective synthesis of the Fc receptor gamma chain (CD32), leading to impaired antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC). CD32 is the low-affinity Fc receptor for IgG found on various cells, including phagocytes. Defects in CD32 are associated with impaired ADCC and potentially increased susceptibility to certain infections, but not the specific pattern seen in CGD (catalase-positive organisms, lack of respiratory burst). This defect would primarily affect NK cell function and macrophage-mediated killing via IgG opsonization.
- (C) Defective synthesis of the CD40 ligand (CD40L), leading to impaired T-cell dependent B-cell activation and macrophage activation. CD40L (on T cells) binds to CD40 (on B cells, macrophages, dendritic cells). This interaction is crucial for T-cell help to B cells (isotype switching, affinity maturation, memory B cell formation) and for macrophage activation (e.g., against intracellular pathogens like *Mycobacterium*). Deficiency causes X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome, characterized by normal or elevated IgM levels but low IgG, IgA, and IgE, leading to susceptibility to opportunistic infections (e.g., *Pneumocystis jirovecii*). This does not fit the clinical picture or the neutrophil function test result.
- (D) Defective synthesis of the C5 convertase, leading to impaired formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC) and increased susceptibility to Neisseria infections. C5 convertase is part of the complement system. Deficiency in terminal complement components (C5-C9) impairs MAC formation, leading to increased susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria, particularly *Neisseria* species (e.g., *N. meningitidis*, *N. gonorrhoeae*). This does not explain the susceptibility to catalase-positive organisms or the lack of respiratory burst.
- (E) Defective synthesis of the mannose-binding lectin (MBL), leading to impaired activation of the lectin pathway of complement. MBL is a pattern recognition molecule that binds to mannose residues on microbial surfaces, activating the lectin pathway of complement. MBL deficiency is associated with increased susceptibility to infections, particularly encapsulated bacteria and fungi, but it does not cause the specific constellation of infections seen in CGD or affect neutrophil respiratory burst function.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD) | X-linked Hyper-IgM Syndrome (CD40L def.) | Complement Deficiency (C5-C9 def.) | MBL Deficiency | CD32 Deficiency |
|-------------------------|---------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------|------------------------------------|----------------|-----------------|
| **Primary Defect**      | NADPH Oxidase Complex                 | CD40 Ligand (CD40L)                       | Terminal Complement Components (C5-C9) | MBL            | FcγRII (CD32)    |
| **Cell Type Affected**  | Phagocytes (Neutrophils, Macrophages) | T cells, B cells, Macrophages             | N/A (Soluble proteins)            | N/A            | Phagocytes, NK cells |
| **Key Mechanism Defect**| Impaired Respiratory Burst (ROS prod.)| Impaired T-B cell interaction, Macrophage activation | Impaired MAC formation            | Impaired Lectin Pathway | Impaired ADCC |
| **Characteristic Pathogens** | Catalase-positive bacteria/fungi (*S. aureus*, *Serratia*, *Aspergillus*) | Opportunistic pathogens (*Pneumocystis*, *Cryptosporidium*) | *Neisseria* spp. | Encapsulated bacteria/fungi | Variable |
| **Key Lab Finding**     | Absent/Reduced ROS production (DHR/NBT test) | High IgM, Low IgG/IgA/IgE               | Low CH50, Impaired MAC lysis      | Low MBL levels | Impaired ADCC |
| **Clinical Manifestations** | Recurrent abscesses, granulomas, pneumonia | Recurrent sinopulmonary infections, opportunistic infections | Recurrent *Neisseria* infections | Increased susceptibility to infections | Variable |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
CGD is a primary immunodeficiency caused by defects in the NADPH oxidase complex, leading to impaired phagocyte killing of catalase-positive organisms. The key diagnostic feature is the inability of neutrophils to produce a respiratory burst. Differentiate CGD from other phagocyte defects (e.g., Leukocyte Adhesion Deficiency), T-cell defects (e.g., DiGeorge syndrome, SCID), B-cell defects (e.g., XLA, CVID), and complement deficiencies based on the specific pathogens involved and the underlying immunological mechanism. Remember the classic pathogens for CGD: *Staphylococcus aureus*, *Serratia marcescens*, *Burkholderia cepacia*, *Nocardia*, and *Aspergillus*.

<a id='question-114'></a>

### Question 114: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 14 (bloodborne)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of primary immunodeficiencies, specifically focusing on the clinical manifestations and underlying immunological defects associated with Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome (WAS), a combined immunodeficiency characterized by eczema, thrombocytopenia, and recurrent infections, often due to defects in T-cell and B-cell function and cytoskeletal organization. (本题考察原发性免疫缺陷病，重点是Wiskott-Aldrich综合征（WAS），这是一种联合免疫缺陷，其特征是湿疹、血小板减少症和复发性感染，通常是由于T细胞和B细胞功能以及细胞骨架组织缺陷所致。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with a history of recurrent severe bacterial infections (otitis media, pneumonia, sepsis) starting in infancy, chronic eczema, and easy bruising/bleeding. His mother reports that he was vaccinated according to schedule but did not develop protective antibody titers after receiving the pneumococcal conjugate vaccine (PCV13). Physical examination reveals petechiae on his lower extremities and generalized eczematous lesions. Laboratory studies show thrombocytopenia (platelet count 25,000/µL) with small platelet size, low serum IgM levels, and normal or elevated IgA and IgG levels. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes reveals decreased expression of CD43 (sialophorin) on T cells and platelets. The patient is being considered for hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (HSCT) due to the severity of his condition. Which of the following cellular processes is most likely defective in this patient?

(A) T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling complex assembly
(B) B-cell receptor (BCR) signaling complex assembly
(C) Actin cytoskeleton polymerization and cell motility
(D) Complement component C3 convertase formation
(E) Phagosome-lysosome fusion in macrophages

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent severe bacterial infections:** Suggests a defect in humoral (B-cell/antibody) and/or cellular (T-cell) immunity. The failure to respond to PCV13 specifically points towards a B-cell defect in antibody production, particularly polysaccharide-specific antibodies.
- **Chronic eczema:** A common feature of several primary immunodeficiencies, including WAS and Hyper-IgE syndrome.
- **Easy bruising/bleeding, thrombocytopenia with small platelets:** Highly characteristic of Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome (WAS). The small platelet size is a key distinguishing feature.
- **Low serum IgM, normal/high IgA/IgG:** This specific immunoglobulin pattern is typical for WAS. Patients often have impaired antibody responses to polysaccharide antigens.
- **Decreased CD43 expression:** CD43 (sialophorin) is a heavily glycosylated transmembrane protein expressed on leukocytes, including T cells and platelets. Its expression is significantly reduced or absent in WAS due to the underlying genetic defect.
- **Consideration for HSCT:** Indicates a severe, life-threatening primary immunodeficiency.
- **Overall picture:** The combination of recurrent infections, eczema, thrombocytopenia with small platelets, low IgM, and decreased CD43 expression strongly points to Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Wiskott-Aldrich Syndrome (WAS):** An X-linked recessive disorder caused by mutations in the *WAS* gene, which encodes the Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome protein (WASP).
- **WASP Function:** WASP is a crucial cytoskeletal adapter protein expressed in hematopoietic cells, particularly T cells, B cells, platelets, and hematopoietic stem cells. It plays a vital role in actin polymerization, cell motility, cell-cell adhesion, and signal transduction downstream of various receptors, including the T-cell receptor (TCR), B-cell receptor (BCR), Fc receptors, and chemokine receptors.
- **Defective Actin Cytoskeleton:** The primary function of WASP is to regulate the actin cytoskeleton. It interacts with various actin-binding proteins like WAVE/Scar proteins, Arp2/3 complex, and N-WASP, promoting actin polymerization and lamellipodia formation, which are essential for cell migration, adhesion, and synapse formation (e.g., immunological synapse).
- **Immunological Consequences:**
    - **T-cell dysfunction:** Impaired T-cell migration, synapse formation, and signaling lead to defective T-cell activation, proliferation, and cytokine production. This contributes to susceptibility to opportunistic infections.
    - **B-cell dysfunction:** Defective B-cell migration, synapse formation, and signaling impair B-cell activation, differentiation into plasma cells, and antibody production, particularly against polysaccharide antigens. This explains the low IgM and poor response to vaccines like PCV13.
    - **Platelet dysfunction:** WASP is essential for platelet formation and function. Its absence leads to thrombocytopenia and the production of abnormally small platelets due to defective actin-dependent membrane remodeling during megakaryocyte maturation and platelet formation.
- **CD43 Expression:** CD43 is a sialoglycoprotein involved in leukocyte adhesion and migration. Its expression is dependent on proper cytoskeletal organization, which is disrupted in WAS due to the lack of functional WASP. Therefore, decreased CD43 expression is a characteristic finding in WAS.

##### 3. Link to the Question Stem:** The question asks for the most likely defective cellular process in a patient presenting with classic features of WAS. Based on the known function of the WASP protein, the most direct consequence of its deficiency is the disruption of actin cytoskeleton polymerization and cell motility, which underlies the T-cell, B-cell, and platelet abnormalities observed in this patient.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling complex assembly:** While WASP is involved in TCR signaling downstream of the receptor (e.g., through interaction with SLP-76), the primary defect is not the assembly of the TCR complex itself (which involves CD3 subunits, zeta chain, etc.). The defect is in the downstream signaling and cytoskeletal reorganization required for effective T-cell activation and function. WASP deficiency impairs the formation of the immunological synapse, which relies on actin polymerization.
- (B) **B-cell receptor (BCR) signaling complex assembly:** Similar to the TCR, WASP participates in BCR signaling downstream of the receptor complex (e.g., through interaction with SLP-76). The primary defect isn't the assembly of the BCR complex itself (which involves Igα/Igβ, CD79a/CD79b, etc.). The defect is in the downstream signaling and cytoskeletal changes needed for B-cell activation, migration, and antibody production. WASP deficiency impairs the formation of the immunological synapse, which relies on actin polymerization.
- (D) **Complement component C3 convertase formation:** This process is part of the complement cascade, which is generally intact in WAS. Defects in complement components lead to different clinical presentations (e.g., recurrent Neisseria infections in C5-C9 deficiency, increased autoimmune disease risk in C1q/C4/C2 deficiency). WAS is not primarily a complement disorder.
- (E) **Phagosome-lysosome fusion in macrophages:** This process is crucial for intracellular killing by macrophages. Defects in this pathway are seen in conditions like Chediak-Higashi syndrome (due to *LYST* gene mutations affecting lysosomal trafficking) or specific macrophage disorders. WAS primarily affects lymphocytes and platelets, not macrophage function in this specific way. While patients with WAS are susceptible to infections, the underlying defect is not in macrophage phagosome-lysosome fusion.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Wiskott-Aldrich Syndrome (WAS) | X-linked Agammaglobulinemia (XLA) | Common Variable Immunodeficiency (CVID) | Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID) | Hyper-IgE Syndrome (HIES) |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------- | :------------------------ |
| **Genetic Basis**        | *WAS* gene (X-linked)          | *BTK* gene (X-linked)             | Variable (often *TNFRSF13B*, *ICOS*)   | Variable (e.g., *IL2RG*, *RAG1/2*)      | *STAT3* gene (AD)         |
| **Key Features**        | Eczema, Thrombocytopenia (small platelets), Recurrent Infections, Low IgM | Recurrent Infections, Absent B cells, Low/Absent Ig | Recurrent Infections, Low IgG/IgA/IgM, Normal B cells | Severe Infections (bacterial, viral, fungal), Failure to Thrive | Eczema, Recurrent Staph Infections, High IgE, Skeletal Abnormalities |
| **Platelets**            | Thrombocytopenia, Small size   | Normal                             | Normal                                 | Normal                                   | Normal                    |
| **Ig Levels**           | Low IgM, Normal/High IgA/IgG   | Very Low IgG, IgA, IgM             | Low IgG, IgA, and/or IgM                | Very Low IgG, IgA, IgM                   | Very High IgE             |
| **Cellular Defect**     | WASP deficiency, Actin cytoskeleton | BTK deficiency, B-cell development | Variable, B-cell differentiation/function | Variable, T-cell and/or B-cell development | STAT3 deficiency, Th17 pathway |
| **CD43 Expression**     | Decreased/Absent               | Normal                             | Normal                                 | Normal                                   | Normal                    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome is characterized by the triad of eczema, thrombocytopenia (with small platelets), and recurrent infections, along with low IgM levels and decreased CD43 expression. The underlying defect is in the WASP protein, which disrupts actin cytoskeleton organization, affecting T-cell, B-cell, and platelet function. Differentiating WAS from other primary immunodeficiencies requires recognizing this specific constellation of findings, especially the platelet abnormalities and low IgM. Key differentials include XLA (absent B cells, panhypogammaglobulinemia, normal platelets), CVID (later onset, normal B cells but impaired function, variable Ig levels), SCID (more severe, early onset, profound T and B cell defects), and HIES (high IgE, staphylococcal infections, skeletal issues).

<a id='question-115'></a>

### Question 115: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 15 (viruses,)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of primary immunodeficiencies, specifically focusing on defects in T-cell function and their implications for viral infections and graft rejection. The core concept is the inability of T cells to recognize and eliminate virally infected cells or allogeneic cells, leading to specific clinical manifestations. (本题考察原发性免疫缺陷，特别是T细胞功能缺陷及其在病毒感染和移植物排斥中的表现。核心概念是T细胞无法识别和清除病毒感染细胞或异体细胞，导致特定临床表现。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy, born to consanguineous parents, presents with recurrent severe viral infections, including persistent cytomegalovirus (CMV) pneumonia and disseminated varicella-zoster virus (VZV) infection. He has failed to thrive and exhibits chronic diarrhea. He received a hematopoietic stem cell transplant (HSCT) from an HLA-matched unrelated donor 6 months ago for severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID). Despite initial engraftment, he is now experiencing acute graft-versus-host disease (GVHD), characterized by severe skin rash, cholestatic jaundice, and gastrointestinal ulceration. Flow cytometry analysis of his peripheral blood lymphocytes reveals a profound deficiency of CD8+ T cells and a normal number of CD4+ T cells, B cells, and NK cells. Further investigation shows that his remaining CD4+ T cells are unable to proliferate in response to mitogenic stimulation (e.g., PHA) or allogeneic stimuli. His serum levels of IL-2 are markedly reduced upon stimulation.

Which of the following cellular defects is most likely responsible for this patient's susceptibility to severe viral infections and acute GVHD following HSCT?

(A) Defective expression of the T-cell receptor (TCR) complex on T cells.
(B) Impaired development of the thymus due to mutations in the *AIRE* gene.
(C) Defective signaling downstream of the TCR due to mutations in the *ZAP-70* gene.
(D) Defective expression of CD3 complex components on T cells.
(E) Defective expression of the common gamma chain (γc) subunit shared by multiple cytokine receptors.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 3-year-old boy, consanguinity (suggests autosomal recessive inheritance).
- **Clinical Presentation:** Recurrent severe viral infections (CMV pneumonia, disseminated VZV), failure to thrive, chronic diarrhea. This points towards a severe T-cell immunodeficiency, as T cells are crucial for controlling viral infections.
- **Diagnosis:** History of SCID requiring HSCT.
- **Post-HSCT Complication:** Acute GVHD. GVHD occurs when donor T cells recognize host tissues as foreign and attack them. The severity of GVHD suggests a potential issue with the recipient's immune system or the donor T cells' ability to be regulated.
- **Immunophenotyping:** Profound deficiency of CD8+ T cells, normal CD4+ T cells, B cells, NK cells. This pattern is unusual for typical SCID (which usually affects both CD4+ and CD8+ T cells) but can be seen in specific T-cell defects.
- **Functional Assay:** Remaining CD4+ T cells fail to proliferate in response to mitogens (PHA) or allogeneic stimuli. This indicates a defect in T-cell activation or signaling, not just a lack of T cells.
- **Cytokine Profile:** Markedly reduced IL-2 production upon stimulation. IL-2 is a key cytokine produced by activated T cells, essential for T-cell proliferation and function. Reduced IL-2 suggests a defect in the signaling pathway required for T-cell activation and cytokine production.
- **Connecting the Dots:** The patient has a T-cell defect (manifesting as severe viral infections and abnormal T-cell function) that was initially diagnosed as SCID. After HSCT, he develops severe GVHD, which is typically mediated by donor T cells. However, the *recipient's* T cells (specifically the remaining CD4+ cells) are functionally impaired, unable to proliferate or produce IL-2. This suggests a defect in the T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling pathway, which is essential for both T-cell activation and the regulation of GVHD. A defect in the early signaling components like ZAP-70 would impair both T-cell activation (leading to viral susceptibility) and the ability of donor T cells to be effectively suppressed by the recipient's T cells (leading to severe GVHD).

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **T-cell Activation Pathway:** Antigen presentation via MHC molecules to the TCR complex on T cells initiates signaling. The TCR complex consists of the TCR itself (αβ or γδ) and associated CD3 molecules (γ, δ, ε, ζ chains). Upon TCR engagement, the ITAMs (Immunoreceptor Tyrosine-based Activation Motifs) on the CD3ζ chain become phosphorylated by Lck kinase. These phosphorylated ITAMs recruit and activate ZAP-70 (Zeta-chain Associated Protein kinase 70). ZAP-70 then phosphorylates downstream targets like LAT (Linker for Activation of T cells), leading to the activation of signaling cascades involving PLCγ, PKC, and ultimately transcription factors like NFAT, AP-1, and NF-κB. These transcription factors induce the expression of genes encoding cytokines (like IL-2), cell surface receptors, and other molecules necessary for T-cell proliferation and effector function.
- **ZAP-70 Deficiency:** Mutations in the *ZAP-70* gene cause a specific form of SCID characterized by a normal number of T cells (or even slightly elevated) but functionally defective T cells. These T cells cannot effectively signal downstream of the TCR, leading to impaired proliferation, cytokine production (especially IL-2), and cytotoxic function. Patients are highly susceptible to viral infections. The defect affects both CD4+ and CD8+ T cells, although CD8+ cells are often more severely affected, explaining the patient's phenotype. Importantly, because the T cells cannot signal properly, they are also unable to effectively regulate GVHD, leading to severe reactions post-HSCT.
- **Other Options:**
    - (A) Defective TCR expression: Would lead to a lack of T cells or severely impaired T-cell development, typically presenting as SCID with very low T-cell counts, not the described immunophenotype.
    - (B) Defective *AIRE* expression: Causes Autoimmune Polyendocrinopathy-Candidiasis-Ectodermal Dystrophy (APECED), an autoimmune disease, not a primary immunodeficiency characterized by viral susceptibility and GVHD.
    - (D) Defective CD3 expression: Similar to defective TCR expression, would lead to severely impaired T-cell development and very low T-cell counts.
    - (E) Defective common gamma chain (γc): Causes X-linked SCID (SCID-X1), affecting T cells, B cells, and NK cells. While it causes severe viral infections, the immunophenotype typically shows low counts of T cells, B cells, and NK cells, which doesn't match the patient's profile (normal B cells and NK cells, deficient CD8+ T cells, normal CD4+ T cells initially).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Defective expression of the T-cell receptor (TCR) complex on T cells: This would result in a severe lack of functional T cells, typically presenting as classic SCID with very low lymphocyte counts. The patient has normal numbers of CD4+ T cells, B cells, and NK cells, and even though CD8+ T cells are deficient, the remaining CD4+ T cells show impaired *function* (proliferation, IL-2 production), suggesting a post-TCR signaling defect rather than a primary TCR expression defect.
- (B) Impaired development of the thymus due to mutations in the *AIRE* gene: *AIRE* mutations cause APECED, an autoimmune disorder characterized by endocrine failure, ectodermal dystrophy, and chronic mucocutaneous candidiasis. It is not a primary immunodeficiency causing susceptibility to viral infections or GVHD in this manner.
- (D) Defective expression of CD3 complex components on T cells: The CD3 complex is essential for TCR signaling. Severe defects in CD3 components lead to a profound block in T-cell development, resulting in very low T-cell numbers, similar to option (A). The patient has a normal number of CD4+ T cells, indicating that CD3 expression and early T-cell development are likely intact, but downstream signaling is impaired.
- (E) Defective expression of the common gamma chain (γc) subunit shared by multiple cytokine receptors: This causes X-linked SCID (SCID-X1). The γc subunit is part of receptors for IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, and IL-21. Deficiency leads to impaired T-cell and NK-cell development (due to lack of IL-7 and IL-15 signaling) and B-cell development (due to lack of IL-4 signaling). The typical immunophenotype is low T cells, low B cells, and low NK cells, which contradicts the patient's findings of normal B cells and NK cells and a specific deficiency in CD8+ T cells with functional impairment of remaining CD4+ T cells.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | ZAP-70 Deficiency (Correct Answer) | Defective TCR/CD3 Expression | X-linked SCID (γc deficiency) | APECED (*AIRE* mutation) |
| :---------------------- | :--------------------------------- | :--------------------------- | :----------------------------- | :----------------------- |
| **Inheritance**         | Autosomal Recessive                | Autosomal Recessive          | X-linked Recessive             | Autosomal Recessive      |
| **T-cell Count**        | Normal/Slightly Elevated           | Very Low                    | Very Low                      | Normal                  |
| **B-cell Count**        | Normal                            | Normal                      | Very Low                      | Normal                  |
| **NK-cell Count**       | Normal                            | Normal                      | Very Low                      | Normal                  |
| **T-cell Function**     | Impaired Proliferation/Signaling   | Absent                      | Impaired Development/Signaling | Normal                  |
| **IL-2 Production**     | Markedly Reduced                  | Absent                      | Impaired                      | Normal                  |
| **Viral Susceptibility** | Severe                             | Severe                      | Severe                        | Mild (Candida)          |
| **GVHD Post-HSCT**      | Severe                             | N/A (No T cells to cause)    | Severe                        | N/A                     |
| **Key Defect**          | TCR Signaling (ZAP-70)             | TCR Expression/Signaling    | Cytokine Receptor Signaling   | Autoimmunity            |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
ZAP-70 deficiency is a specific form of SCID characterized by normal T-cell numbers but impaired T-cell function due to defective TCR signaling. This leads to severe viral infections and paradoxical severe GVHD post-HSCT. Differentiating it from other SCID forms requires careful analysis of lymphocyte counts and functional assays. Key differentials include defects in TCR/CD3 expression (low T-cell counts) and X-linked SCID (low T, B, and NK cells).
