<a id='question-130'></a>

### Question 130: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 30 (activated)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Differentiate between X-linked agammaglobulinemia (Bruton's) and Common Variable Immunodeficiency (CVID) based on clinical presentation, B cell development, and specific antibody responses. (区分 X 连锁丙种球蛋白缺乏症 (Bruton 病) 和 共同变量免疫缺陷症 (CVID) 的临床表现、B 细胞发育和特异性抗体反应。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with recurrent sinopulmonary infections, including pneumonia and otitis media, starting after 6 months of age. He has not received any live vaccines. Physical examination reveals normal growth and development, but no palpable lymph nodes or tonsils. Laboratory studies show markedly reduced serum IgG, IgA, and IgM levels, with absent B cells (<1% of lymphocytes) in peripheral blood flow cytometry. His mother and maternal grandfather have a history of similar recurrent infections. A bone marrow biopsy shows a normal number of pro-B cells and pre-B cells, but a severe deficiency of immature and mature B cells. The patient is being considered for hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (HSCT). Which of the following molecular defects is most likely responsible for this patient's condition?

(A) Defective CD40 ligand (CD40L) expression on T cells
(B) Defective Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK)
(C) Defective inducible costimulator (ICOS)
(D) Defective T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling
(E) Defective interleukin-2 receptor gamma chain (IL-2Rγc)

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Age of onset:** Recurrent infections starting after 6 months of age suggest a defect in humoral immunity, as maternal IgG protection wanes around this time.
*   **Type of infections:** Recurrent sinopulmonary infections (pneumonia, otitis media) are characteristic of antibody deficiencies, particularly those affecting encapsulated bacteria like *Streptococcus pneumoniae* and *Haemophilus influenzae*.
*   **Lack of lymphoid tissue:** Absence of palpable lymph nodes and tonsils points towards a severe B cell deficiency, as these structures are rich in B cells and germinal centers.
*   **Immunoglobulin levels:** Markedly reduced IgG, IgA, and IgM confirm a panhypogammaglobulinemia.
*   **B cell count:** Absent B cells (<1%) in peripheral blood is a critical finding, indicating a block in B cell development.
*   **Family history:** History of similar infections in the mother and maternal grandfather suggests an X-linked inheritance pattern.
*   **Bone marrow findings:** Normal pro-B and pre-B cells but absent immature and mature B cells indicate a block in B cell maturation *after* the pre-B cell stage, specifically at the transition from bone marrow to peripheral lymphoid organs.
*   **Treatment consideration:** HSCT is the definitive treatment for severe primary immunodeficiencies like this one.

These clues collectively point towards X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA), also known as Bruton's disease.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA):** This is a primary immunodeficiency caused by mutations in the *BTK* gene, located on the X chromosome. BTK is a non-receptor tyrosine kinase crucial for B cell development.
*   **BTK function:** BTK is essential for signaling downstream of the pre-B cell receptor (pre-BCR) and the B cell receptor (BCR). It phosphorylates various downstream targets, including Syk, which is critical for B cell receptor signaling, proliferation, and differentiation.
*   **Pathogenesis:** In XLA, the lack of functional BTK leads to a block in B cell development at the pre-B to immature B cell transition in the bone marrow. This results in a profound deficiency of mature B cells in the circulation and lymphoid tissues, leading to panhypogammaglobulinemia and impaired antibody responses. Pro-B and pre-B cells are typically normal in number because BTK is not essential for their initial development.
*   **Clinical features:** Recurrent bacterial infections (especially encapsulated organisms), starting after maternal antibodies wane (around 6 months), lack of tonsils/lymph nodes, panhypogammaglobulinemia, absent B cells, X-linked inheritance.
*   **Differential diagnosis:** CVID (Common Variable Immunodeficiency) also presents with hypogammaglobulinemia and recurrent infections, but typically has normal or near-normal B cell numbers, although B cell function is impaired. Hyper-IgM syndromes (caused by CD40L deficiency or CD40 deficiency) present with normal or elevated IgM but low IgG, IgA, and IgE, and often have normal B cell numbers. Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID) involves defects in both T and B cell function, presenting earlier and more severely.

##### 3. Molecular Basis of XLA:
*   The *BTK* gene encodes Bruton's tyrosine kinase, a cytoplasmic enzyme essential for B cell development.
*   Mutations in *BTK* prevent the proper signaling required for B cell maturation beyond the pre-B cell stage.
*   This leads to the characteristic phenotype of XLA: absent mature B cells and panhypogammaglobulinemia.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective CD40 ligand (CD40L) expression on T cells:** This causes X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome. Patients have normal B cell numbers but cannot undergo class switching or affinity maturation because T cells cannot provide the necessary CD40L signal to B cells expressing CD40. This results in low IgG, IgA, IgE, but normal or high IgM. The vignette describes absent B cells, not normal B cells with impaired function.
- (C) **Defective inducible costimulator (ICOS):** This causes Hyper-IgM syndrome (autosomal recessive). ICOS is crucial for T cell help to B cells, particularly for class switching and germinal center formation. Patients have normal B cell numbers but impaired antibody responses, similar to CD40L deficiency (low IgG, IgA, IgE, normal/high IgM). Again, the vignette describes absent B cells.
- (D) **Defective T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling:** This would cause severe T cell deficiency, likely leading to a form of Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID). SCID involves defects in both T and B cell function, presenting much earlier and more severely than described in the vignette. B cell numbers might be low due to lack of T cell help, but the primary defect is in T cell development/function. The vignette specifically points to a B cell maturation defect with normal T cell function (implied by the lack of T cell-related symptoms).
- (E) **Defective interleukin-2 receptor gamma chain (IL-2Rγc):** This causes X-linked Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (X-SCID). The IL-2Rγc (encoded by the *IL2RG* gene) is a common signaling subunit for receptors for IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, and IL-21. Its deficiency severely impairs T cell and NK cell development and function, and also affects B cell development (due to lack of T cell help). This presents as a severe immunodeficiency in infancy with opportunistic infections, failure to thrive, and lymphopenia (especially T cells and NK cells). The vignette describes a later onset, primarily bacterial infections, and specifically absent B cells with likely normal T cells.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | X-linked Agammaglobulinemia (XLA) | X-linked Hyper-IgM Syndrome (CD40L Def.) | CVID                     | Hyper-IgM Syndrome (ICOS Def.) | X-linked SCID (IL-2Rγc Def.) |
| :------------------ | :-------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------- | :----------------------- | :----------------------------- | :--------------------------- |
| Inheritance         | X-linked                          | X-linked                                 | Variable (often AD)      | Autosomal Recessive            | X-linked                    |
| Primary Defect      | BTK                               | CD40L                                    | Variable (B cell defect) | ICOS                           | IL-2Rγc                     |
| B Cell Count        | Absent (<1%)                      | Normal/Near Normal                       | Normal/Near Normal       | Normal/Near Normal             | Low (due to lack of T help)  |
| T Cell Count        | Normal                            | Normal                                   | Normal                   | Normal                         | Severely Low                |
| Immunoglobulins     | Panhypogammaglobulinemia (IgG, IgA, IgM low) | Low IgG, IgA, IgE; Normal/High IgM       | Low IgG, IgA, +/- IgM     | Low IgG, IgA, IgE; Normal/High IgM | Low all Igs                 |
| Lymphoid Tissue     | Absent (tonsils, nodes)            | Present                                  | Present                  | Present                        | Absent/Hypoplastic          |
| Typical Infections  | Encapsulated bacteria (after 6 mo) | Opportunistic (Pneumocystis, Cryptosporidium) | Encapsulated bacteria   | Opportunistic                  | Severe, broad (infancy)     |
| Key Molecular Defect | BTK mutation                      | CD40L mutation                            | Variable                | ICOS mutation                  | IL-2Rγc mutation             |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
X-linked agammaglobulinemia (Bruton's disease) is characterized by a block in B cell development at the pre-B cell stage due to BTK deficiency, leading to absent mature B cells and panhypogammaglobulinemia. Differentiate it from other primary immunodeficiencies like CVID (normal B cells, impaired function), Hyper-IgM syndromes (normal B cells, impaired class switching), and SCID (combined T and B cell defects). Key features are male predominance, onset after 6 months, recurrent bacterial infections, absent lymphoid tissue, and absent B cells.

<a id='question-131'></a>

### Question 131: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 31 (titers)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the specific antibody deficiency in Common Variable Immunodeficiency (CVID) and its implications for vaccine response and susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria. (理解共同变量免疫缺陷 (CVID) 中特异性抗体缺乏及其对疫苗反应和对荚膜细菌易感性的影响。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old male presents to his physician complaining of recurrent sinopulmonary infections, including pneumonia and bronchitis, occurring approximately 4-6 times per year since childhood. He reports that he has never responded well to vaccinations, particularly the pneumococcal vaccine, and has had multiple episodes of otitis media. His medical history is notable for chronic diarrhea and weight loss. Physical examination reveals chronic rhinosinusitis and decreased breath sounds bilaterally. Laboratory studies show normal total IgG, IgA, and IgM levels, but markedly reduced specific antibody titers (e.g., anti-tetanus toxoid, anti-diphtheria toxoid, anti-pneumococcal polysaccharide) following vaccination. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes reveals normal numbers of B cells and T cells, with normal expression of CD19, CD20, CD3, CD4, and CD8. He is diagnosed with Common Variable Immunodeficiency (CVID). Which of the following is the MOST likely underlying immunological defect in this patient?

(A) Defective B cell development leading to low numbers of mature B cells.
(B) Impaired T cell-dependent B cell activation and isotype switching.
(C) Defective phagocyte function, specifically impaired opsonization.
(D) Deficient complement component C3, leading to impaired bacterial lysis.
(E) Defective T cell help due to impaired CD40 ligand (CD40L) expression.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent sinopulmonary infections:** Suggests impaired humoral immunity, particularly against encapsulated bacteria like *Streptococcus pneumoniae* and *Haemophilus influenzae*.
- **Poor response to vaccinations:** Indicates a defect in the ability to mount a specific antibody response, even with antigenic challenge.
- **Chronic diarrhea and weight loss:** Common extra-pulmonary manifestations of CVID, potentially due to malabsorption or chronic inflammation.
- **Normal total IgG, IgA, IgM levels:** Rules out X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA) or severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID), where total immunoglobulin levels are typically very low.
- **Markedly reduced specific antibody titers post-vaccination:** This is the hallmark diagnostic feature of CVID. Patients can produce total antibodies, but fail to mount an effective response to specific antigens, especially polysaccharide antigens (like those in pneumococcal vaccine).
- **Normal B cell numbers and CD markers:** Rules out defects in B cell development (like XLA, where CD19+ B cells are absent) or maturation.
- **Diagnosis of CVID:** The clinical picture and lab findings strongly point towards CVID.

The question asks for the *underlying immunological defect* in CVID, given these findings. CVID is characterized by impaired antibody production despite normal or near-normal numbers of B cells. This points to a defect in the *function* of B cells, specifically their ability to differentiate into plasma cells and produce high-affinity antibodies, particularly in response to T-cell help.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **CVID Pathophysiology:** CVID is a heterogeneous group of primary immunodeficiencies characterized by impaired B cell differentiation into plasma cells and memory B cells, leading to hypogammaglobulinemia (though total Ig levels can be normal, *specific* antibody responses are deficient) and increased susceptibility to infections, particularly sinopulmonary infections caused by encapsulated bacteria.
- **Antibody Production:** Antibody production, especially against T-dependent antigens (like proteins and conjugated polysaccharides), requires interaction between B cells and T helper cells (specifically follicular helper T cells, Tfh). B cells present processed antigen on MHC class II molecules to Tfh cells, which recognize the antigen via their T cell receptor (TCR) and provide co-stimulatory signals (e.g., CD40L-CD40 interaction) and cytokines (e.g., IL-4, IL-21) necessary for B cell activation, proliferation, isotype switching (e.g., from IgM to IgG, IgA, IgE), affinity maturation, and differentiation into plasma cells and memory B cells.
- **Defect in CVID:** The precise defect in CVID is often unknown but is thought to involve abnormalities in B cell signaling pathways, T cell help, or the interaction between B and T cells. A common defect involves impaired T-dependent B cell activation and isotype switching, leading to the inability to produce high-affinity, class-switched antibodies in response to vaccination or infection. This explains the normal total Ig levels (which may include IgM) but poor specific antibody responses.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective B cell development leading to low numbers of mature B cells:** This describes X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA), caused by mutations in the *BTK* gene, leading to a block in B cell development at the pre-B cell stage. Patients with XLA have very low levels of all immunoglobulins and absent B cells (CD19+). This patient has normal B cell numbers and normal total Ig levels, making XLA incorrect.
- (C) **Defective phagocyte function, specifically impaired opsonization:** While patients with CVID are susceptible to encapsulated bacteria, the primary defect is not in phagocyte function itself. Opsonization by antibodies (and complement) is crucial for phagocytosis, and the lack of specific antibodies in CVID impairs opsonization, but the *underlying* defect is the antibody deficiency, not the phagocyte. Defects in phagocyte function are seen in conditions like Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD) or Leukocyte Adhesion Deficiency (LAD).
- (D) **Deficient complement component C3, leading to impaired bacterial lysis:** C3 deficiency is a primary immunodeficiency affecting the complement system. It leads to increased susceptibility to pyogenic bacteria, particularly encapsulated organisms, due to impaired opsonization (C3b) and bacterial lysis (MAC). However, CVID is primarily a B cell/antibody defect, not a complement defect. Patients with C3 deficiency typically have normal antibody responses.
- (E) **Defective T cell help due to impaired CD40 ligand (CD40L) expression:** This describes X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome. CD40L on activated T cells binds to CD40 on B cells, providing a crucial co-stimulatory signal for isotype switching and affinity maturation. Deficiency leads to impaired class switching, resulting in high levels of IgM but low levels of IgG, IgA, and IgE. While this involves defective T cell help, the clinical presentation of CVID (normal total Ig levels, though poor specific responses) is distinct from Hyper-IgM syndrome (high IgM, low other Igs). Furthermore, the vignette states normal total Ig levels, which argues against Hyper-IgM.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | CVID (Correct Answer)                                  | XLA (Distractor A)                                     | Hyper-IgM (Distractor E)                               | C3 Deficiency (Distractor D)                             | Phagocyte Defect (Distractor C)                          |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | Impaired T-dependent B cell activation/differentiation | Defective B cell development (BTK mutation)            | Defective T cell help (CD40L mutation)                 | Defective complement (C3 gene mutation)                 | Defective phagocyte function (e.g., NADPH oxidase)       |
| **B Cell Numbers**      | Normal                                                 | Absent/Very Low                                        | Normal                                                 | Normal                                                 | Normal                                                 |
| **Total Ig Levels**     | Normal or Low (often IgG>IgA>IgM)                      | Very Low (all classes)                                  | High IgM, Low IgG, IgA, IgE                             | Normal                                                 | Normal                                                 |
| **Specific Ab Response** | Markedly Deficient                                     | Absent                                                 | Deficient (impaired class switch)                       | Normal                                                 | Normal                                                 |
| **CD40L Expression**    | Normal                                                 | Normal                                                 | Deficient                                              | Normal                                                 | Normal                                                 |
| **Typical Infections**  | Encapsulated bacteria (sinopulmonary)                  | Encapsulated bacteria (sinopulmonary)                  | Opportunistic infections (Pneumocystis, Cryptosporidium) | Encapsulated bacteria (sinopulmonary, meningococcal) | Catalase-positive organisms (Staph, Aspergillus, etc.) |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
CVID is characterized by impaired specific antibody responses despite normal or near-normal B cell numbers and often normal total immunoglobulin levels. This points to a defect in T-dependent B cell activation, isotype switching, or differentiation into plasma cells. Differentiate CVID from XLA (low B cells, low total Ig), Hyper-IgM syndrome (high IgM, low other Ig, defective CD40L), and complement deficiencies (normal antibody response).

<a id='question-132'></a>

### Question 132: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 32 (without)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the molecular defect in Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome (WAS), a primary immunodeficiency characterized by eczema, thrombocytopenia, and recurrent infections, specifically focusing on the role of the WAS protein (WASP) in actin cytoskeleton regulation and its impact on immune cell function, particularly T-cell signaling and function. (本题考察Wiskott-Aldrich综合征（WAS）的分子缺陷，这是一种原发性免疫缺陷病，其特征是湿疹、血小板减少症和复发性感染。重点考察WASP蛋白在肌动 cytoskeleton调控中的作用及其对免疫细胞功能的影响，特别是T细胞信号传导和功能。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with persistent eczema, petechiae, and recurrent severe infections, including pneumonia and otitis media. His mother reports frequent bleeding from minor cuts and nosebleeds. Physical examination reveals widespread eczematous lesions, multiple petechiae on his lower extremities, and mild splenomegaly. Laboratory findings show thrombocytopenia (platelet count 25,000/µL) with small platelets, and low levels of IgM and IgA, with normal IgG and IgE. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes reveals decreased T-cell proliferation in response to mitogenic stimulation (PHA) and impaired cytotoxic T lymphocyte (CTL) function. Genetic testing confirms a mutation in the *WAS* gene located on the X chromosome.

Which of the following molecular defects is most directly responsible for the impaired T-cell function observed in this patient?

(A) Defective signaling downstream of the T-cell receptor (TCR) due to mutations in the *CD3* gene complex.
(B) Impaired actin polymerization and cytoskeletal rearrangement due to mutations in the *WAS* gene, leading to defective immunological synapse formation and T-cell activation.
(C) Defective B-cell maturation and immunoglobulin class switching due to mutations in the *AID* gene.
(D) Impaired phagosome-lysosome fusion and intracellular killing due to mutations in the *MYO5B* gene.
(E) Defective complement component C3 production due to mutations in the *C3* gene.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old boy with eczema, thrombocytopenia (small platelets), recurrent infections (pneumonia, otitis media), petechiae, nosebleeds, mild splenomegaly. This triad (eczema, thrombocytopenia, recurrent infections) is highly suggestive of Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome (WAS).
- **Laboratory Findings:** Thrombocytopenia with small platelets is a characteristic feature of WAS. Low IgM and IgA with normal IgG and IgE is the typical immunoglobulin profile.
- **Immunological Findings:** Decreased T-cell proliferation to mitogens (PHA) and impaired CTL function indicate a T-cell defect.
- **Genetic Testing:** Confirmed mutation in the *WAS* gene.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular defect responsible for the impaired T-cell function.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Wiskott-Aldrich Syndrome (WAS):** An X-linked recessive primary immunodeficiency caused by mutations in the *WAS* gene, which encodes the Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome protein (WASP).
- **WASP Function:** WASP is a crucial cytoplasmic protein involved in actin cytoskeleton regulation. It interacts with various signaling molecules and actin-binding proteins (like Arp2/3 complex, WAVE/Scar complex) to control actin polymerization, branching, and membrane dynamics.
- **Impact on T-cells:** In T-cells, WASP is essential for:
    - **Immunological Synapse Formation:** Actin rearrangement is critical for the formation and stability of the immunological synapse between T-cells and antigen-presenting cells (APCs). WASP deficiency impairs this process.
    - **TCR Signaling:** WASP participates in signaling pathways downstream of the T-cell receptor (TCR), including the activation of Rac1 and Cdc42, which are involved in cytoskeletal reorganization and cell migration.
    - **T-cell Activation and Proliferation:** Defective actin dynamics and signaling impair T-cell activation, proliferation, and effector functions (like CTL activity).
- **Other Manifestations:**
    - **Thrombocytopenia:** WASP is also important for megakaryocyte development and platelet formation/function. Mutations lead to low platelet counts and small platelet size.
    - **Eczema:** The exact mechanism is unclear but likely involves dysregulation of immune cells in the skin.
- **Molecular Defect:** The core defect is the absence or dysfunction of the WASP protein, leading to impaired actin cytoskeleton dynamics. This directly affects T-cell activation, proliferation, and synapse formation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Defective signaling downstream of the TCR due to mutations in the *CD3* gene complex causes X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA), characterized by a lack of B cells and immunoglobulins, not the specific T-cell defects and thrombocytopenia seen in WAS. The CD3 complex is part of the TCR complex itself, essential for signal transduction, but mutations here cause a different immunodeficiency.
- (C): Impaired B-cell maturation and immunoglobulin class switching due to mutations in the *AID* gene causes Hyper-IgM syndrome type 2. Patients have normal or elevated IgM but low IgG, IgA, and IgE. While there are immunoglobulin abnormalities in WAS, the primary defect is not in AID, and the pattern differs (low IgM/IgA, normal IgG/IgE).
- (D): Impaired phagosome-lysosome fusion and intracellular killing due to mutations in the *MYO5B* gene causes Griscelli syndrome type 2. This is characterized by partial albinism, immunodeficiency (often involving cytotoxic T cells and NK cells), and neurological problems. The primary defect is in motor protein function within phagocytes, not actin regulation in T-cells.
- (E): Defective complement component C3 production due to mutations in the *C3* gene causes C3 deficiency, leading to increased susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria infections. This affects the complement system, not the actin cytoskeleton or T-cell signaling directly.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Wiskott-Aldrich Syndrome (WAS) | X-linked Agammaglobulinemia (XLA) | Hyper-IgM Syndrome Type 2 | Griscelli Syndrome Type 2 | C3 Deficiency |
|----------------------|---------------------------------|-----------------------------------|---------------------------|---------------------------|---------------|
| **Gene Defect**      | *WAS* (WASP)                    | *BTK* (Bruton's Tyrosine Kinase) | *AID*                     | *MYO5B*                   | *C3*          |
| **Primary Cell Type Affected** | T-cells, B-cells, Platelets | B-cells                          | B-cells                   | NK cells, T-cells, Phagocytes | N/A (Complement) |
| **Key Clinical Features** | Eczema, Thrombocytopenia (small platelets), Recurrent Infections | Recurrent bacterial infections, Absent B cells | Recurrent infections, Normal/High IgM, Low IgG/IgA/IgE | Partial albinism, Immunodeficiency, Neurological issues | Recurrent bacterial infections |
| **Immunoglobulin Profile** | Low IgM/IgA, Normal IgG/IgE | Absent all Ig classes             | Normal/High IgM, Low IgG/IgA/IgE | Variable                  | Normal        |
| **T-cell Function**  | Impaired proliferation/activation | Normal                            | Normal                    | Impaired CTL/NK           | Normal        |
| **Molecular Defect** | Impaired actin cytoskeleton regulation | Defective B-cell development | Defective class switch recombination | Defective intracellular transport | Defective complement protein |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome is caused by mutations in the *WAS* gene, leading to a defective WASP protein. WASP is crucial for actin cytoskeleton regulation in hematopoietic cells, particularly T-cells. This defect impairs T-cell activation, proliferation, and synapse formation, contributing to the immunodeficiency. Remember the classic triad: eczema, thrombocytopenia (with small platelets), and recurrent infections. Differentiate WAS from XLA (no B cells), Hyper-IgM syndromes (defective class switching), and other primary immunodeficiencies based on clinical features, immunoglobulin profiles, and specific cellular defects.

<a id='question-133'></a>

### Question 133: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 33 (isotypes,)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of primary immunodeficiencies affecting immunoglobulin class switching and the resulting clinical manifestations, specifically recurrent infections with encapsulated bacteria and impaired response to polysaccharide antigens. (考察原发性免疫缺陷，影响免疫球蛋白类别转换，及其导致的临床表现，特别是包被细菌的复发性感染和对多糖抗原的免疫应答障碍。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with recurrent sinopulmonary infections, including pneumonia and otitis media, starting at 6 months of age after maternal antibodies waned. He has been hospitalized twice for severe *Streptococcus pneumoniae* infections. His growth is normal, and he has no history of opportunistic infections or graft-versus-host disease following a blood transfusion. Immunoglobulin levels show markedly decreased IgG, IgA, and IgE, while IgM levels are normal or elevated. Antibody responses to protein antigens (e.g., tetanus toxoid) are normal, but responses to polysaccharide antigens (e.g., pneumococcal polysaccharide vaccine) are severely impaired. Flow cytometry reveals normal numbers of B cells, T cells, and NK cells. Genetic testing confirms a mutation in the *CD40LG* gene.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying immunological defect in this patient?

(A) Defective T-cell help required for immunoglobulin class switching.
(B) Impaired B-cell receptor signaling leading to defective isotype switching.
(C) Defective activation-induced cytidine deaminase (AID) enzyme activity.
(D) Defective Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK) leading to impaired B-cell development.
(E) Defective complement component C3 leading to impaired opsonization.

#### Correct Answer: (A)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent sinopulmonary infections with encapsulated bacteria (*S. pneumoniae*)**: This points towards a defect in humoral immunity, specifically antibody production against polysaccharide capsules.
- **Onset at 6 months**: This timing is typical for antibody deficiencies, as maternal IgG wanes.
- **Normal growth, no opportunistic infections, no GVHD**: This helps rule out severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) and other T-cell defects, which often present earlier with failure to thrive and opportunistic infections. Absence of GVHD rules out defects in T-cell function that prevent rejection of transfused lymphocytes.
- **Decreased IgG, IgA, IgE, normal/high IgM**: This pattern is characteristic of Hyper-IgM syndrome, indicating a block in class switching from IgM to other isotypes.
- **Normal response to protein antigens, impaired response to polysaccharide antigens**: This further supports a defect in T-cell dependent class switching (required for protein antigens) and polysaccharide-specific B-cell responses.
- **Normal B cell numbers**: This rules out defects in B-cell development itself (like X-linked agammaglobulinemia).
- ***CD40LG* gene mutation**: This gene encodes CD40 ligand (CD40L), which is expressed on activated T cells. CD40L interacts with CD40 on B cells, dendritic cells, and other antigen-presenting cells. This interaction is crucial for T-cell dependent B-cell activation, class switching, and somatic hypermutation.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **CD40L-CD40 Interaction**: Activated T helper cells express CD40L. Upon interaction with CD40 on B cells, it provides a critical co-stimulatory signal (Signal 2) alongside BCR signaling (Signal 1) and cytokine signaling (Signal 3). This interaction is essential for:
    - **Class Switch Recombination (CSR)**: Switching from IgM/IgD to IgG, IgA, or IgE. This process involves activation of the AID enzyme, which initiates DNA breaks at specific switch regions upstream of the constant region genes for the desired isotype. The CD40L-CD40 interaction is required for proper CSR.
    - **Somatic Hypermutation (SHM)**: Introducing point mutations into the variable regions of immunoglobulin genes to increase antibody affinity. This also requires the CD40L-CD40 interaction.
    - **Germinal Center Formation**: CD40L-CD40 interaction is crucial for the formation and maintenance of germinal centers, where B cells undergo affinity maturation and class switching.
- **Hyper-IgM Syndrome (HIGM)**: Mutations in *CD40LG* (X-linked HIGM1) or *CD40* (autosomal recessive HIGM3) lead to defective CD40L-CD40 signaling. This results in the inability of B cells to class switch, leading to high levels of IgM (the default isotype) and low levels of IgG, IgA, and IgE. Patients are susceptible to recurrent infections, particularly with encapsulated bacteria (due to lack of IgG and IgA) and opportunistic pathogens like *Pneumocystis jirovecii* (due to lack of IgA and possibly impaired T-cell function in some forms). The impaired response to polysaccharide antigens is also characteristic.
- **T-cell help**: CD40L is expressed on activated T cells (specifically, T follicular helper cells, Tfh cells) within the germinal center. Therefore, the defect in CD40L directly impairs T-cell help required for B-cell class switching and affinity maturation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective T-cell help required for immunoglobulin class switching**: This is the correct answer. The *CD40LG* mutation leads to a lack of functional CD40L on T cells, preventing the necessary co-stimulation of B cells for class switching. This directly impairs T-cell dependent B-cell activation.
- (B) **Impaired B-cell receptor signaling leading to defective isotype switching**: While BCR signaling (Signal 1) is necessary for B-cell activation, the primary defect in this case is the lack of co-stimulation via CD40L-CD40 interaction (Signal 2), which is provided by T cells. Defects in BCR signaling itself are associated with other conditions, such as certain forms of B-cell lymphoma or specific B-cell receptor mutations.
- (C) **Defective activation-induced cytidine deaminase (AID) enzyme activity**: AID is essential for both class switch recombination and somatic hypermutation. While defects in AID cause Hyper-IgM syndrome (HIGM2), the vignette specifies a *CD40LG* mutation. AID deficiency also typically presents with normal or elevated levels of IgE, which is inconsistent with the patient's low IgE.
- (D) **Defective Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK) leading to impaired B-cell development**: BTK is crucial for B-cell development beyond the pre-B cell stage. Mutations in *BTK* cause X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA). Patients with XLA have very low numbers of mature B cells and pan-hypogammaglobulinemia (low levels of all immunoglobulin isotypes, including IgM). This patient has normal B cell numbers and normal/high IgM, ruling out XLA.
- (E) **Defective complement component C3 leading to impaired opsonization**: C3 deficiency is a severe complement deficiency leading to recurrent infections with encapsulated bacteria due to impaired opsonization and lysis. However, it does not explain the specific pattern of immunoglobulin isotypes (high IgM, low IgG/IgA/IgE) or the impaired response to polysaccharide antigens seen in this patient. Complement deficiencies typically present with normal immunoglobulin levels.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                     | CD40LG Mutation (HIGM1) | AID Deficiency (HIGM2) | BTK Deficiency (XLA) | C3 Deficiency |
|-----------------------------|--------------------------|-------------------------|-----------------------|---------------|
| Gene Defect                 | *CD40LG*                 | *AID*                   | *BTK*                 | *C3*          |
| Inheritance                 | X-linked                 | Autosomal Recessive     | X-linked              | Autosomal     |
| B Cell Numbers              | Normal                   | Normal                  | Very Low              | Normal        |
| IgM Levels                  | Normal/High              | Normal/High             | Very Low              | Normal        |
| IgG, IgA, IgE Levels        | Low                      | Low                     | Very Low              | Normal        |
| Response to Protein Ag      | Normal                   | Normal                  | Absent                | Normal        |
| Response to Polysaccharide Ag | Impaired                 | Impaired                | Absent                | Normal        |
| Opportunistic Infections    | Yes (e.g., *Pneumocystis*) | Yes (e.g., *Pneumocystis*) | Yes                   | Less Common  |
| CD40L Expression on T cells | Absent/Defective         | Normal                  | Normal                | Normal        |
| CD40 Expression on B cells | Normal                   | Normal                  | Normal                | Normal        |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The clinical presentation of recurrent infections with encapsulated bacteria, combined with the specific immunoglobulin profile (high IgM, low IgG/IgA/IgE) and impaired polysaccharide responses, strongly suggests a defect in immunoglobulin class switching. A *CD40LG* mutation causes X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome by preventing T-cell help to B cells via the CD40L-CD40 interaction, which is essential for class switching. Differentiating this from other primary immunodeficiencies like XLA (low B cells, low all Ig isotypes), AID deficiency (normal IgE), or complement deficiencies (normal Ig levels) requires careful analysis of the clinical and laboratory findings.

<a id='question-134'></a>

### Question 134: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 34 (normal)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Differentiating primary immunodeficiencies based on specific clinical manifestations, particularly recurrent infections with encapsulated bacteria and impaired antibody responses, leading to the identification of the underlying defect in B-cell development or function. (根据特定临床表现，特别是包被细菌的复发性感染和受损的抗体反应，区分原发性免疫缺陷，从而确定B细胞发育或功能的潜在缺陷。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with recurrent episodes of otitis media, sinusitis, and pneumonia, typically caused by *Streptococcus pneumoniae* and *Haemophilus influenzae*. He has never received live vaccines (e.g., MMR, varicella) due to concerns about potential complications. Physical examination reveals normal growth and development, but no palpable lymph nodes or tonsils. Laboratory tests show markedly reduced serum IgG, IgA, and IgE levels, with normal or slightly elevated IgM levels. Peripheral blood flow cytometry reveals a significant reduction in the number of circulating B lymphocytes (CD19+ cells). T-cell numbers (CD3+ cells) and function appear normal. The patient's mother has a similar history of recurrent infections.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying defect in this patient?

(A) Defective T-cell receptor signaling leading to impaired T-cell help for B cells.
(B) Mutation in the *BTK* gene affecting B-cell receptor signaling.
(C) Deficient expression of CD40 ligand (CD40L) on activated T cells.
(D) Impaired class switch recombination due to a defect in activation-induced cytidine deaminase (AID).
(E) Defective immunoglobulin heavy chain transcription leading to a lack of all immunoglobulin isotypes.

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent infections with encapsulated bacteria (*S. pneumoniae*, *H. influenzae*)**: This points towards a defect in humoral immunity, specifically the inability to produce effective antibodies against polysaccharide antigens. Encapsulated bacteria are opsonized and cleared primarily by antibody-mediated mechanisms and complement.
- **Age of onset (3 years old)**: Suggests a primary immunodeficiency, as maternal antibodies would have waned by this age.
- **Lack of live vaccines**: Indicates a known or suspected immunodeficiency, often involving cell-mediated immunity or B-cell function, where live vaccines pose a risk.
- **Normal growth and development**: Rules out severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) or other profound immunodeficiencies presenting with failure to thrive.
- **No palpable lymph nodes or tonsils**: Suggests a defect in B-cell development or maturation, as these lymphoid tissues are rich in B cells.
- **Markedly reduced serum IgG, IgA, and IgE levels, with normal/elevated IgM**: This pattern is characteristic of defects in class switch recombination (CSR), where B cells can produce IgM but fail to switch to other isotypes.
- **Reduced circulating B lymphocytes (CD19+ cells)**: Indicates a problem with B-cell development or survival.
- **Normal T-cell numbers and function**: Rules out T-cell deficiencies like DiGeorge syndrome or SCID.
- **Family history (mother with similar history)**: Suggests an inherited disorder, likely autosomal recessive or X-linked.

These clues collectively point towards a primary immunodeficiency affecting B-cell maturation and antibody isotype switching, specifically Hyper-IgM syndrome.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Humoral Immunity & Class Switch Recombination (CSR)**: B cells initially produce IgM. Upon activation by T-cell help (CD40-CD40L interaction) and specific cytokines, they undergo CSR, changing their constant region genes to produce IgG, IgA, or IgE. This process is crucial for effective responses to protein antigens and opsonization/neutralization of encapsulated bacteria.
- **Activation-Induced Cytidine Deaminase (AID)**: AID is an enzyme essential for CSR and somatic hypermutation (SHM). It initiates the process by deaminating cytosine to uracil in DNA, triggering DNA repair pathways that lead to the exchange of VDJ segments with downstream constant region segments.
- **Hyper-IgM Syndromes**: These are a group of primary immunodeficiencies characterized by normal or elevated IgM levels but low levels of IgG, IgA, and IgE. They result from defects in the CSR pathway.
    - **X-linked Hyper-IgM Syndrome (HIGM1)**: Caused by mutations in the *CD40LG* gene, encoding CD40L on T cells. This prevents T-cell help for B-cell CSR. (Option C)
    - **Autosomal Recessive Hyper-IgM Syndrome (HIGM2)**: Caused by mutations in the *AID* gene. This directly impairs the CSR machinery. (Option D)
    - **Other forms**: Defects in CD40, UNG (uracil-DNA glycosylase), or cytidine deaminase.
- **Clinical Manifestations of Hyper-IgM**: Recurrent bacterial infections (especially encapsulated organisms), opportunistic infections (like *Pneumocystis jirovecii* in CD40L deficiency), increased risk of autoimmune diseases, and certain malignancies.
- **B-cell Development**: B cells develop in the bone marrow. Pre-B cells express IgM. Mature B cells express both IgM and IgD. CSR occurs after B-cell activation in germinal centers. The patient's reduced B-cell numbers (CD19+) could be secondary to the defect in CSR or maturation, or potentially related to a specific subtype of HIGM.

In this case, the combination of low IgG, IgA, IgE, normal/high IgM, recurrent encapsulated bacterial infections, and reduced B cells strongly suggests a defect in CSR, most commonly caused by mutations in *AID* (HIGM2) or *CD40LG* (HIGM1). The question asks for the *most likely* underlying defect. While both are possible, defects in AID (Option D) are a common cause of autosomal recessive HIGM, fitting the clinical picture.

##### 3. Evaluating the Options
- **(A) Defective T-cell receptor signaling**: This would primarily affect T-cell function, leading to impaired T-cell help for B cells (similar to HIGM1) or defects in cell-mediated immunity. While impaired T-cell help can cause low IgG, IgA, IgE, it doesn't typically cause the profound reduction in B cells seen here, and T-cell numbers/function are stated as normal.
- **(B) Mutation in the *BTK* gene**: This causes X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA). Patients have very low levels of *all* immunoglobulin isotypes (including IgM) and absent B cells. This patient has normal/high IgM and reduced, but not absent, B cells.
- **(C) Deficient expression of CD40 ligand (CD40L)**: This causes X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome (HIGM1). Patients have low IgG, IgA, IgE, and normal/high IgM, similar to this case. However, HIGM1 is often associated with susceptibility to *Pneumocystis jirovecii* pneumonia (PJP) due to impaired T-cell interaction with macrophages, which is not mentioned here. Also, B-cell numbers are typically normal or slightly reduced, not markedly reduced as stated. While possible, the markedly reduced B cells make Option D slightly more fitting, although both C and D cause Hyper-IgM. The question asks for the *most likely* defect given *all* the information.
- **(D) Impaired class switch recombination due to a defect in activation-induced cytidine deaminase (AID)**: This causes autosomal recessive Hyper-IgM syndrome (HIGM2). It perfectly explains the low IgG, IgA, IgE, normal/high IgM, recurrent encapsulated bacterial infections, and the defect in CSR. Reduced B cell numbers can sometimes be seen in HIGM2. This fits the clinical picture well.
- **(E) Defective immunoglobulin heavy chain transcription**: This would lead to a complete absence of all immunoglobulin heavy chains and thus all immunoglobulin isotypes (pan-hypogammaglobulinemia) and absent B cells. This patient has normal/high IgM, ruling this out.

Comparing (C) and (D): Both cause Hyper-IgM. However, the markedly reduced B-cell count is more consistently associated with AID deficiency (HIGM2) than CD40L deficiency (HIGM1), although B cell numbers can vary. Given the specific mention of *markedly* reduced B cells, AID deficiency is a slightly better fit.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Incorrect. Defective T-cell receptor signaling primarily affects T cells. While T-cell help is needed for CSR, the vignette states T-cell numbers and function are normal, and the primary defect appears to be in B-cell maturation/function (reduced B cells, abnormal isotype profile). This option describes defects like T-cell immunodeficiency or certain forms of combined immunodeficiency, which don't fit the normal T-cell findings.
- (B): Incorrect. *BTK* mutations cause XLA, characterized by a complete absence of mature B cells and pan-hypogammaglobulinemia (very low levels of *all* Ig isotypes, including IgM). This patient has normal/high IgM and reduced, but not absent, B cells.
- (C): Incorrect. CD40L deficiency causes X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome. While it explains the low IgG, IgA, IgE, and normal/high IgM, it typically presents with normal B-cell numbers. The vignette specifically mentions *markedly reduced* B cells, making this less likely than a defect directly affecting B-cell CSR machinery like AID. Also, HIGM1 often involves susceptibility to PJP, not mentioned here.
- (D): Correct. AID deficiency causes autosomal recessive Hyper-IgM syndrome. It directly impairs CSR, leading to the characteristic immunoglobulin profile (low IgG, IgA, IgE; normal/high IgM). It can also be associated with reduced B-cell numbers. This aligns perfectly with the clinical presentation.
- (E): Incorrect. A defect in immunoglobulin heavy chain transcription would prevent the production of *all* immunoglobulin heavy chains, resulting in a complete absence of all immunoglobulin isotypes (pan-hypogammaglobulinemia) and absent B cells. This patient has normal/high IgM, ruling out this severe defect.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | XLA (*BTK* mutation) | HIGM1 (*CD40L* mutation) | HIGM2 (*AID* mutation) | This Patient's Case |
|-------------------------|----------------------|--------------------------|------------------------|----------------------|
| **Ig Levels**           | All low (pan-hypo)   | Low IgG, IgA, IgE; Normal/High IgM | Low IgG, IgA, IgE; Normal/High IgM | Low IgG, IgA, IgE; Normal/High IgM |
| **B-cell Count**        | Absent               | Normal/Slightly Reduced  | Reduced                | Markedly Reduced     |
| **T-cell Count/Function**| Normal               | Normal                   | Normal                 | Normal               |
| **Inheritance**         | X-linked             | X-linked                 | Autosomal Recessive    | Likely Autosomal Recessive (family history) |
| **Key Defect**          | B-cell development   | T-cell help for CSR      | CSR machinery          | CSR machinery          |
| **PJP Risk**            | No                   | Yes                      | No                     | No (not mentioned)   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The classic presentation of recurrent encapsulated bacterial infections with low IgG, IgA, IgE, and normal/high IgM points towards Hyper-IgM syndrome. Differentiating between X-linked (CD40L deficiency) and autosomal recessive (AID deficiency) forms requires careful attention to B-cell numbers and associated clinical features (like PJP risk in CD40L deficiency). Markedly reduced B cells favor AID deficiency. Remember to differentiate Hyper-IgM from XLA (absent B cells, all Ig low) and Common Variable Immunodeficiency (CVID) (low IgG, IgA, IgE; normal IgM; normal B cells).

<a id='question-135'></a>

### Question 135: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 35 (numbers,)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of primary immunodeficiencies, specifically focusing on the characteristic features and underlying defects associated with Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome (WAS), including thrombocytopenia, eczema, recurrent infections, and the specific immune cell abnormalities (T-cell dysfunction, impaired antibody response, defective cytoskeletal organization). (本题考察原发性免疫缺陷病，特别是Wiskott-Aldrich综合征（WAS）的特征和潜在缺陷，包括血小板减少症、湿疹、复发性感染以及特异性免疫细胞异常（T细胞功能障碍、抗体反应受损、细胞骨架组织缺陷）。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with a history of recurrent otitis media, pneumonia, and severe eczema since infancy. He also has petechiae and easy bruising. His mother reports that he has had difficulty gaining weight and frequently develops diarrhea. Physical examination reveals pallor, multiple excoriations consistent with eczema, and small, purpuric spots on his lower extremities. Laboratory studies show thrombocytopenia (platelet count 25,000/µL) with small platelet size, elevated IgE levels, and decreased IgM levels. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes reveals reduced expression of CD43 (sialophorin) on T cells and platelets. Genetic testing confirms a mutation in the *WAS* gene.

Which of the following cellular defects is most directly responsible for the impaired immune function and characteristic features observed in this patient?

(A) Defective T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling due to mutations in the *ZAP70* gene.
(B) Impaired B-cell development and maturation due to mutations in the *BTK* gene.
(C) Defective actin polymerization and cytoskeletal rearrangement due to mutations in the *WAS* gene.
(D) Impaired phagocyte chemotaxis and oxidative burst due to mutations in the *CYBB* gene.
(E) Defective class switching recombination due to mutations in the *AID* gene.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 3-year-old boy. This age is typical for the onset of symptoms in Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome (WAS).
*   **Clinical Presentation:** Recurrent infections (otitis media, pneumonia), severe eczema, thrombocytopenia (petechiae, bruising, small platelets), failure to thrive, diarrhea. This triad (eczema, thrombocytopenia, recurrent infections) is highly suggestive of WAS.
*   **Laboratory Findings:** Thrombocytopenia with *small* platelets is a hallmark of WAS. Elevated IgE and decreased IgM are also characteristic immunological findings.
*   **Flow Cytometry:** Reduced CD43 expression on T cells and platelets is a specific finding in WAS. CD43 (sialophorin) is a heavily glycosylated transmembrane protein involved in cell adhesion and signaling, and its expression is dependent on the WAS protein.
*   **Genetic Testing:** Confirmed mutation in the *WAS* gene confirms the diagnosis.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the *cellular defect* responsible for the phenotype.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Wiskott-Aldrich Syndrome (WAS):** An X-linked recessive primary immunodeficiency caused by mutations in the *WAS* gene, located on the X chromosome (Xp11.23).
*   **WAS Protein (WASP):** The WAS protein is a cytoplasmic protein that acts as a crucial regulator of the actin cytoskeleton. It interacts with various signaling molecules, including Vav family proteins, N-WASP, and WASN1, to control actin polymerization, cell motility, adhesion, and signaling pathways downstream of various receptors (e.g., TCR, BCR, Fc receptors, chemokine receptors).
*   **Cellular Defects in WAS:**
    *   **T Cells:** Impaired T-cell activation, proliferation, and migration due to defective actin rearrangement required for immunological synapse formation and cytokine secretion. This leads to susceptibility to infections, particularly opportunistic infections.
    *   **B Cells:** Impaired B-cell function, including reduced antibody responses (especially to polysaccharide antigens), leading to recurrent bacterial infections. The decreased IgM and elevated IgE/IgA are characteristic.
    *   **Platelets:** Defective platelet formation and function, resulting in thrombocytopenia and small platelet size. This is due to the role of WASp in megakaryocyte development and platelet formation/function, involving actin-dependent processes.
    *   **Other Cells:** Defects in dendritic cells and NK cells also contribute to the immunodeficiency.
*   **Mechanism:** The core defect is the inability of the WAS protein to properly regulate actin dynamics. This disrupts various cellular processes essential for immune cell function and platelet development.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Defective T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling due to mutations in the *ZAP70* gene:** Mutations in *ZAP70* cause ZAP70 deficiency, another primary immunodeficiency. This leads to impaired T-cell and NK-cell activation due to defective signaling downstream of the TCR and Fc receptors. While it causes T-cell dysfunction and recurrent infections, it is *not* associated with thrombocytopenia, eczema, or small platelets. The characteristic triad and small platelets point away from ZAP70 deficiency.
*   **(B) Impaired B-cell development and maturation due to mutations in the *BTK* gene:** Mutations in *BTK* cause X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA). This results in a profound deficiency of mature B cells and immunoglobulins (all isotypes), leading to severe recurrent bacterial infections starting in infancy. Patients with XLA do *not* typically have eczema or thrombocytopenia. The presence of eczema, thrombocytopenia, and the specific Ig profile (low IgM, high IgE) rules out XLA.
*   **(D) Impaired phagocyte chemotaxis and oxidative burst due to mutations in the *CYBB* gene:** Mutations in *CYBB* (encoding gp91phox, a component of NADPH oxidase) cause Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD). This leads to defective phagocyte killing of catalase-positive organisms, resulting in recurrent bacterial and fungal infections, granuloma formation, and inflammatory complications. CGD does *not* cause eczema, thrombocytopenia, or the specific T-cell/B-cell defects seen in the vignette.
*   **(E) Defective class switching recombination due to mutations in the *AID* gene:** Mutations in *AID* cause Hyper-IgM syndrome type 2. This results in the inability of B cells to switch immunoglobulin classes, leading to normal or elevated IgM levels but deficient IgG, IgA, and IgE. Patients have recurrent bacterial infections. This condition does *not* cause eczema or thrombocytopenia, and the Ig profile (low IgM, high IgE) is opposite to that seen in Hyper-IgM syndrome.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Wiskott-Aldrich Syndrome (WAS) | X-linked Agammaglobulinemia (XLA) | ZAP70 Deficiency | Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD) | Hyper-IgM Syndrome Type 2 |
| :------------------ | :----------------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :--------------- | :---------------------------------- | :------------------------ |
| Gene                | *WAS*                          | *BTK*                             | *ZAP70*          | *CYBB*                              | *AID*                     |
| Inheritance         | X-linked Recessive             | X-linked Recessive                | Autosomal Recessive | X-linked or Autosomal Recessive     | Autosomal Recessive       |
| Key Features        | Eczema, Thrombocytopenia (small platelets), Recurrent Infections | Absent B cells, Low Ig (all isotypes) | Recurrent Infections, Impaired T/NK activation | Recurrent bacterial/fungal infections, Granulomas | Recurrent Infections, Normal/High IgM, Low IgG/IgA/IgE |
| Platelets           | Low, Small                     | Normal                             | Normal           | Normal                              | Normal                    |
| Eczema              | Yes                            | No                                | No               | No                                  | No                        |
| Ig Levels           | Low IgM, High IgE/IgA          | Very Low All Ig                   | Normal           | Normal                              | High IgM, Low Others      |
| Primary Cell Defect | T cells, B cells, Platelets    | B cells                           | T cells, NK cells | Phagocytes                          | B cells                   |
| Underlying Defect   | Actin Cytoskeleton Regulation  | B-cell Development                | TCR Signaling    | Phagocyte Killing                   | Class Switch Recombination |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome is characterized by the triad of eczema, thrombocytopenia (with small platelets), and recurrent infections, along with specific immunological abnormalities (low IgM, high IgE). The underlying defect is a mutation in the *WAS* gene, leading to impaired actin cytoskeleton regulation in hematopoietic cells. Differentiating WAS from other primary immunodeficiencies like XLA (no eczema/thrombocytopenia, absent B cells), CGD (no eczema/thrombocytopenia, phagocyte defect), and Hyper-IgM syndromes (different Ig profile, no eczema/thrombocytopenia) relies on recognizing this unique clinical and laboratory presentation.

<a id='question-136'></a>

### Question 136: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 36 (susceptibility)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of primary immunodeficiencies, specifically focusing on the susceptibility to opportunistic infections and the underlying immune defect in Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD). It requires differentiating CGD from other phagocytic defects and understanding the molecular basis of its pathophysiology. (本题考察原发性免疫缺陷，特别是慢性肉芽肿病 (CGD) 对机会性感染的易感性，以及其潜在的免疫缺陷。需要区分 CGD 与其他吞噬细胞缺陷，并理解其病理生理学的基础。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 5-year-old boy with a history of recurrent, severe bacterial and fungal infections (including *Staphylococcus aureus*, *Aspergillus*, and *Serratia marcescens*) since infancy is admitted to the hospital with pneumonia. His infections often involve abscess formation in the lungs, skin, liver, and lymph nodes. He has failed to thrive and has a history of inflammatory bowel disease-like symptoms. Flow cytometry analysis of his neutrophils reveals normal expression of CD14, CD16, and CD47, but markedly reduced production of reactive oxygen species (ROS) upon stimulation with phorbol myristate acetate (PMA). Genetic testing confirms a mutation in the *CYBB* gene. Which of the following best describes the underlying defect in this patient's immune system?

(A) Defective synthesis of the Fc receptor gamma chain, leading to impaired phagocytosis of opsonized bacteria.
(B) Impaired expression of CD40 ligand on T cells, resulting in defective B cell class switching and antibody production.
(C) Defective NADPH oxidase complex assembly, leading to reduced production of superoxide radicals required for intracellular killing of phagocytosed microbes.
(D) Defective expression of the common gamma chain (γc) shared by multiple cytokine receptors, resulting in impaired T cell and NK cell development and function.
(E) Defective dectin-1 receptor on phagocytes, leading to impaired recognition and phagocytosis of fungal pathogens.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent severe bacterial and fungal infections:** Suggests a defect in phagocytic function or cell-mediated immunity. The specific pathogens (*S. aureus*, *Aspergillus*, *Serratia*) are known to cause severe infections in CGD.
- **Abscess formation:** Characteristic feature of CGD due to the inability of phagocytes to kill ingested microbes, leading to granuloma formation and abscesses.
- **Inflammatory bowel disease-like symptoms:** Common extra-infectious manifestation of CGD, often involving granulomatous inflammation in the GI tract.
- **Flow cytometry: Normal CD14, CD16, CD47:** Rules out defects in general phagocyte development or surface marker expression related to Fc receptors (CD16) or complement receptors (CD35/CR1, CD21/CR2, CD11b/CR3, CD11c/CR4 - CD47 is not typically assessed in this context but its normality is consistent).
- **Flow cytometry: Markedly reduced ROS production upon PMA stimulation:** This is the key finding. PMA activates protein kinase C (PKC), which normally leads to the activation of the NADPH oxidase complex and subsequent ROS production (respiratory burst). Reduced ROS indicates a defect in this pathway.
- **Genetic testing: Mutation in *CYBB* gene:** The *CYBB* gene encodes the gp91phox subunit of the NADPH oxidase complex. Mutations in this gene are the most common cause of X-linked CGD.
- **Conclusion:** The clinical presentation (recurrent bacterial/fungal infections, abscesses), specific pathogens, flow cytometry results (normal surface markers, defective ROS production), and genetic finding (*CYBB* mutation) all point strongly to Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD).

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD):** A primary immunodeficiency characterized by defects in the NADPH oxidase complex.
- **NADPH Oxidase Complex:** A multi-subunit enzyme complex located in the phagosomal membrane of phagocytes (neutrophils, macrophages). Its function is to generate superoxide radicals (O2-) by transferring electrons from NADPH to molecular oxygen.
- **Respiratory Burst:** The rapid release of ROS (superoxide, hydrogen peroxide, hydroxyl radicals) by phagocytes upon activation. This process is crucial for killing phagocytosed microbes.
- **Mechanism in CGD:** Mutations in genes encoding subunits of the NADPH oxidase complex (e.g., *CYBB* for gp91phox, *CYBA* for p22phox, *NCF1* for p47phox, *NCF2* for p67phox, *NCF4* for p40phox) lead to impaired assembly or function of the complex. This results in a failure of the respiratory burst and inability to produce ROS.
- **Consequences:** Phagocytes can engulf microbes but cannot effectively kill certain catalase-positive organisms (like *S. aureus*, *Aspergillus*, *Serratia*, *Burkholderia cepacia*, *Nocardia*). This leads to persistent infection, granuloma formation, and abscesses.
- **Diagnosis:** Dihydrorhodamine (DHR) test or nitroblue tetrazolium (NBT) test measures ROS production. Flow cytometry using DHR123 is commonly used. Genetic testing identifies the specific gene mutation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective synthesis of the Fc receptor gamma chain, leading to impaired phagocytosis of opsonized bacteria.** This describes X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA) or Bruton's agammaglobulinemia. XLA is characterized by a lack of B cells and antibodies due to a defect in the BTK gene, not a phagocytic defect. The Fc receptor gamma chain (FcγRIIb) is involved in antibody-mediated phagocytosis, but its defect causes susceptibility primarily to encapsulated bacteria, not the broad range of catalase-positive organisms seen in CGD. The flow cytometry results (normal CD16) also contradict this.
- (B) **Impaired expression of CD40 ligand on T cells, resulting in defective B cell class switching and antibody production.** This describes X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome. Patients have normal or elevated IgM levels but deficient IgG, IgA, and IgE due to defective T cell help for B cell class switching. They are susceptible to opportunistic infections, particularly *Pneumocystis jirovecii*. This does not fit the clinical picture of recurrent bacterial/fungal abscesses or the specific flow cytometry findings (defective ROS production).
- (D) **Defective expression of the common gamma chain (γc) shared by multiple cytokine receptors, resulting in impaired T cell and NK cell development and function.** This describes X-linked Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (X-SCID). Patients have profound defects in both T and B cell immunity due to impaired lymphocyte development. They present early in life with severe, life-threatening infections (viral, bacterial, fungal, opportunistic). This is much more severe than the presentation described and involves T cell deficiency, which is not the primary defect in CGD. The flow cytometry findings are also inconsistent.
- (E) **Defective dectin-1 receptor on phagocytes, leading to impaired recognition and phagocytosis of fungal pathogens.** Dectin-1 is a C-type lectin receptor involved in recognizing β-glucans on fungal cell walls. Defects in dectin-1 can lead to increased susceptibility to fungal infections, but it does not explain the susceptibility to catalase-positive bacteria or the specific defect in ROS production seen in this patient. The clinical picture is also less typical for a dectin-1 defect compared to CGD.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD) | X-linked Agammaglobulinemia (XLA) | X-linked Hyper-IgM Syndrome | X-linked SCID | Dectin-1 Deficiency |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :------------------------------- | :-------------------------- | :------------ | :------------------ |
| **Primary Defect**      | NADPH oxidase complex                | BTK gene (B cell development)    | CD40L gene (T cell help)     | γc gene (Lymphocyte development) | Dectin-1 receptor   |
| **Cell Type Affected**  | Phagocytes (Neutrophils, Macrophages) | B cells                         | T cells                     | T & B cells   | Phagocytes          |
| **Key Function Defect** | ROS production (Respiratory Burst)   | Antibody production             | Class switching             | Lymphocyte development | Fungal recognition  |
| **Typical Infections**  | Catalase+ bacteria, *Aspergillus*   | Encapsulated bacteria           | *Pneumocystis jirovecii*    | Broad (Viral, Bacterial, Fungal, Opportunistic) | Fungi               |
| **Clinical Features**   | Abscesses, granulomas, IBD-like      | Recurrent sinopulmonary infections | Recurrent infections        | Severe infections early in life | Increased fungal infections |
| **Flow Cytometry**      | ↓ ROS production (DHR/NBT test)      | ↓ B cells (CD19+)                | Normal                     | ↓ T cells (CD3+), ↓ NK cells (CD16/56+) | Normal              |
| **Genetic Basis**       | *CYBB*, *CYBA*, *NCF1*, *NCF2*, *NCF4* | *BTK*                           | *CD40LG*                    | *IL2RG*       | *CLEC7A*            |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
CGD is a phagocytic defect characterized by impaired intracellular killing due to defective ROS production. Key features include recurrent infections with catalase-positive organisms and abscess formation. Differentiate CGD from other primary immunodeficiencies affecting phagocytes (like Leukocyte Adhesion Deficiency) or lymphocytes (like SCID, XLA, Hyper-IgM). Remember the specific pathogens and clinical manifestations associated with each defect.

<a id='question-137'></a>

### Question 137: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 37 (encapsulated)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the underlying immunological defect in Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome (WAS), specifically the role of the WAS protein (WASP) in actin cytoskeleton organization and its impact on immune cell function, particularly T-cell signaling and platelet formation. (本题考察Wiskott-Aldrich综合征（WAS）的潜在免疫缺陷，特别是WASP蛋白在actin细胞骨架组织中的作用，以及它对免疫细胞功能，特别是T细胞信号传导和血小板形成的影响。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with recurrent infections (otitis media, pneumonia), eczema, and thrombocytopenia (small platelets). His mother reports a history of bleeding gums and easy bruising since infancy. Family history reveals that his maternal uncle died at age 10 from lymphoma. Immunological workup shows low IgM levels, normal IgG levels, and elevated IgA and IgE levels. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes reveals decreased expression of CD43 (sialophorin) on T cells and platelets. Genetic testing confirms a mutation in the *WAS* gene located on the X chromosome.

Which of the following cellular processes is most directly impaired in this patient due to the underlying genetic defect?

(A) Complement activation via the classical pathway
(B) T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling and actin cytoskeleton reorganization
(C) B-cell isotype switching from IgM to IgG
(D) Neutrophil chemotaxis mediated by formyl peptide receptors
(E) Macrophage phagocytosis of opsonized bacteria

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent infections (otitis media, pneumonia), eczema, thrombocytopenia (small platelets):** This classic triad is highly suggestive of Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome (WAS).
- **Bleeding gums, easy bruising:** Consistent with thrombocytopenia and small platelet size, characteristic of WAS.
- **Maternal uncle died at age 10 from lymphoma:** WAS patients have an increased risk of autoimmunity and malignancy, particularly lymphoma.
- **Low IgM, normal IgG, elevated IgA and IgE:** This specific immunoglobulin profile is characteristic of WAS.
- **Decreased CD43 expression on T cells and platelets:** CD43 (sialophorin) is a heavily glycosylated transmembrane protein involved in cell adhesion and signaling. Its expression is often reduced or absent in WAS due to the role of WASp in its trafficking and function.
- ***WAS* gene mutation on X chromosome:** Confirms the diagnosis of WAS.
- **Question asks for the most directly impaired cellular process:** This requires understanding the function of the WAS protein (WASP).

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Wiskott-Aldrich Syndrome (WAS):** An X-linked recessive primary immunodeficiency caused by mutations in the *WAS* gene.
- **WASP Protein:** A cytoplasmic protein crucial for actin cytoskeleton organization and cell signaling. It interacts with various proteins, including N-WASP, WASN1, and WAVE/Scar complex, to regulate actin polymerization and cell motility.
- **Role in T cells:** WASp is essential for T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling. Upon TCR engagement, WASp is activated and promotes actin polymerization, leading to the formation of immunological synapses and efficient T-cell activation, proliferation, and cytokine production. Defective WASp impairs these processes, leading to defective T-cell function.
- **Role in Platelets:** WASp is also critical for platelet formation and function. It regulates platelet size and actin cytoskeleton dynamics, which are necessary for platelet adhesion, aggregation, and thrombus formation. Mutations in *WAS* lead to small, dysfunctional platelets and thrombocytopenia.
- **Role in other cells:** WASp is expressed in various hematopoietic cells, including B cells, NK cells, dendritic cells, and macrophages, contributing to their function.
- **Clinical Manifestations:** The combination of impaired T-cell function (leading to recurrent infections), defective platelet function (leading to thrombocytopenia and bleeding), and eczema results from the widespread role of WASp in actin cytoskeleton regulation and cell signaling. The immunoglobulin profile (low IgM, high IgA/IgE) reflects defects in B-cell function and antibody production, often secondary to impaired T-cell help.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Complement activation via the classical pathway:** WAS primarily affects cellular immunity (T cells, platelets) and actin cytoskeleton organization, not the complement system. While infections in WAS patients might trigger complement activation, the primary defect is not in the complement pathway itself.
- (C) **B-cell isotype switching from IgM to IgG:** While B-cell function is affected in WAS (leading to the characteristic Ig profile), the primary defect is not specifically in the isotype switching mechanism itself, but rather in the overall immune dysregulation, including impaired T-cell help required for efficient isotype switching. The defect in WAS is more fundamental, affecting actin dynamics required for various cellular processes, including B-cell function.
- (D) **Neutrophil chemotaxis mediated by formyl peptide receptors:** Neutrophil chemotaxis is generally intact in WAS. The primary defect lies in T-cell and platelet function due to impaired actin dynamics. While severe infections can sometimes affect neutrophil function secondarily, it's not the primary defect caused by the *WAS* gene mutation.
- (E) **Macrophage phagocytosis of opsonized bacteria:** Macrophage function is generally considered relatively preserved in WAS compared to T-cell and platelet defects. While some studies suggest subtle macrophage defects, the hallmark features of WAS are related to T-cell and platelet dysfunction mediated by WASp's role in actin cytoskeleton regulation.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Wiskott-Aldrich Syndrome (WAS)                                  | X-linked Agammaglobulinemia (XLA)                               | Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD)                             | Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID)                          |
|----------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Genetic Defect**   | *WAS* gene (X-linked)                                           | *BTK* gene (X-linked)                                           | Various genes (e.g., *CYBB*, *NCF1*, *NCF2*, *NCF4*) (X-linked or AR) | Various genes (e.g., *IL2RG*, *RAG1/2*, *ADA*) (X-linked or AR) |
| **Primary Cell Type Affected** | T cells, Platelets, B cells (actin cytoskeleton)                 | B cells (development)                                           | Phagocytes (neutrophils, macrophages) (oxidative burst)         | T cells, B cells, NK cells (lymphocyte development/function)   |
| **Key Clinical Features** | Eczema, Thrombocytopenia (small platelets), Recurrent infections, Autoimmunity, Malignancy | Recurrent bacterial infections (especially encapsulated), Absent B cells | Recurrent bacterial and fungal infections (catalase-positive organisms), Granulomas | Severe recurrent infections (bacterial, viral, fungal, opportunistic), Failure to thrive |
| **Immunoglobulin Profile** | Low IgM, Normal/High IgG, High IgA/IgE                          | Very low/absent all isotypes (IgG, IgA, IgM, IgE)                 | Normal                                                           | Very low/absent all isotypes (IgG, IgA, IgM, IgE)                 |
| **Cellular Defect**   | Impaired T-cell signaling, actin reorganization, platelet function | Absent B cells, impaired antibody production                  | Defective NADPH oxidase, impaired killing of phagocytosed microbes | Impaired lymphocyte development/function                          |
| **CD43 Expression**  | Decreased/Absent                                                 | Normal                                                           | Normal                                                           | Variable depending on specific SCID type                          |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome is characterized by the triad of eczema, thrombocytopenia (with small platelets), and recurrent infections, along with a specific immunoglobulin profile (low IgM, high IgA/IgE). The underlying defect is in the *WAS* gene, leading to a dysfunctional WASp protein, which is crucial for actin cytoskeleton organization and T-cell receptor signaling. This contrasts with other primary immunodeficiencies like XLA (B-cell defect, low all Ig), CGD (phagocyte defect, impaired oxidative burst), and SCID (severe T and B cell defects). Remember the classic triad and the X-linked inheritance pattern.
