<a id='question-138'></a>

### Question 138: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 38 (bacteria)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of primary immunodeficiencies, specifically focusing on the defect in phagocytic function leading to recurrent bacterial infections, particularly with catalase-positive organisms, and the characteristic diagnostic finding of absent or reduced nitroblue tetrazolium (NBT) reduction or dihydrorhodamine (DHR) oxidation. (本题考察原发性免疫缺陷，特别是吞噬功能缺陷导致的细菌感染，尤其是与 catalase 阳性菌的感染，以及特征性的 NBT 还原或 DHR 氧化缺失/减少的诊断发现。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 5-year-old boy presents with recurrent, severe bacterial infections, including pneumonia, skin abscesses (often caused by *Staphylococcus aureus* and *Serratia marcescens*), and lymphadenitis, starting shortly after birth. His infections are notably resistant to standard antibiotic therapy. Family history reveals that his maternal grandfather also suffered from similar recurrent infections. Laboratory investigations show normal immunoglobulin levels (IgG, IgA, IgM), normal lymphocyte counts, and normal complement levels (C3, C4, CH50). A neutrophil function test using dihydrorhodamine (DHR) 123 oxidation shows minimal fluorescence upon stimulation with phorbol myristate acetate (PMA), indicating a failure of neutrophils to produce reactive oxygen species (ROS). Genetic testing reveals a mutation in the *CYBB* gene located on the X chromosome.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying defect in this patient's neutrophils?
(A) Defective assembly of the NADPH oxidase complex due to mutations in the *gp91phox* gene.
(B) Defective binding of superoxide dismutase (SOD) to the phagosomal membrane.
(C) Defective translocation of the NADPH oxidase complex to the phagosome membrane.
(D) Defective production of the *p47phox* subunit of the NADPH oxidase complex.
(E) Defective binding of catalase to superoxide radicals within the phagosome.

#### Correct Answer: (A)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent severe bacterial infections (pneumonia, skin abscesses, lymphadenitis) starting early in life:** Suggests a primary immunodeficiency affecting innate immunity, particularly phagocyte function.
- **Infections caused by *Staphylococcus aureus* and *Serratia marcescens*:** These are catalase-positive organisms. Phagocytes normally kill catalase-positive bacteria using the respiratory burst (ROS production).
- **Resistance to standard antibiotic therapy:** Consistent with impaired bacterial killing by phagocytes, making infections harder to clear.
- **Normal immunoglobulin levels, lymphocyte counts, and complement levels:** Rules out humoral immunodeficiencies (e.g., XLA, CVID), cellular immunodeficiencies (e.g., SCID), and complement deficiencies.
- **DHR oxidation test shows minimal fluorescence:** DHR is oxidized to fluorescent rhodamine by ROS produced during the respiratory burst. Minimal fluorescence indicates a defect in ROS production.
- **Mutation in the *CYBB* gene (X-linked):** *CYBB* encodes gp91phox (NOX2), a crucial component of the NADPH oxidase complex.
- **The combination of clinical presentation (recurrent catalase-positive bacterial infections), defective ROS production (DHR test), and *CYBB* gene mutation points directly to Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD).**

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD):** A primary immunodeficiency characterized by defects in the NADPH oxidase complex, which is responsible for generating superoxide radicals (O2-) and other ROS within phagocytes (neutrophils, macrophages).
- **NADPH Oxidase Complex:** A multi-subunit enzyme complex located in the phagosomal membrane. It transfers electrons from NADPH in the cytoplasm to molecular oxygen (O2) within the phagosome, generating superoxide (O2-). Superoxide is then converted to other ROS like hydrogen peroxide (H2O2) by superoxide dismutase (SOD) and hydroxyl radicals (•OH) via the Fenton reaction.
- **Components of NADPH Oxidase:** The complex consists of membrane-bound subunits (gp91phox/NOX2 and p22phox) and cytosolic subunits (p47phox, p67phox, p40phox).
- **Mechanism of ROS Production:** Upon phagocyte activation (e.g., by bacteria), cytosolic subunits translocate to the membrane and assemble with gp91phox and p22phox to form the active NADPH oxidase. Electrons from NADPH are transferred to O2, generating O2-.
- **Role of ROS in Immunity:** ROS are crucial for killing ingested bacteria and fungi, especially catalase-positive organisms. Catalase breaks down H2O2 into water and oxygen, preventing the formation of toxic hydroxyl radicals. Therefore, phagocytes rely heavily on ROS production to kill catalase-positive bacteria.
- **CGD Pathophysiology:** Defects in any of the NADPH oxidase subunits lead to impaired ROS production. The most common form (about 70% of cases) is X-linked CGD, caused by mutations in the *CYBB* gene encoding the gp91phox subunit. Other forms are autosomal recessive, caused by mutations in genes encoding p22phox, p47phox, p67phox, or p40phox.
- **Clinical Manifestations of CGD:** Recurrent infections with catalase-positive bacteria (*S. aureus*, *Serratia marcescens*, *Burkholderia cepacia*, *Nocardia*) and fungi (*Aspergillus*). Granuloma formation in various organs due to the inability to clear microbes.
- **Diagnosis of CGD:** Dihydrorhodamine (DHR) oxidation test or Nitroblue Tetrazolium (NBT) reduction test. In the DHR test, neutrophils are stimulated, and DHR is added. Normally, ROS oxidize DHR to fluorescent rhodamine. In CGD, fluorescence is absent or markedly reduced.
- **Treatment of CGD:** Prophylactic antibiotics (e.g., trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole) and interferon-gamma (IFN-γ) to enhance phagocyte function. Hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (HSCT) can be curative.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Correct.** The *CYBB* gene encodes gp91phox (NOX2), a critical membrane-bound subunit of the NADPH oxidase complex. Mutations in *CYBB* are the most common cause of X-linked CGD, leading to defective ROS production. The clinical presentation, DHR test results, and genetic finding all point to this defect.
- (B): **Incorrect.** Superoxide dismutase (SOD) converts superoxide (O2-) to hydrogen peroxide (H2O2). While important for ROS metabolism, a defect in SOD binding would not explain the primary failure to produce superoxide, as indicated by the DHR test. SOD deficiency is not a known cause of CGD-like symptoms.
- (C): **Incorrect.** Defective translocation of the NADPH oxidase complex to the phagosome membrane is characteristic of Leukocyte Adhesion Deficiency type 1 (LAD-1), caused by mutations in the *ITGB2* gene encoding the CD18 subunit of β2 integrins. LAD-1 presents with recurrent bacterial infections, impaired wound healing, and delayed umbilical cord separation, but phagocytes can produce ROS normally. The DHR test would be normal in LAD-1.
- (D): **Incorrect.** The *p47phox* subunit is a cytosolic component of the NADPH oxidase complex. Mutations in the gene encoding *p47phox* (*NCF1*) cause autosomal recessive CGD. While this is a cause of CGD, the vignette specifically mentions a mutation in the *CYBB* gene, which encodes gp91phox. Therefore, this option is incorrect in the context of the given genetic information.
- (E): **Incorrect.** Catalase is an enzyme produced by bacteria (like *S. aureus*) and some host cells that breaks down hydrogen peroxide (H2O2). A defect in catalase binding to superoxide radicals is not a recognized mechanism in human phagocytes. Phagocytes rely on producing ROS (including H2O2) to kill catalase-positive bacteria, not on binding catalase to superoxide.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD) | Leukocyte Adhesion Deficiency (LAD-1) | Chediak-Higashi Syndrome | Myeloperoxidase (MPO) Deficiency |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :------------------------------------ | :------------------------ | :------------------------------ |
| **Primary Defect**      | NADPH Oxidase Function               | β2 Integrin (CD18) Function           | Lysosomal Trafficking    | Myeloperoxidase Enzyme Activity |
| **Gene Involved**       | *CYBB*, *NCF1*, *NCF2*, *NCF4*, *CYBA* | *ITGB2*                               | *LYST*                    | *MPO*                           |
| **Key Cell Type**       | Neutrophils, Macrophages             | Neutrophils                           | Neutrophils, Melanocytes  | Neutrophils                     |
| **Hallmark Finding**   | Defective ROS Production (DHR/NBT -) | Impaired Adhesion, Delayed Separation | Giant Lysosomes, Albinism | Normal ROS, Impaired H2O2 -> HOCl |
| **Typical Infections**  | Catalase+ Bacteria, *Aspergillus*    | Bacteria (no specific type)           | Bacteria, Viruses         | Candida                         |
| **Clinical Features**   | Granulomas, Abscesses                | Delayed Umbilical Cord Separation, Impaired Wound Healing | Partial Albinism, Neuropathy | Normal Immune Response (mostly) |
| **DHR Test Result**     | Negative/Reduced                     | Normal                                | Normal                    | Normal                          |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
CGD is a primary immunodeficiency caused by defects in the NADPH oxidase complex, leading to impaired ROS production and susceptibility to catalase-positive organisms. The DHR test is the standard diagnostic tool. Remember to differentiate CGD from other phagocyte disorders like LAD-1 (adhesion defect, normal ROS) and Chediak-Higashi syndrome (lysosomal defect, giant granules). The most common genetic cause is X-linked, involving the *CYBB* gene encoding gp91phox.

### Question 139: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 39 (Opportunistic Infections)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of primary immunodeficiencies, specifically focusing on defects in T-cell function (e.g., DiGeorge syndrome, SCID) and their susceptibility to opportunistic infections, particularly Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia (PJP), and the characteristic diagnostic findings. (本题考察原发性免疫缺陷，特别是T细胞功能缺陷（如DiGeorge综合征、重症联合免疫缺陷）对机会性感染的易感性，特别是肺囊虫肺炎（PJP），以及其特征性诊断发现。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-month-old male infant, born full-term without complications, presents with failure to thrive, recurrent respiratory infections (including thrush and pneumonia), and chronic diarrhea since 2 months of age. His mother reports that he has never had a fever despite these infections. Physical examination reveals a heart murmur, hypocalcemia (serum calcium 6.5 mg/dL), and facial dysmorphism (low-set ears, hypertelorism, micrognathia). Chest X-ray shows diffuse bilateral interstitial infiltrates. Bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL) fluid analysis reveals numerous cysts containing organisms with Giemsa staining, appearing as crushed ping-pong balls. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes shows markedly reduced numbers of CD3+ and CD4+ T cells, with normal numbers of CD19+ B cells and CD16+/CD56+ NK cells. Genetic testing confirms a 22q11.2 deletion.

Which of the following is the MOST likely underlying immunological defect contributing to this patient's susceptibility to Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia?

(A) Defective complement component C3 leading to impaired opsonization of encapsulated bacteria.
(B) Absence of functional T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling due to mutations in the ZAP-70 kinase gene.
(C) Impaired development of the thymus leading to deficient T-cell maturation and function.
(D) Defective B-cell class switching resulting in elevated IgM and reduced IgG, IgA, and IgE levels.
(E) Deficient expression of CD40 ligand (CD40L) on T cells, impairing T-cell dependent B-cell activation and macrophage activation.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Age and Presentation:** 6-month-old infant with failure to thrive, recurrent infections (thrush, pneumonia, diarrhea) since 2 months, absence of fever despite infections. This points towards a severe primary immunodeficiency affecting cell-mediated immunity, as fever is often a sign of robust inflammatory response.
*   **Physical Exam:** Heart murmur, hypocalcemia, facial dysmorphism (low-set ears, hypertelorism, micrognathia). These are classic features of DiGeorge syndrome (22q11.2 deletion syndrome). Hypocalcemia is due to parathyroid hypoplasia/aplasia.
*   **Chest X-ray:** Diffuse bilateral interstitial infiltrates are characteristic of Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia (PJP).
*   **BAL Fluid:** Giemsa staining showing cysts containing organisms resembling "crushed ping-pong balls" confirms PJP.
*   **Flow Cytometry:** Markedly reduced CD3+ and CD4+ T cells, normal CD19+ B cells and CD16+/CD56+ NK cells. This indicates a severe T-cell deficiency.
*   **Genetic Testing:** Confirms 22q11.2 deletion, confirming DiGeorge syndrome.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying immunological defect causing susceptibility to PJP.

The combination of clinical features (DiGeorge syndrome), severe T-cell deficiency (low CD3+, CD4+ cells), and opportunistic infection (PJP) strongly points to impaired T-cell development due to thymic hypoplasia/aplasia, a hallmark of DiGeorge syndrome.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **DiGeorge Syndrome (22q11.2 deletion syndrome):** Caused by a microdeletion on chromosome 22q11.2, affecting the development of the 3rd and 4th pharyngeal pouches. This leads to thymic hypoplasia/aplasia (resulting in T-cell deficiency), parathyroid hypoplasia/aplasia (hypocalcemia), congenital heart defects (e.g., conotruncal abnormalities like tetralogy of Fallot, interrupted aortic arch), and characteristic facial features.
*   **T-cell Function:** T cells (specifically CD4+ helper T cells) are crucial for cell-mediated immunity. They recognize antigens presented by MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells (APCs) via the T-cell receptor (TCR). Upon activation, they proliferate and differentiate into effector cells (e.g., Th1, Th2, Th17) and memory cells. They orchestrate immune responses by secreting cytokines that activate macrophages, B cells, and cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs).
*   **Pneumocystis jirovecii:** An opportunistic fungus that causes PJP, primarily in immunocompromised individuals, especially those with T-cell defects (like HIV/AIDS, DiGeorge syndrome, post-transplant patients on immunosuppressants). PJP involves the proliferation of P. jirovecii in the alveoli, leading to interstitial pneumonia.
*   **Immune Defense against P. jirovecii:** Effective defense against P. jirovecii relies heavily on cell-mediated immunity, particularly CD4+ T cells. Activated CD4+ T cells produce cytokines like IFN-γ, which activates macrophages to phagocytose and kill the organisms. CTLs (CD8+ T cells) also contribute. B cells and antibodies play a less significant role.
*   **Pathophysiology in DiGeorge:** The thymic defect in DiGeorge syndrome leads to a severe reduction in the number and function of mature T cells. This impaired cell-mediated immunity makes patients highly susceptible to intracellular pathogens and opportunistic infections like P. jirovecii.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective complement component C3:** C3 deficiency impairs opsonization (especially of encapsulated bacteria) and formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC). Patients are susceptible to recurrent pyogenic bacterial infections (e.g., Streptococcus pneumoniae, Haemophilus influenzae, Staphylococcus aureus) but typically not opportunistic fungal infections like PJP. The vignette describes PJP and T-cell deficiency, not typical bacterial infections associated with C3 deficiency.
- (B) **Absence of functional TCR signaling due to ZAP-70 mutations:** ZAP-70 is a tyrosine kinase essential for TCR signaling. Mutations cause X-linked severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID), characterized by a profound deficiency of both T cells and NK cells (T- B+ NK- SCID). While SCID patients are susceptible to PJP, the flow cytometry in the vignette shows normal NK cells (CD16+/CD56+), ruling out this specific type of SCID. Furthermore, the clinical features (heart murmur, hypocalcemia, facial dysmorphism) are characteristic of DiGeorge, not ZAP-70 deficiency.
- (D) **Defective B-cell class switching:** This leads to Hyper-IgM syndrome, characterized by normal or elevated IgM levels but low levels of IgG, IgA, and IgE. Patients are susceptible to extracellular bacterial infections (due to lack of opsonizing IgG) and opportunistic infections (like PJP, Cryptosporidium) due to impaired T-cell help for B-cell maturation and macrophage activation. While PJP can occur, the primary defect is in B-cell function, and the flow cytometry shows normal B-cell numbers, but the key finding is the severe T-cell deficiency, which is the direct cause of susceptibility to PJP in this context. The clinical picture strongly points to DiGeorge syndrome, which is primarily a T-cell defect.
- (E) **Deficient CD40L expression:** CD40L (CD154) is expressed on activated T cells and binds to CD40 on B cells, APCs, and other cells. Deficiency (X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome) impairs T-cell dependent B-cell activation (leading to low IgG, IgA, IgE, normal IgM) and macrophage activation. Patients are susceptible to opportunistic infections (like PJP) and extracellular bacteria. Similar to (D), while PJP can occur, the primary defect is related to T-cell help, but the flow cytometry specifically shows a severe reduction in T cells themselves (CD3+, CD4+), not just a defect in their interaction with B cells or macrophages. The clinical features also point strongly to DiGeorge syndrome.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | DiGeorge Syndrome (22q11.2 deletion) | ZAP-70 SCID (T- B+ NK-) | C3 Deficiency | Hyper-IgM (CD40L def.) | Hyper-IgM (AID def.) |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :--------------------- | :----------- | :-------------------- | :------------------ |
| **Primary Defect**      | Thymic hypoplasia/aplasia            | TCR signaling          | Complement   | T-cell help for B/Mac | B-cell class switch |
| **T Cells (CD3+)**      | Severely reduced                    | Absent                 | Normal       | Normal                | Normal              |
| **B Cells (CD19+)**     | Normal                              | Normal                 | Normal       | Normal                | Normal              |
| **NK Cells (CD16/56+)** | Normal                              | Absent                 | Normal       | Normal                | Normal              |
| **Ig Levels**           | Normal (usually)                     | Normal (usually)       | Normal       | High IgM, Low IgG/IgA/IgE | High IgM, Low IgG/IgA/IgE |
| **Typical Infections**  | PJP, fungal, viral                  | PJP, viral, bacterial   | Encapsulated bacteria | PJP, bacterial        | PJP, bacterial      |
| **Other Features**      | Heart defects, hypocalcemia, dysmorphism | None specific          | None specific | None specific         | None specific       |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The vignette describes a classic presentation of DiGeorge syndrome (22q11.2 deletion), characterized by T-cell deficiency (low CD3+, CD4+ cells), leading to susceptibility to opportunistic infections like PJP. The key immunological defect is impaired thymic development. Differentiating DiGeorge from other primary immunodeficiencies like SCID (e.g., ZAP-70, RAG deficiencies) or Hyper-IgM syndromes requires careful analysis of the specific cell lineage deficiencies (T, B, NK cells), immunoglobulin levels, and associated clinical features (cardiac defects, hypocalcemia, dysmorphism in DiGeorge).

<a id='question-140'></a>

### Question 140: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 40 (pathogens.)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the specific pathogens associated with defects in the complement system, particularly C3 deficiency, and its clinical manifestations. (理解补体系统缺陷，特别是C3缺陷，及其相关特定病原体和临床表现。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with recurrent, severe bacterial infections, including pneumonia, meningitis, and sepsis, starting in infancy. His infections are often caused by encapsulated bacteria, such as *Streptococcus pneumoniae*, *Haemophilus influenzae* type b (Hib), and *Neisseria meningitidis*. Despite receiving standard childhood vaccinations, including the Hib vaccine, he developed invasive Hib disease at age 2. Laboratory studies reveal normal immunoglobulin levels (IgG, IgA, IgM) and normal T-cell counts and function. However, his serum C3 levels are markedly reduced, while C1q, C4, and terminal complement components (C5-C9) are within normal limits. He also has a history of recurrent Neisseria infections, including gonococcal arthritis.

(A) Deficiency in CD40 ligand (CD40L)
(B) Deficiency in Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK)
(C) Deficiency in C3
(D) Deficiency in DiGeorge syndrome (22q11.2 deletion)
(E) Deficiency in NADPH oxidase

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Recurrent severe bacterial infections (pneumonia, meningitis, sepsis) starting in infancy:** Suggests a primary immunodeficiency affecting opsonization or complement pathways, crucial for defense against encapsulated bacteria.
*   **Infections caused by encapsulated bacteria (*S. pneumoniae*, *H. influenzae* type b, *N. meningitidis*):** These organisms are particularly susceptible to complement-mediated lysis and opsonization (via C3b). This strongly points towards a defect in the complement system or antibody production.
*   **Failure to respond to Hib vaccine despite vaccination:** The Hib vaccine relies on antibody production. However, the vignette states normal immunoglobulin levels, making a primary antibody deficiency less likely. Recurrent infections with encapsulated organisms despite vaccination can also occur with complement deficiencies, as complement is crucial for clearing these organisms even with adequate antibodies.
*   **Normal immunoglobulin levels (IgG, IgA, IgM) and normal T-cell counts/function:** Rules out common variable immunodeficiency (CVID), X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA), and severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) or DiGeorge syndrome, which typically involve antibody or T-cell defects.
*   **Markedly reduced serum C3 levels, normal C1q, C4, and C5-C9:** This specific pattern isolates the defect to the C3 component of the complement system. C3 is central to all three complement pathways (classical, alternative, lectin).
*   **Recurrent *Neisseria* infections (gonococcal arthritis):** *Neisseria* species are particularly susceptible to complement-mediated killing, especially via the membrane attack complex (MAC, C5b-C9). While C3 deficiency impairs all pathways leading to MAC formation, deficiencies in terminal components (C5-C9) cause more pronounced *Neisseria* susceptibility. However, C3 deficiency significantly increases susceptibility to *Neisseria* infections compared to healthy individuals.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Complement System Overview:** The complement system is a crucial part of innate immunity, involving a cascade of proteins that lead to opsonization (C3b), inflammation (C3a, C5a), and direct lysis of pathogens (MAC, C5b-C9).
*   **C3 Central Role:** C3 is cleaved by C3 convertases (C4b2a in classical/lectin, C3bBb in alternative) into C3a (anaphylatoxin) and C3b (opsonin). C3b also binds to the C3 convertase to form C5 convertase (C4b2a3b or C3bBb3b), initiating the terminal pathway. Deficiency in C3 disrupts all three pathways downstream of C3 activation.
*   **Clinical Manifestations of C3 Deficiency:** Patients with C3 deficiency are highly susceptible to infections with encapsulated bacteria (like *S. pneumoniae*, *H. influenzae*, *N. meningitidis*) due to impaired opsonization and lysis. They also have increased susceptibility to *Neisseria* infections, although less severe than terminal complement deficiencies. Autoimmune diseases, particularly systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE)-like syndromes, are also common due to impaired clearance of immune complexes and apoptotic debris.
*   **Pathogen Virulence:** Encapsulated bacteria evade phagocytosis by inhibiting complement activation or opsonization. Complement deficiency removes this crucial defense mechanism. *Neisseria* species are susceptible to complement-mediated lysis via the MAC.
*   **Vaccine Response:** While antibody response is normal in C3 deficiency, the effectiveness of vaccines against encapsulated bacteria may be reduced because complement is essential for clearing the bacteria after vaccination.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Deficiency in CD40 ligand (CD40L):** CD40L deficiency (X-linked hyper-IgM syndrome) affects T-cell dependent B-cell activation and isotype switching. Patients have normal or elevated IgM but low IgG, IgA, and IgE. They are susceptible to opportunistic infections (like *Pneumocystis jirovecii*) and bacterial infections, but the hallmark is the immunoglobulin profile, which is normal in this patient.
*   **(B) Deficiency in Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK):** BTK deficiency (X-linked agammaglobulinemia, XLA) prevents B-cell maturation beyond the pre-B cell stage. Patients have profoundly low levels of all immunoglobulin isotypes and absent B cells. They are highly susceptible to encapsulated bacteria, but the normal immunoglobulin levels rule this out.
*   **(D) Deficiency in DiGeorge syndrome (22q11.2 deletion):** DiGeorge syndrome involves thymic hypoplasia/aplasia, leading to T-cell deficiency. Patients have variable T-cell counts and function, often associated with hypocalcemia (due to parathyroid hypoplasia) and congenital heart defects. While they are susceptible to infections, particularly viral and fungal, the normal T-cell function and specific complement defect pattern rule this out.
*   **(E) Deficiency in NADPH oxidase:** NADPH oxidase deficiency (Chronic Granulomatous Disease, CGD) impairs the ability of phagocytes (neutrophils, macrophages) to produce reactive oxygen species (ROS) needed to kill ingested microbes. Patients are susceptible to catalase-positive organisms (like *Staphylococcus aureus*, *Aspergillus*, *Serratia*) that can survive within phagocytes. The clinical picture and specific lab findings (normal immunoglobulins, normal T-cells, low C3) do not fit CGD.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | C3 Deficiency (Correct)                                   | CD40L Deficiency (A)                 | BTK Deficiency (B)                  | DiGeorge Syndrome (D)              | NADPH Oxidase Deficiency (E)        |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :--------------------------------- | :----------------------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | C3 protein                                                | CD40L on T cells                     | BTK in B cells                      | Thymic development (T cells)       | NADPH oxidase in phagocytes         |
| **Immunoglobulin Levels** | Normal                                                    | Normal/High IgM, Low IgG/IgA/IgE      | Very Low IgG/IgA/IgM/IgE             | Normal (usually)                   | Normal                               |
| **T-cell Function**     | Normal                                                    | Normal                               | Normal                               | Deficient                          | Normal                               |
| **Key Pathogens**       | Encapsulated bacteria (*S. pneumo*, *H. flu*, *N. men*), *Neisseria* | Opportunistic, Encapsulated bacteria | Encapsulated bacteria               | Viral, Fungal, Opportunistic       | Catalase-positive bacteria/fungi    |
| **Complement Levels**   | Low C3, Normal C1q/C4/C5-C9                               | Normal                               | Normal                               | Normal                             | Normal                               |
| **Typical Presentation** | Recurrent bacterial infections, Autoimmunity              | Recurrent infections, High IgM        | Recurrent bacterial infections       | Infections, Hypocalcemia, Heart Defect | Granulomas, Abscesses, Infections |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
C3 deficiency is a primary immunodeficiency characterized by susceptibility to infections with encapsulated bacteria and *Neisseria* species, often associated with autoimmune disease. The key diagnostic finding is low serum C3 levels with normal levels of other complement components and immunoglobulins. Differentiate it from other primary immunodeficiencies based on specific immunoglobulin profiles (XLA, Hyper-IgM), T-cell defects (DiGeorge), or phagocyte dysfunction (CGD).

### Question 141: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 41 (Selective)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of primary immunodeficiencies, specifically focusing on defects in T-cell development and function, leading to susceptibility to opportunistic infections and graft rejection. It requires differentiating between various T-cell deficiencies based on clinical presentation and immunological findings. (本题考察原发性免疫缺陷，特别是T细胞发育和功能缺陷，导致对机会性感染和移植物排斥的易感性。需要根据临床表现和免疫学发现区分不同的T细胞缺陷。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with recurrent severe infections, including Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia (PJP) at 6 months of age, persistent oral candidiasis, and failure to thrive. He received Bacillus Calmette-Guérin (BCG) vaccine at birth, which resulted in disseminated BCG infection. He underwent a hematopoietic stem cell transplant (HSCT) at age 2 for severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID). Post-transplant, he developed severe graft-versus-host disease (GVHD), characterized by skin rash, diarrhea, and liver dysfunction. Immunological evaluation after HSCT revealed a lack of T cells (CD3+) in the patient's peripheral blood, but the donor-derived T cells (CD3+) were present in the patient's blood and skin biopsies, indicating successful engraftment of the donor immune system. Flow cytometry on the patient's pre-transplant peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs) showed a marked reduction in CD3+ T cells, CD4+ helper T cells, and CD8+ cytotoxic T cells, with normal numbers of B cells (CD19+) and NK cells (CD16+/CD56+). Genetic testing revealed a homozygous mutation in the *RAG1* gene.

Which of the following immunological defects is most consistent with this patient's pre-transplant condition?

(A) Defective T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling due to mutations in the *ZAP70* gene.
(B) Impaired development of the thymus due to mutations in the *TBX1* gene.
(C) Failure of T-cell and B-cell development due to defective V(D)J recombination.
(D) Defective MHC class II expression due to mutations in the *CIITA* gene.
(E) Defective T-cell maturation and survival due to mutations in the *IL2RG* gene.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent severe infections (PJP, candidiasis):** Suggests a profound T-cell deficiency, as T cells are crucial for controlling intracellular pathogens like *Pneumocystis jirovecii* and fungi like *Candida*.
- **Disseminated BCG infection:** BCG is an attenuated *Mycobacterium bovis* vaccine. Disseminated infection after BCG vaccination is a classic sign of severe T-cell immunodeficiency, particularly SCID.
- **Failure to thrive:** Common in severe immunodeficiencies due to chronic infections and malabsorption.
- **Hematopoietic stem cell transplant (HSCT) for SCID:** Confirms the diagnosis of a severe primary immunodeficiency affecting lymphocyte development.
- **Severe graft-versus-host disease (GVHD) post-HSCT:** Indicates successful engraftment of donor T cells, which are then attacking the recipient's tissues due to the lack of recipient T-cell regulation. This further supports a severe T-cell defect in the recipient.
- **Flow cytometry pre-transplant:** Shows absence of CD3+ T cells (both CD4+ and CD8+), normal CD19+ B cells, and normal CD16+/CD56+ NK cells. This pattern is characteristic of SCID.
- **Genetic testing: Homozygous mutation in the *RAG1* gene:** *RAG1* and *RAG2* genes encode enzymes essential for V(D)J recombination, the process that generates diverse T-cell receptors (TCRs) and B-cell receptors (BCRs). Mutations in *RAG1* or *RAG2* lead to a failure of both T-cell and B-cell development.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **V(D)J Recombination:** This process is critical for the development of adaptive immunity. Recombination activating genes (*RAG1* and *RAG2*) encode enzymes that initiate the process by introducing double-strand breaks at specific recombination signal sequences (RSSs) flanking the variable (V), diversity (D), and joining (J) gene segments. This allows for the rearrangement and joining of these segments to create unique antigen receptors (TCRs and BCRs).
- **Role of RAG1/RAG2:** Without functional RAG1/RAG2, lymphocytes cannot undergo V(D)J recombination. This results in a profound block in the development of both T cells (in the thymus) and B cells (in the bone marrow).
- **SCID due to RAG deficiency:** Patients with *RAG1* or *RAG2* mutations typically present with SCID, characterized by the absence of mature T cells and B cells, leading to severe susceptibility to infections (bacterial, viral, fungal, protozoal) early in life. NK cell function is usually normal.
- **Differential Diagnosis of SCID:** SCID is a heterogeneous group of disorders. Common genetic defects include mutations in *IL2RG* (X-linked SCID, affecting common gamma chain of cytokine receptors), *JAK3* (autosomal recessive SCID, affecting cytokine signaling), *ADA* (adenosine deaminase deficiency, leading to toxic metabolite accumulation), *RAG1/RAG2* (V(D)J recombination defect), *DCLRE1C* (Artemis deficiency, involved in V(D)J recombination repair), and others.
- **Clinical Manifestations:** The clinical picture described (early onset severe infections, PJP, candidiasis, disseminated BCG, failure to thrive) is highly suggestive of SCID. The specific finding of absent T cells and B cells (but normal NK cells) points towards defects in lymphocyte development, such as RAG deficiency or ADA deficiency.
- **Mechanism of *RAG1* mutation:** A homozygous mutation in *RAG1* prevents the RAG1 protein from functioning correctly, thereby halting the V(D)J recombination process. This leads to a lack of TCR gene rearrangement in developing thymocytes, preventing their maturation into functional T cells. Similarly, BCR gene rearrangement is blocked in developing B cells, preventing their maturation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling due to mutations in the *ZAP70* gene:** *ZAP70* is a tyrosine kinase crucial for TCR signaling. Mutations in *ZAP70* cause an autosomal recessive form of SCID, but typically patients have normal or near-normal numbers of T cells (CD3+), although these T cells are non-functional due to impaired signaling. The flow cytometry in the vignette shows a *lack* of T cells, making this incorrect.
- (B) **Impaired development of the thymus due to mutations in the *TBX1* gene:** *TBX1* mutations cause DiGeorge syndrome (22q11.2 deletion syndrome). This syndrome is characterized by thymic hypoplasia or aplasia, leading to T-cell deficiency (variable severity, often partial T-cell deficiency). Patients typically have normal B-cell numbers. While T-cell deficiency is present, the complete absence of T cells and B cells seen in the vignette is not typical for DiGeorge syndrome, and the genetic defect is different.
- (D) **Defective MHC class II expression due to mutations in the *CIITA* gene:** Mutations in *CIITA* (Class II Transactivator) cause MHC class II deficiency (also known as Bare Lymphocyte Syndrome type II). This defect primarily affects antigen presentation by MHC class II molecules, leading to impaired CD4+ T-cell help. CD8+ T cells and B cells are generally normal in number, although B-cell function may be impaired due to lack of T-cell help. The flow cytometry shows a lack of *all* T cells (CD3+, CD4+, CD8+), not just CD4+ T cells, making this incorrect.
- (E) **Defective T-cell maturation and survival due to mutations in the *IL2RG* gene:** Mutations in *IL2RG* cause X-linked SCID (the most common form of SCID). The IL-2 receptor gamma chain (encoded by *IL2RG*) is a component of receptors for several cytokines crucial for lymphocyte development and survival (IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, IL-21). Mutations lead to a lack of functional T cells and NK cells, but B cells are usually present, although their function is impaired due to lack of T-cell help. The vignette shows normal NK cells and absent B cells, which is inconsistent with X-linked SCID.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | RAG1/RAG2 Deficiency (SCID) | ZAP70 Deficiency (SCID) | TBX1 Deficiency (DiGeorge) | CIITA Deficiency (MHC II Def.) | IL2RG Deficiency (X-linked SCID) |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------- | :----------------------- | :------------------------- | :----------------------------- | :-------------------------------- |
| **Genetic Defect**      | V(D)J Recombination         | TCR Signaling           | Thymic Development         | MHC Class II Expression        | Cytokine Receptor Signaling      |
| **T Cells (CD3+)**      | Absent                     | Normal/Near Normal      | Reduced/Absent             | Normal                        | Absent                            |
| **B Cells (CD19+)**      | Absent                     | Normal                  | Normal                    | Normal                        | Present (function impaired)      |
| **NK Cells (CD16+/56+)** | Normal                     | Normal                  | Normal                    | Normal                        | Absent                            |
| **Key Clinical Feature** | Absent T & B cells         | Non-functional T cells  | Variable T cell deficiency | Impaired CD4+ T cell help      | Absent T & NK cells              |
| **BCG Reaction**        | Disseminated                | Disseminated            | Variable                  | Normal                        | Disseminated                      |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The absence of both T cells and B cells, with normal NK cells, is the hallmark of SCID caused by defects in lymphocyte development, such as mutations in *RAG1*, *RAG2*, *DCLRE1C* (Artemis), or *ADA*. Differentiating SCID subtypes requires understanding the specific molecular defect and its impact on lymphocyte development pathways. Remember that *RAG1/RAG2* mutations block V(D)J recombination, leading to a complete absence of mature T and B cells.

<a id='question-142'></a>

### Question 142: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 42 (deficiency)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of specific primary immunodeficiencies, particularly focusing on the molecular defect and resulting clinical manifestations associated with defects in DNA repair pathways, specifically in the context of graft-versus-host disease (GVHD) and hypersensitivity reactions. The core concept is linking a specific genetic defect (ATM mutation) to a specific immunodeficiency (Ataxia-Telangiectasia) and its characteristic clinical features (neurological symptoms, telangiectasias, immunodeficiency, increased cancer risk) and complications (GVHD, hypersensitivity).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-year-old boy with a history of recurrent sinopulmonary infections, progressive cerebellar ataxia, and oculocutaneous telangiectasias is admitted to the hospital following a hematopoietic stem cell transplant (HSCT) from a matched unrelated donor for severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID). The transplant was initially successful, but 6 months post-transplant, the boy develops severe skin rash, diarrhea, and liver dysfunction. Biopsy of the skin and liver reveals lymphocytic infiltration and apoptosis of epithelial cells. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a significant reduction in CD8+ T cells and increased numbers of activated CD4+ T cells. Serum immunoglobulins are normal. The boy's mother has a history of similar, albeit milder, neurological symptoms and telangiectasias. Which of the following molecular defects is most likely responsible for this patient's underlying immunodeficiency and subsequent complications?

(A) Defective recombination activating genes (RAG1/RAG2) leading to impaired V(D)J recombination and T-cell/B-cell development.
(B) Defective DNA double-strand break repair leading to genomic instability, impaired T-cell development, and increased sensitivity to ionizing radiation.
(C) Defective CD40 ligand (CD40L) expression on activated T cells leading to impaired B-cell class switching and T-cell dependent antibody responses.
(D) Defective Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK) leading to impaired B-cell development and maturation.
(E) Defective interleukin-2 receptor gamma chain (IL2RG) leading to impaired T-cell, NK-cell, and B-cell development.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 6-year-old boy.
*   **History:** Recurrent sinopulmonary infections (suggests immunodeficiency), progressive cerebellar ataxia (neurological symptom), oculocutaneous telangiectasias (characteristic physical finding). This triad strongly points towards Ataxia-Telangiectasia (A-T).
*   **Current Event:** HSCT for SCID (patient likely had underlying SCID, possibly A-T related SCID or another primary immunodeficiency requiring transplant).
*   **Post-Transplant Complication:** 6 months post-HSCT, develops severe skin rash, diarrhea, liver dysfunction (classic signs of Graft-versus-Host Disease - GVHD).
*   **Biopsy Findings:** Lymphocytic infiltration and apoptosis of epithelial cells in skin and liver (consistent with GVHD).
*   **Flow Cytometry:** Reduced CD8+ T cells, increased activated CD4+ T cells (suggests donor T cells attacking host tissues, characteristic of GVHD).
*   **Serum Immunoglobulins:** Normal (rules out some B-cell defects like XLA).
*   **Family History:** Mother has similar neurological symptoms and telangiectasias (suggests autosomal recessive inheritance pattern).
*   **Question:** Asks for the *underlying* molecular defect responsible for the patient's immunodeficiency and complications (including susceptibility to GVHD).

The combination of ataxia, telangiectasias, recurrent infections, and the development of severe GVHD post-HSCT is highly characteristic of Ataxia-Telangiectasia (A-T). A-T is caused by mutations in the *ATM* gene, which encodes a protein kinase crucial for DNA double-strand break repair. This defect leads to genomic instability, impaired lymphocyte development (especially T cells), neurological degeneration, and increased sensitivity to ionizing radiation. Patients with A-T are particularly susceptible to severe GVHD after HSCT, likely due to impaired T-cell regulation and tolerance mechanisms.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Ataxia-Telangiectasia (A-T):** An autosomal recessive disorder caused by mutations in the *ATM* gene (Ataxia Telangiectasia Mutated). The ATM protein is a serine/threonine kinase that plays a central role in the cellular response to DNA double-strand breaks (DSBs). It activates downstream signaling pathways involving proteins like p53, Chk2, and BRCA1, leading to cell cycle arrest, DNA repair, or apoptosis.
*   **Molecular Defect:** Loss-of-function mutations in *ATM* impair the repair of DSBs, leading to genomic instability. This affects rapidly dividing cells, including lymphocytes, leading to impaired T-cell development (thymic atrophy, lymphopenia) and increased risk of malignancy (especially lymphomas and leukemias). Neurological symptoms (ataxia, oculomotor apraxia) result from cerebellar degeneration. Telangiectasias are caused by vascular abnormalities.
*   **Immunodeficiency in A-T:** Primarily affects T-cell immunity due to impaired V(D)J recombination and thymic selection, although B-cell function can also be affected. Patients typically have low IgA and IgE levels, and impaired T-cell responses to antigens.
*   **GVHD in A-T:** Patients with A-T undergoing HSCT have a significantly increased risk of severe, often fatal, GVHD. The exact mechanism is not fully understood but may involve impaired donor T-cell regulation, defective recipient antigen presentation, or increased sensitivity of host tissues to donor T-cell attack due to genomic instability.
*   **Other Options:**
    *   (A) RAG1/RAG2 defects cause SCID by preventing V(D)J recombination, leading to a lack of mature T and B cells. While this patient received an HSCT for SCID, the specific triad of ataxia, telangiectasias, and GVHD points away from RAG deficiency as the *primary* underlying defect.
    *   (C) CD40L deficiency (X-linked hyper-IgM syndrome) impairs T-cell help to B cells, leading to defective class switching and low IgG, IgA, IgE. Patients have recurrent infections but typically do not have ataxia or telangiectasias.
    *   (D) BTK deficiency (X-linked agammaglobulinemia, XLA) causes a block in B-cell development, leading to absent B cells and immunoglobulins. Patients have recurrent bacterial infections but normal T-cell function, no ataxia, and no telangiectasias.
    *   (E) IL2RG deficiency (X-linked SCID) affects signaling through the common gamma chain of several cytokine receptors (IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, IL-21), leading to impaired development of T cells, NK cells, and B cells. While it causes SCID, it does not cause ataxia or telangiectasias.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Defective RAG1/RAG2 leads to SCID due to failure of V(D)J recombination, resulting in absent T and B cells. This patient has a history of infections and underwent HSCT for SCID, but the specific clinical features (ataxia, telangiectasias) and the development of severe GVHD are not typical for RAG deficiency. RAG deficiency primarily affects lymphocyte development, not DNA repair or neurological function in this manner.
- (B): This is the correct answer. Defective ATM protein impairs DNA double-strand break repair, leading to genomic instability, impaired T-cell development (causing immunodeficiency), neurological degeneration (ataxia), and increased cancer risk. Patients with A-T are known to be highly susceptible to severe GVHD post-HSCT. The clinical triad presented in the vignette is pathognomonic for A-T.
- (C): Defective CD40L (hyper-IgM syndrome) impairs T-cell dependent B-cell activation and class switching. Patients have recurrent infections, particularly opportunistic infections, but lack the neurological and vascular features of A-T. GVHD is not a characteristic complication.
- (D): Defective BTK (XLA) causes a block in B-cell maturation, leading to agammaglobulinemia and recurrent bacterial infections. T-cell function is normal. Patients do not have ataxia, telangiectasias, or the specific GVHD susceptibility seen in A-T.
- (E): Defective IL2RG (X-linked SCID) affects multiple lymphocyte lineages (T, NK, B cells) due to impaired cytokine signaling. This causes severe combined immunodeficiency but does not explain the ataxia, telangiectasias, or the specific predisposition to GVHD associated with A-T.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Ataxia-Telangiectasia (ATM defect) | RAG1/RAG2 Deficiency (SCID) | CD40L Deficiency (Hyper-IgM) | BTK Deficiency (XLA) | IL2RG Deficiency (X-linked SCID) |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------------- | :-------------------------- | :--------------------------- | :------------------ | :-------------------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | DNA DSB Repair (ATM gene)           | V(D)J Recombination (RAG1/2) | CD40L Expression             | BTK Expression      | IL-2Rγc Signaling (IL2RG)        |
| **Key Clinical Features** | Ataxia, Telangiectasias, Infections, Cancer Risk, GVHD susceptibility | Severe Infections (early onset) | Recurrent Infections, Opportunistic Infections | Recurrent Bacterial Infections | Severe Infections (early onset) |
| **Lymphocyte Affected** | T cells (primarily), B cells        | T cells, B cells            | B cells                      | B cells             | T cells, NK cells, B cells        |
| **Immunoglobulin Levels** | Often low IgA, IgE                 | Absent Ig (all classes)     | Normal/High IgM, Low IgG/IgA/IgE | Absent Ig (all classes) | Absent Ig (all classes)           |
| **Neurological**        | Progressive Cerebellar Ataxia       | Normal                     | Normal                       | Normal              | Normal                            |
| **Vascular**            | Telangiectasias                     | Normal                     | Normal                       | Normal              | Normal                            |
| **GVHD Risk**           | High                                | Variable                   | Normal                       | Normal              | Normal                            |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Ataxia-Telangiectasia is a primary immunodeficiency caused by mutations in the *ATM* gene, leading to defective DNA double-strand break repair. Clinically, it presents with the triad of cerebellar ataxia, oculocutaneous telangiectasias, and recurrent sinopulmonary infections. Patients are highly susceptible to severe graft-versus-host disease (GVHD) following hematopoietic stem cell transplantation. Differentiating A-T from other SCID forms (like RAG, IL2RG, CD40L deficiencies) relies on recognizing the characteristic neurological and vascular findings. XLA (BTK defect) primarily affects B cells and lacks neurological features.

<a id='question-143'></a>

### Question 143: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 43 (Multiple)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of primary immunodeficiencies, specifically focusing on defects in T-cell development and function, and their clinical manifestations, including susceptibility to opportunistic infections and graft rejection. (本题考察原发性免疫缺陷，特别是T细胞发育和功能缺陷，及其临床表现，包括对机会性感染的易感性和移植物排斥。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with recurrent severe infections, including Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia (PJP) and disseminated cytomegalovirus (CMV) infection, starting within the first few months of life. He has failed to thrive and exhibits chronic diarrhea. He received a hematopoietic stem cell transplant (HSCT) from an HLA-matched unrelated donor at age 2. One year post-transplant, he develops acute graft-versus-host disease (GVHD), characterized by skin rash, diarrhea, and liver dysfunction. Despite immunosuppressive therapy, the GVHD is refractory. Flow cytometry analysis of his peripheral blood lymphocytes reveals a profound deficiency of CD3+ T cells and CD4+ T cells, with normal numbers of CD19+ B cells and CD16+/CD56+ NK cells. Further investigation shows that the donor T cells used for the HSCT were irradiated prior to infusion to prevent GVHD. However, the patient's own T cells, which persisted despite the transplant, were found to be non-functional and unable to proliferate in response to mitogenic stimulation. Genetic testing reveals a homozygous mutation in the *RAG1* gene.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying immunological defect in this patient?

(A) Defective expression of the T-cell receptor (TCR) complex due to impaired V(D)J recombination.
(B) Impaired development of the thymus due to a defect in the DiGeorge syndrome critical region (DSCR).
(C) Defective signaling downstream of the pre-TCR complex due to mutations in the *CD3* signaling pathway.
(D) Defective expression of CD4 or CD8 co-receptors due to mutations in the *IL2RG* gene.
(E) Defective expression of MHC class I or class II molecules due to mutations in the *TAP1/TAP2* or *CIITA* genes.

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent severe infections (PJP, CMV) early in life:** Suggests a severe T-cell immunodeficiency, as T cells are crucial for controlling intracellular pathogens like *Pneumocystis* and viruses like CMV.
- **Failure to thrive, chronic diarrhea:** Common manifestations of severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) due to lack of T-cell help for B cells and impaired cell-mediated immunity.
- **Hematopoietic stem cell transplant (HSCT) from unrelated donor:** Treatment for SCID, aiming to reconstitute the immune system.
- **Acute graft-versus-host disease (GVHD) post-HSCT:** Indicates the presence of functional donor T cells capable of recognizing host antigens as foreign. The irradiation of donor cells was intended to prevent this, but the patient's own non-functional T cells persisted.
- **Flow cytometry: Deficiency of CD3+ and CD4+ T cells, normal B cells and NK cells:** Points towards a defect specifically affecting T-cell development or function, consistent with SCID.
- **Patient's own T cells are non-functional and unable to proliferate:** Suggests an intrinsic defect in the patient's T-cell lineage.
- **Homozygous mutation in the *RAG1* gene:** This is the key genetic clue. *RAG1* and *RAG2* genes encode recombinase enzymes essential for V(D)J recombination, the process that generates the diverse T-cell receptor (TCR) and B-cell receptor (BCR) repertoires.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **V(D)J Recombination:** This process occurs in developing lymphocytes (T and B cells) in the bone marrow (B cells) and thymus (T cells). Recombinase activating genes (RAG1 and RAG2) initiate the process by creating double-strand breaks at specific recombination signal sequences (RSSs) flanking the variable (V), diversity (D), and joining (J) gene segments. These segments are then joined together, creating a unique gene encoding the antigen receptor.
- ***RAG1* Mutation:** A mutation in *RAG1* (or *RAG2*) leads to a failure of V(D)J recombination. This results in a profound block in the development of both T cells (CD3+, CD4+, CD8+) and B cells. However, the vignette specifically highlights the T-cell deficiency based on flow cytometry and clinical presentation. The lack of functional T cells explains the susceptibility to opportunistic infections (PJP, CMV) and the inability to mount an immune response. The persistence of non-functional patient T cells after HSCT explains the development of GVHD, as the donor T cells (despite irradiation, some may survive or the irradiation may have been insufficient) recognize the patient's tissues as foreign, but the patient's own T cells cannot regulate this response.
- **SCID Types:** SCID encompasses various genetic defects leading to severe T-cell deficiency. *RAG1/RAG2* mutations cause Omenn syndrome (a leaky SCID with eosinophilia, erythroderma, hepatosplenomegaly, and lymphadenopathy) or severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) depending on the specific mutation and residual RAG activity. The clinical picture described (severe infections, failure to thrive) is classic for SCID.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Defective expression of the T-cell receptor (TCR) complex due to impaired V(D)J recombination.** This is the correct answer. Mutations in *RAG1* or *RAG2* directly impair V(D)J recombination, preventing the formation of functional TCR genes and thus the development of mature T cells. This aligns perfectly with the genetic finding and the clinical/immunological phenotype.
- (B): **Impaired development of the thymus due to a defect in the DiGeorge syndrome critical region (DSCR).** DiGeorge syndrome (22q11.2 deletion syndrome) involves defects in the development of the 3rd and 4th pharyngeal pouches, leading to thymic hypoplasia/aplasia, parathyroid hypoplasia, and cardiac defects. While it causes T-cell deficiency, it typically involves *reduced* numbers of T cells, not a complete block in development due to V(D)J recombination failure. B-cell numbers are usually normal, and NK cells are normal. The genetic defect is in DSCR genes (e.g., *TBX1*), not *RAG1*.
- (C): **Defective signaling downstream of the pre-TCR complex due to mutations in the *CD3* signaling pathway.** Mutations in genes like *CD3D*, *CD3E*, *CD3G*, or *ZAP70* (involved in the pre-TCR and TCR signaling) cause T-cell deficiencies, often characterized by a block at the double-positive (DP) stage of T-cell development in the thymus. This leads to reduced numbers of mature single-positive (SP) T cells (CD4+ and CD8+). While it affects T-cell function, the primary defect is in signaling, not V(D)J recombination, and the genetic cause is different (*CD3* pathway genes, not *RAG1*).
- (D): **Defective expression of CD4 or CD8 co-receptors due to mutations in the *IL2RG* gene.** Mutations in *IL2RG* (encoding the common gamma chain, shared by IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, and IL-21 receptors) cause X-linked SCID (SCID-X1). This defect primarily affects T-cell development and function (due to lack of IL-7 and IL-15 signaling) and NK cell function. B-cell numbers are typically normal or increased, but B-cell function is impaired due to lack of T-cell help. The genetic defect is in *IL2RG*, not *RAG1*.
- (E): **Defective expression of MHC class I or class II molecules due to mutations in the *TAP1/TAP2* or *CIITA* genes.** Defects in MHC expression or antigen presentation lead to Bare Lymphocyte Syndrome (BLS). MHC class I deficiency (due to *TAP1/TAP2* mutations) primarily affects CD8+ T cells, leading to susceptibility to viral infections. MHC class II deficiency (due to *CIITA* mutations) affects CD4+ T cells, leading to susceptibility to bacterial infections. While these cause immunodeficiency, the primary defect is in antigen presentation, not T-cell receptor generation via V(D)J recombination, and the genetic cause is different (*TAP1/2*, *CIITA*, not *RAG1*).

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | RAG1 Deficiency (Correct Answer) | DiGeorge Syndrome (DSCR defect) | CD3 Signaling Defect (e.g., ZAP70) | X-linked SCID (IL2RG defect) | MHC Class II Deficiency (CIITA defect) |
|-------------------------|-----------------------------------|-----------------------------------|--------------------------------------|---------------------------------|----------------------------------------|
| **Primary Defect**      | V(D)J Recombination              | Thymic Development              | Pre/Post-TCR Signaling              | IL-7/IL-15 Signaling           | Antigen Presentation (MHC-II)         |
| **Gene(s) Involved**    | *RAG1*, *RAG2*                   | *TBX1*, *GATA3*, etc. (DSCR)     | *CD3D*, *CD3E*, *CD3G*, *ZAP70*      | *IL2RG*                        | *CIITA*                               |
| **T-cell Development**  | Blocked (no TCR rearrangement)   | Reduced numbers (thymic hypoplasia) | Blocked at DP stage (reduced SP cells) | Reduced numbers/function        | Normal development, impaired function |
| **B-cell Development**  | Blocked (no BCR rearrangement)   | Normal                          | Normal                               | Normal/Increased (poor function)  | Normal                                |
| **NK Cells**            | Normal                          | Normal                          | Normal                               | Deficient                       | Normal                                |
| **Typical Infections**  | Opportunistic (PJP, CMV)         | Opportunistic (PJP, Candida)     | Opportunistic (PJP, CMV)             | Opportunistic (PJP, CMV, Viral)   | Bacterial (Encapsulated organisms)   |
| **Genetic Pattern**     | Autosomal Recessive              | Variable (often de novo)         | Autosomal Recessive                  | X-linked Recessive              | Autosomal Recessive                   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Mutations in *RAG1* or *RAG2* genes impair V(D)J recombination, leading to a block in both T-cell and B-cell development, resulting in SCID. This is a critical cause of SCID to differentiate from other primary immunodeficiencies affecting T-cell development or function.
- **Differentials:** Remember to differentiate *RAG1/2* deficiency (V(D)J recombination block) from DiGeorge syndrome (thymic hypoplasia), CD3 signaling defects (block at DP stage), X-linked SCID (*IL2RG* defect, impaired IL-7/15 signaling), and MHC deficiencies (antigen presentation defects). Each has distinct genetic causes, cellular phenotypes, and clinical presentations.

<a id='question-144'></a>

### Question 144: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 44 (genetic)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of primary immunodeficiencies, specifically focusing on the genetic defect in Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome (WAS) and its resulting immunological and clinical manifestations, including eczema, thrombocytopenia, recurrent infections, and increased risk of autoimmunity and malignancy. (本题考察原发性免疫缺陷病，特别是Wiskott-Aldrich综合征（WAS）的遗传缺陷及其导致的免疫学和临床表现，包括湿疹、血小板减少症、复发性感染以及自身免疫和恶性肿瘤的风险增加。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with a history of severe eczema since infancy, recurrent otitis media, and easy bruising. His mother reports that he has had multiple episodes of pneumonia requiring hospitalization. Physical examination reveals generalized eczema, petechiae on his lower extremities, and mild splenomegaly. Laboratory tests show thrombocytopenia (platelet count 25,000/µL) with small platelets, and low levels of IgM and IgA, with normal IgG and IgE. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes reveals absent expression of the CD43 protein on T cells and platelets. The patient's older brother died at age 5 from complications of a lymphoma. Which of the following genetic defects is most likely responsible for this patient's condition?

(A) Defect in the WAS gene encoding the Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome protein (WASP)
(B) Defect in the gene encoding the common gamma chain (γc), shared by IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, and IL-21 receptors
(C) Defect in the gene encoding Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK)
(D) Defect in the gene encoding the CD40 ligand (CD40L)
(E) Defect in the gene encoding the adenosine deaminase (ADA) enzyme

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 3-year-old boy. This age is typical for the presentation of several primary immunodeficiencies.
- **Clinical Triad:** Eczema (since infancy), thrombocytopenia (easy bruising, petechiae, small platelets), recurrent infections (otitis media, pneumonia). This classic triad strongly suggests Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome (WAS).
- **Laboratory Findings:** Thrombocytopenia with *small* platelets is a hallmark of WAS. Immunoglobulin profile shows low IgM and IgA, normal IgG, and normal/high IgE. This pattern is characteristic of WAS.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Absent CD43 expression on T cells and platelets. CD43 (sialophorin) is normally expressed on leukocytes and platelets. Its absence is a specific finding in WAS.
- **Family History:** Older brother died from lymphoma. Patients with WAS have a significantly increased risk of developing autoimmune disorders and malignancies, particularly lymphomas.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying genetic defect.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Wiskott-Aldrich Syndrome (WAS):** An X-linked recessive primary immunodeficiency caused by mutations in the *WAS* gene located on the X chromosome (Xp11.23).
- **WASP Protein:** The *WAS* gene encodes the Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome protein (WASP). WASP is a cytoplasmic protein crucial for actin cytoskeleton organization and signaling in hematopoietic cells, particularly T cells, B cells, platelets, and NK cells.
- **Mechanism of Defect:** WASP interacts with various signaling molecules, including Vav family proteins, N-WASP, and WASN1, to regulate actin polymerization and cell motility. Defects in WASP lead to impaired T cell function (especially T cell synapse formation and migration), defective B cell responses (leading to abnormal immunoglobulin production), and abnormal platelet formation and function (leading to thrombocytopenia with small platelets).
- **Clinical Manifestations:** The classic triad of eczema, thrombocytopenia (with small platelets), and recurrent infections (due to impaired T cell and B cell function). Patients are also prone to autoimmune diseases (e.g., autoimmune hemolytic anemia, inflammatory bowel disease) and malignancies (especially lymphomas).
- **Immunological Features:** Low IgM, low IgA, normal IgG, elevated IgE. Impaired T cell responses to polysaccharide antigens. Defective cytotoxic T lymphocyte (CTL) and natural killer (NK) cell function. Absent CD43 expression on lymphocytes and platelets is a characteristic finding.
- **CD43 (Sialophorin):** A heavily glycosylated transmembrane protein expressed on leukocytes and platelets. Its function is not fully understood, but it is involved in cell adhesion, migration, and signaling. Its absence is a specific marker for WAS.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Correct.** This option correctly identifies the genetic defect in WAS, which aligns perfectly with the clinical triad, laboratory findings (thrombocytopenia with small platelets, Ig profile, absent CD43), and family history presented in the vignette.
- (B): **Incorrect.** Defects in the common gamma chain (γc) cause X-linked Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (X-SCID). X-SCID presents much earlier (within the first few months of life) with severe, life-threatening infections (bacterial, viral, fungal, opportunistic) due to profound defects in T cells, B cells, and NK cells. Patients typically have very low lymphocyte counts and absent T cell function. Eczema and thrombocytopenia are not characteristic features.
- (C): **Incorrect.** Defects in Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK) cause X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA). XLA presents after 6 months of age (when maternal antibodies wane) with recurrent bacterial infections, particularly encapsulated organisms (e.g., *Streptococcus pneumoniae*, *Haemophilus influenzae*). Patients have very low levels of all immunoglobulin isotypes (IgG, IgA, IgM, IgE) and absent B cells. Eczema and thrombocytopenia are not features of XLA.
- (D): **Incorrect.** Defects in the CD40 ligand (CD40L) cause X-linked hyper-IgM syndrome. Patients have normal or elevated IgM levels but low IgG, IgA, and IgE levels due to impaired class switching in B cells. They present with recurrent bacterial infections (especially opportunistic infections like *Pneumocystis jirovecii*) and often have neutropenia. Eczema and thrombocytopenia are not typical features.
- (E): **Incorrect.** Defects in the adenosine deaminase (ADA) enzyme cause Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID), specifically ADA-SCID. This is an autosomal recessive disorder. ADA deficiency leads to the accumulation of toxic metabolites (deoxyadenosine) that are particularly toxic to lymphocytes, resulting in profound lymphopenia and severe combined immunodeficiency similar to X-SCID. Eczema and thrombocytopenia are not characteristic features.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Wiskott-Aldrich Syndrome (WAS) | X-linked SCID (γc defect) | XLA (BTK defect) | Hyper-IgM Syndrome (CD40L defect) | ADA-SCID |
|----------------------|---------------------------------|---------------------------|-------------------|-----------------------------------|----------|
| Inheritance          | X-linked Recessive              | X-linked Recessive        | X-linked Recessive| X-linked Recessive                | AR      |
| Gene Defect          | *WAS* (WASP protein)            | *IL2RG* (γc)              | *BTK*             | *CD40LG*                          | *ADA*    |
| Key Clinical Features| Eczema, Thrombocytopenia (small platelets), Recurrent Infections, Autoimmunity, Malignancy | Severe Infections (early), Failure to Thrive | Recurrent Bacterial Infections (after 6mo) | Recurrent Infections (opportunistic), Neutropenia | Severe Infections (early), Failure to Thrive |
| Ig Levels            | Low IgM, Low IgA, Normal IgG, High IgE | Very Low All Igs        | Very Low All Igs | High IgM, Low IgG/IgA/IgE        | Very Low All Igs |
| Lymphocyte Count     | Normal or slightly low          | Very Low                 | Normal            | Normal                            | Very Low |
| Platelets            | Low, Small                      | Normal                   | Normal            | Normal                            | Normal  |
| CD43 Expression      | Absent                          | Normal                   | Normal            | Normal                            | Normal  |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome is characterized by the triad of eczema, thrombocytopenia (with small platelets), and recurrent infections, along with low IgM/IgA, normal IgG, high IgE, and absent CD43 expression. It is caused by mutations in the *WAS* gene. Differentiate WAS from other primary immunodeficiencies like X-SCID (severe early infections, low all Igs), XLA (low all Igs, no B cells), Hyper-IgM (high IgM, low other Igs), and ADA-SCID (severe early infections, low all Igs) based on clinical presentation, Ig levels, platelet count/size, and specific markers like CD43.
