<a id='question-145'></a>

### Question 145: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 45 (causes)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of primary immunodeficiencies, specifically focusing on the genetic defects leading to impaired T-cell development and function, particularly DiGeorge syndrome (22q11.2 deletion syndrome) and its associated immunological consequences, including susceptibility to specific infections and potential autoimmune phenomena. (本题考察原发性免疫缺陷，特别是导致T细胞发育和功能障碍的遗传缺陷，如DiGeorge综合征（22号染色体11.2区缺失），以及其相关的免疫学后果，包括对特定感染的易感性和潜在的自身免疫现象。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with recurrent sinopulmonary infections, including pneumonia and otitis media, starting in infancy. He has a history of failure to thrive and developmental delay. Physical examination reveals characteristic facial features including hypertelorism, a short philtrum, and a low-set mouth. Cardiac evaluation reveals conotruncal heart defects, specifically Tetralogy of Fallot. Immunological workup shows markedly reduced numbers of peripheral T lymphocytes (CD3+ cells) and absent T-cell mediated delayed-type hypersensitivity (DTH) responses to common antigens like Candida albicans. B-cell numbers (CD19+ cells) are normal, but immunoglobulin levels (IgG, IgA, IgM) are low, particularly IgA. He is being treated for chronic *Candida* esophagitis refractory to standard antifungal therapy. Genetic testing confirms a heterozygous deletion on chromosome 22q11.2.

Which of the following is the MOST likely underlying immunological defect responsible for this patient's susceptibility to chronic *Candida* infection?

(A) Defective production of mannose-binding lectin (MBL) leading to impaired complement activation.
(B) Impaired T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling due to mutations in the ZAP-70 gene.
(C) Defective development of the thymus gland due to abnormal migration of neural crest cells.
(D) Deficient expression of CD40 ligand (CD40L) on T cells, impairing B-cell class switching.
(E) Defective phagocyte NADPH oxidase activity leading to impaired intracellular killing of catalase-positive organisms.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Recurrent sinopulmonary infections (pneumonia, otitis media) since infancy:** Suggests a primary immunodeficiency affecting cellular immunity (T-cells) or humoral immunity (B-cells/antibodies), or both.
*   **Failure to thrive, developmental delay:** Common features in severe primary immunodeficiencies.
*   **Characteristic facial features (hypertelorism, short philtrum, low-set mouth):** Classic dysmorphic features associated with DiGeorge syndrome.
*   **Conotruncal heart defects (Tetralogy of Fallot):** Strongly associated with DiGeorge syndrome.
*   **Markedly reduced peripheral T lymphocytes (CD3+ cells):** Indicates a severe T-cell deficiency.
*   **Absent DTH responses:** Confirms impaired T-cell mediated immunity.
*   **Normal B-cell numbers (CD19+), low immunoglobulins (especially IgA):** Suggests T-cell help is required for normal B-cell function and antibody production, consistent with a T-cell defect.
*   **Chronic *Candida* esophagitis refractory to standard therapy:** *Candida* is an opportunistic fungus. Chronic, severe infections suggest a profound defect in cell-mediated immunity, which is crucial for controlling fungal infections.
*   **Genetic testing confirms 22q11.2 deletion:** Confirms the diagnosis of DiGeorge syndrome (also known as 22q11.2 deletion syndrome or velocardiofacial syndrome).

The combination of characteristic facial features, conotruncal heart defects, T-cell deficiency, and 22q11.2 deletion points definitively to DiGeorge syndrome. The underlying cause of the T-cell deficiency in DiGeorge syndrome is the failure of the thymus gland to develop properly.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **DiGeorge Syndrome (22q11.2 deletion syndrome):** Caused by a microdeletion on chromosome 22q11.2, affecting multiple genes, including *TBX1*.
*   **Pathogenesis:** The *TBX1* gene is crucial for the development of the third and fourth pharyngeal pouches during embryogenesis. These pouches give rise to the thymus and parathyroid glands. Failure of proper development leads to thymic hypoplasia or aplasia, resulting in impaired T-cell maturation and selection. Neural crest cell migration defects also contribute to cardiac outflow tract abnormalities (conotruncal defects) and characteristic facial features.
*   **Immunological Consequences:** Severe reduction in T-cell numbers (CD3+, CD4+, CD8+), impaired T-cell function (e.g., DTH), leading to susceptibility to opportunistic infections (fungi like *Candida*, viruses). B-cell numbers may be normal, but antibody production is often impaired due to lack of T-cell help, especially for cell-mediated antigens.
*   ***Candida* Infection:** *Candida* species are opportunistic fungi. Cell-mediated immunity (T-cells, particularly Th1 and Th17 responses) is critical for controlling *Candida* infections, especially disseminated or chronic forms. Defects in T-cell function, as seen in DiGeorge syndrome, lead to increased susceptibility to severe and refractory *Candida* infections.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective production of mannose-binding lectin (MBL) leading to impaired complement activation:** MBL deficiency is a primary immunodeficiency affecting the lectin pathway of complement. It primarily increases susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria, not typically severe fungal infections like chronic *Candida* esophagitis. The vignette describes a T-cell defect, not a complement defect.
- (B) **Impaired T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling due to mutations in the ZAP-70 gene:** ZAP-70 is a tyrosine kinase essential for TCR signaling. Mutations cause X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA), which affects B-cell development, leading to absent B cells and immunoglobulins. This patient has normal B-cell numbers, ruling out XLA. The primary defect here is T-cell development, not TCR signaling *per se* (although T-cell development is impaired).
- (D) **Deficient expression of CD40 ligand (CD40L) on T cells, impairing B-cell class switching:** CD40L deficiency (X-linked hyper-IgM syndrome) prevents T-cells from providing help to B-cells for class switching and affinity maturation. Patients have normal or elevated IgM but low IgG, IgA, and IgE. While this patient has low IgA, the primary defect is the profound T-cell deficiency, not specifically CD40L. Also, B-cell numbers are typically normal in CD40L deficiency, but the T-cell defect is the central issue in this case.
- (E) **Defective phagocyte NADPH oxidase activity leading to impaired intracellular killing of catalase-positive organisms:** This describes Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD). CGD affects phagocytes (neutrophils, macrophages), impairing their ability to kill certain bacteria and fungi (especially catalase-positive ones like *Staphylococcus aureus*, *Aspergillus*, *Serratia*, *Burkholderia cepacia*). While CGD patients can get fungal infections, the primary defect is in phagocytes, not T-cells, and the clinical presentation (facial features, heart defects, T-cell numbers) does not fit CGD.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | DiGeorge Syndrome (22q11.2 deletion) | MBL Deficiency | ZAP-70 Deficiency (XLA) | CD40L Deficiency (Hyper-IgM) | CGD (NADPH Oxidase Defect) |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :------------- | :---------------------- | :--------------------------- | :------------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | Thymic hypoplasia/aplasia (T-cell)   | Complement     | B-cell development      | T-cell help to B-cells       | Phagocyte function         |
| **Key Cell Type Affected** | T-cells (CD3+)                      | N/A            | B-cells (CD19+)         | T-cells (CD40L), B-cells     | Phagocytes (Neutrophils)   |
| **Typical Infections**  | Fungal (*Candida*), Viral            | Encapsulated bacteria | Encapsulated bacteria | Opportunistic (Pneumocystis) | Catalase+ bacteria/fungi |
| **Immunoglobulins**     | Low IgG, IgA, IgM (due to lack T-help) | Normal         | Absent all Ig classes   | High IgM, Low IgG, IgA, IgE   | Normal                    |
| **Other Features**      | Facial dysmorphism, Heart defects    | None           | None                   | None                        | Granuloma formation        |
| **Genetic Basis**       | 22q11.2 deletion (*TBX1*)           | MBL2 gene      | ZAP70 (X-linked)        | CD40LG (X-linked)            | Various (e.g., CYBB)       |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
DiGeorge syndrome (22q11.2 deletion) is characterized by thymic hypoplasia leading to T-cell deficiency, resulting in susceptibility to opportunistic infections like *Candida*, along with characteristic facial features and conotruncal heart defects. Differentiating it from other primary immunodeficiencies requires recognizing the specific constellation of clinical findings and immunological abnormalities. Key differentials include other T-cell deficiencies (e.g., SCID), B-cell deficiencies (e.g., XLA, CVID), phagocyte defects (e.g., CGD), and complement deficiencies.

<a id='question-146'></a>

### Question 146: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 46 (Decreased)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of primary immunodeficiencies characterized by decreased numbers or function of specific immune cells, specifically focusing on the clinical manifestations and underlying defect in **Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID)**, particularly the common **X-linked SCID (γc chain deficiency)**, and differentiating it from other immunodeficiencies presenting with similar features like opportunistic infections and failure to thrive. (本题考察原发性免疫缺陷病，特别是细胞数量或功能减少型，重点是**重症联合免疫缺陷症 (SCID)**，尤其是常见的**X 连锁 SCID (γc 链缺陷)**，以及区分其与具有相似特征的其他免疫缺陷病，如机会性感染和生长迟缓。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-month-old male infant presents with recurrent severe infections, including *Pneumocystis jirovecii* pneumonia, persistent oral candidiasis, and failure to thrive. He has never received any vaccinations. Family history reveals that his maternal uncle died in infancy from unknown causes. Physical examination shows lymphopenia and hepatosplenomegaly. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes reveals markedly decreased numbers of T cells (CD3+) and natural killer (NK) cells (CD16+/CD56+), while B cell numbers (CD19+) are normal or slightly increased. The infant is being considered for hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (HSCT). Which of the following molecular defects is most likely responsible for this patient's immunodeficiency?

(A) Defective gene encoding the common gamma chain (γc) subunit shared by multiple cytokine receptors.
(B) Defective gene encoding the enzyme adenosine deaminase (ADA).
(C) Defective gene encoding the Bruton tyrosine kinase (BTK).
(D) Defective gene encoding the CD40 ligand (CD40L).
(E) Defective gene encoding the RAG1 or RAG2 recombinase enzymes.

#### Correct Answer: (A)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Age and Sex:** 6-month-old male. This age is typical for the onset of symptoms in severe primary immunodeficiencies, as maternal antibodies wane. Male sex is relevant for X-linked disorders.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Recurrent severe infections (*Pneumocystis jirovecii* pneumonia, oral candidiasis), failure to thrive. These are hallmark signs of profound T-cell deficiency, leading to susceptibility to opportunistic pathogens.
- **Vaccination History:** Never received vaccinations. This is common in infants with severe immunodeficiency who are too ill or have contraindications.
- **Family History:** Maternal uncle died in infancy. Suggests a possible X-linked inheritance pattern.
- **Physical Examination:** Lymphopenia, hepatosplenomegaly. Lymphopenia indicates a lack of lymphocytes. Hepatosplenomegaly can be seen in various infections and immune disorders.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Markedly decreased T cells (CD3+) and NK cells (CD16+/CD56+), normal/increased B cells (CD19+). This specific pattern is crucial. The absence of T cells points to a T-cell defect. The absence of NK cells narrows down the possibilities significantly. Normal B cells suggest the B-cell lineage is intact, but their function might be impaired due to lack of T-cell help.
- **Proposed Treatment:** HSCT. This is the standard curative treatment for SCID.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the most likely molecular defect.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **SCID Overview:** Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID) is a group of genetic disorders characterized by profound defects in both T-cell and B-cell immunity, often affecting NK cells as well. This leads to extreme susceptibility to infections early in life.
- **Flow Cytometry Interpretation:** The key finding is the absence of T cells (CD3+) and NK cells (CD16+/CD56+). B cells (CD19+) are present. This pattern strongly suggests a defect affecting the development of T cells and NK cells, while sparing the B-cell lineage.
- **X-linked SCID (γc chain deficiency):** This is the most common form of SCID (accounting for ~50% of cases). It is caused by mutations in the *IL2RG* gene, located on the X chromosome. This gene encodes the common gamma chain (γc), a protein subunit shared by the receptors for several key cytokines essential for lymphocyte development and function, including IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, and IL-21.
    - **IL-7 Receptor:** Crucial for T-cell and NK-cell development in the bone marrow and thymus. Deficiency leads to impaired T-cell and NK-cell maturation and survival.
    - **IL-15 Receptor:** Essential for NK-cell development and survival.
    - **B-cell Development:** B-cell development is largely independent of the γc chain (it uses the μ heavy chain for signaling), explaining the presence of B cells in X-linked SCID. However, B-cell function (antibody production) is severely impaired due to the lack of T-cell help (T cells are required for class switching and affinity maturation).
- **Other Forms of SCID and Distractors:**
    - **ADA Deficiency (B):** Causes accumulation of toxic metabolites (deoxyadenosine) that are toxic to lymphocytes, leading to lymphopenia affecting T, B, and NK cells. Flow cytometry typically shows pan-lymphopenia (low T, B, and NK cells), not the specific pattern seen here (normal B cells).
    - **BTK Deficiency (X-linked Agammaglobulinemia, XLA) (C):** Affects B-cell development, leading to a lack of mature B cells (CD19+) and immunoglobulins. T cells and NK cells are normal. This patient has normal B cells.
    - **CD40L Deficiency (D):** Affects T-cell-dependent B-cell activation and isotype switching. T cells are present and functional, but B cells cannot class switch effectively. Patients have normal T cells and NK cells, but low levels of IgG, IgA, IgE, and often normal or high IgM. This patient has absent T cells and NK cells.
    - **RAG1/RAG2 Deficiency (E):** These enzymes are essential for V(D)J recombination, the process that generates diverse T-cell receptors (TCRs) and B-cell receptors (BCRs). Deficiency leads to a lack of mature T cells and B cells (T-B-NK+ SCID). NK cells are typically present. This patient has normal B cells.

##### 3. Linking Clues to the Correct Answer
The combination of male infant, recurrent opportunistic infections, failure to thrive, family history suggestive of X-linked inheritance, lymphopenia, and crucially, the flow cytometry pattern of absent T cells (CD3+) and NK cells (CD16+/CD56+) with normal B cells (CD19+) is pathognomonic for X-linked SCID due to γc chain deficiency. The γc chain is essential for signaling through receptors for IL-7 (T-cell and NK-cell development) and IL-15 (NK-cell development). Its absence prevents the proper development and survival of these lymphocytes.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Defective gene encoding the common gamma chain (γc) subunit shared by multiple cytokine receptors.** This is the correct answer. Mutations in *IL2RG* encoding γc cause X-linked SCID, leading to the characteristic absence of T cells and NK cells due to impaired IL-7 and IL-15 signaling, while B cells are usually present. This perfectly matches the clinical and flow cytometry findings.
- (B): **Defective gene encoding the enzyme adenosine deaminase (ADA).** ADA deficiency causes SCID, but it typically results in pan-lymphopenia (low T, B, and NK cells) due to the toxic accumulation of metabolites. The flow cytometry pattern in this case (normal B cells) is inconsistent with ADA deficiency.
- (C): **Defective gene encoding the Bruton tyrosine kinase (BTK).** BTK deficiency causes X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA), characterized by a failure of B-cell maturation and a lack of B cells and immunoglobulins. T cells and NK cells are normal. This patient has normal B cells and absent T cells/NK cells, ruling out XLA.
- (D): **Defective gene encoding the CD40 ligand (CD40L).** CD40L deficiency causes X-linked hyper-IgM syndrome. T cells are present and functional, but B cells cannot undergo class switching, leading to high IgM and low IgG, IgA, IgE. T cells and NK cells are normal. This patient has absent T cells/NK cells, ruling out CD40L deficiency.
- (E): **Defective gene encoding the RAG1 or RAG2 recombinase enzymes.** RAG1/RAG2 deficiency causes SCID by impairing V(D)J recombination, leading to a lack of both mature T cells and B cells (T-B-NK+ SCID). NK cells are typically present. This patient has normal B cells, ruling out RAG deficiency.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | X-linked SCID (γc deficiency) | ADA Deficiency | XLA (BTK deficiency) | CD40L Deficiency | RAG1/RAG2 Deficiency |
|----------------------|--------------------------------|----------------|----------------------|-------------------|-----------------------|
| Inheritance         | X-linked                       | Autosomal Recessive | X-linked             | X-linked         | Autosomal Recessive  |
| T Cells (CD3+)      | Absent                         | Low            | Normal               | Normal            | Absent                |
| B Cells (CD19+)      | Normal/Increased               | Low            | Absent               | Normal            | Absent                |
| NK Cells (CD16+/56+) | Absent                         | Low            | Normal               | Normal            | Normal                |
| Immunoglobulin Levels| Low (all classes)              | Low            | Very Low (all classes)| High IgM, Low IgG/A/E | Very Low (all classes)|
| Key Defect          | γc subunit of cytokine receptors| ADA enzyme    | BTK enzyme           | CD40L protein     | RAG1/2 enzymes        |
| Typical Infections  | Opportunistic (PJP, Candida)    | Opportunistic | Encapsulated bacteria| Opportunistic     | Opportunistic         |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The specific pattern of absent T cells and NK cells with normal B cells on flow cytometry is highly characteristic of X-linked SCID due to common gamma chain (γc) deficiency. Differentiating SCID subtypes based on flow cytometry findings is crucial for diagnosis. Remember to distinguish this pattern from other SCID forms (e.g., ADA deficiency with pan-lymphopenia, RAG deficiency with T-B-NK+ phenotype) and non-SCID immunodeficiencies affecting specific lineages (e.g., XLA affecting B cells, CD40L deficiency affecting B cell class switching).

<a id='question-147'></a>

### Question 147: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 47 (levels)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of primary immunodeficiencies, specifically focusing on defects in lymphocyte development and maturation, leading to impaired humoral and cell-mediated immunity, and the associated clinical manifestations, including susceptibility to specific infections and autoimmune phenomena. (本题考察原发性免疫缺陷，特别是淋巴细胞发育和成熟缺陷，导致体液和细胞免疫受损，以及相关的临床表现，包括对特定感染的易感性和自身免疫现象。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with recurrent sinopulmonary infections (otitis media, pneumonia, sinusitis) starting at 6 months of age, after maternal antibody protection waned. He has also experienced failure to thrive and chronic diarrhea. Physical examination reveals lymphadenopathy and splenomegaly. Immunological workup shows markedly reduced levels of all immunoglobulin isotypes (IgG, IgA, IgM), absent B cells (CD19+), and severely decreased T cells (CD3+, CD4+, CD8+). A bone marrow biopsy shows markedly reduced numbers of lymphoid precursors. The patient is started on prophylactic antibiotics and receives intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIG) infusions. Despite this, he develops severe graft-versus-host disease (GVHD) following a blood transfusion with non-irradiated blood products. Which of the following cellular defects is most likely responsible for this patient's clinical presentation and complications?

(A) Defective T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling leading to impaired positive selection in the thymus.
(B) Defective B-cell receptor (BCR) signaling leading to impaired negative selection in the bone marrow.
(C) Defective DNA repair mechanism leading to impaired V(D)J recombination and lymphocyte development.
(D) Defective immunoglobulin class switching due to a mutation in activation-induced cytidine deaminase (AID).
(E) Defective CD40 ligand (CD40L) expression on T cells leading to impaired T-dependent B cell activation.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent sinopulmonary infections starting at 6 months:** Suggests a defect in humoral immunity (B cells/antibodies) as maternal IgG wanes.
- **Failure to thrive and chronic diarrhea:** Common manifestations of severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) due to broad susceptibility to infections.
- **Lymphadenopathy and splenomegaly:** Can be seen in various immunodeficiencies, sometimes due to chronic infections or autoimmune phenomena.
- **Markedly reduced levels of all immunoglobulin isotypes (IgG, IgA, IgM):** Indicates a severe defect in B cell function or development.
- **Absent B cells (CD19+):** Points towards a very early defect in B cell lineage development.
- **Severely decreased T cells (CD3+, CD4+, CD8+):** Indicates a defect affecting T cell development as well, suggesting SCID.
- **Bone marrow biopsy shows markedly reduced numbers of lymphoid precursors:** Confirms a severe defect in lymphocyte production originating in the bone marrow.
- **Severe graft-versus-host disease (GVHD) after non-irradiated blood transfusion:** This is a critical clue. GVHD occurs when donor T cells (present in the transfused blood) recognize the recipient's tissues as foreign and attack them. The absence or severe deficiency of functional T cells in the recipient normally prevents GVHD. The development of severe GVHD in this patient implies that the transfused T cells are encountering a severely immunocompromised host *lacking* functional T cells, but also suggests a potential issue with the *donor* T cells or the *recipient's* ability to regulate immune responses, even if severely deficient. However, the primary defect described (absent B and T cells, reduced precursors) points strongly towards SCID. The development of GVHD after transfusion in a patient with SCID is a known complication, often severe, because the recipient lacks the T cells to mount a counter-regulatory response against the donor T cells. This clinical picture strongly suggests a defect affecting both T and B cell development.
- **Differential Diagnosis:** The combination of absent B cells, absent T cells, and reduced lymphoid precursors in the bone marrow is characteristic of Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID). Several genetic defects can cause SCID.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **SCID Overview:** SCID encompasses a group of >50 genetic disorders characterized by profound defects in T cell development, often accompanied by defects in B cell and/or NK cell development. This leads to extreme susceptibility to infections early in life.
- **Lymphocyte Development:** T cells develop in the thymus, while B cells develop in the bone marrow. Both cell types require functional V(D)J recombination for the generation of diverse antigen receptors (TCR and BCR, respectively). This process involves specialized enzymes, including RAG1 and RAG2 (Recombination Activating Genes).
- **V(D)J Recombination:** This is a crucial step in the development of both T and B lymphocytes. It involves the somatic recombination of variable (V), diversity (D), and joining (J) gene segments to create unique antigen receptor genes. This process is initiated by the RAG1/RAG2 enzyme complex, which creates double-strand breaks in the DNA at specific recombination signal sequences (RSSs). These breaks are then processed by other enzymes (Artemis, TdT, DNA ligase IV) to join the gene segments.
- **Defects in V(D)J Recombination:** Mutations in genes encoding RAG1, RAG2, Artemis, DNA ligase IV, or TdT can severely impair or abolish V(D)J recombination. This leads to a block in lymphocyte development at an early stage, resulting in a profound deficiency of both T cells and B cells (T-B- SCID). The bone marrow shows a paucity of lymphoid precursors because they cannot mature.
- **Option (C) - Defective DNA repair mechanism leading to impaired V(D)J recombination and lymphocyte development:** This directly describes the underlying mechanism of RAG-deficient SCID or SCID caused by defects in other V(D)J recombination enzymes (Artemis, Ligase IV, TdT). This defect explains the absence of both T and B cells and the reduced lymphoid precursors in the bone marrow, fitting the clinical picture perfectly. The lack of functional T cells explains the susceptibility to infections and the development of severe GVHD after transfusion.
- **Option (A) - Defective TCR signaling leading to impaired positive selection in the thymus:** Defects in TCR signaling (e.g., ZAP70 deficiency) cause T-B+ SCID. Patients have absent T cells but normal or near-normal B cells. This does not fit the clinical picture of absent B cells.
- **Option (B) - Defective BCR signaling leading to impaired negative selection in the bone marrow:** Defects in BCR signaling (e.g., μ heavy chain deficiency) cause agammaglobulinemia (XLA), characterized by absent B cells and immunoglobulins but normal T cells. This does not fit the clinical picture of absent T cells.
- **Option (D) - Defective immunoglobulin class switching due to a mutation in activation-induced cytidine deaminase (AID):** AID deficiency causes hyper-IgM syndrome. Patients have normal or elevated IgM levels but deficient IgG, IgA, and IgE. T cell numbers are typically normal. This does not fit the clinical picture of absent B cells, absent T cells, and low levels of all immunoglobulin isotypes.
- **Option (E) - Defective CD40 ligand (CD40L) expression on T cells leading to impaired T-dependent B cell activation:** CD40L deficiency (X-linked hyper-IgM syndrome) causes impaired T-dependent B cell activation and class switching. Patients have normal T cell numbers but low IgG, IgA, and IgE, with normal or elevated IgM. This does not fit the clinical picture of absent B cells and absent T cells.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Incorrect. Defects in TCR signaling (like ZAP70 deficiency) cause T-B+ SCID, where T cells are absent or non-functional, but B cells are present (though often non-functional). This patient has absent B cells.
- (B): Incorrect. Defects in BCR signaling (like μ heavy chain deficiency, Bruton's agammaglobulinemia) cause agammaglobulinemia, characterized by absent B cells and immunoglobulins but normal T cells. This patient has absent T cells.
- (D): Incorrect. AID deficiency causes hyper-IgM syndrome, characterized by normal T cells and B cells but impaired class switching, leading to high IgM and low IgG/IgA/IgE. This patient has absent B cells and T cells.
- (E): Incorrect. CD40L deficiency causes X-linked hyper-IgM syndrome, characterized by normal T cells but impaired T-dependent B cell activation and class switching, leading to high IgM and low IgG/IgA/IgE. This patient has absent B cells and T cells.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Correct Answer (C) - V(D)J Defect (e.g., RAG1/2, Artemis) | (A) TCR Signaling Defect (e.g., ZAP70) | (B) BCR Signaling Defect (e.g., BTK) | (D) AID Deficiency | (E) CD40L Deficiency |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------ | :----------------------------------- | :--------------- | :------------------ |
| **T Cells**             | Absent/Severely Reduced                                   | Absent/Severely Reduced               | Normal                               | Normal           | Normal              |
| **B Cells**             | Absent/Severely Reduced                                   | Present (often non-functional)        | Absent                               | Present          | Present             |
| **Immunoglobulins**     | All isotypes severely reduced/absent                      | All isotypes severely reduced/absent  | Absent (except IgM sometimes)        | High IgM, Low IgG/A/E | High IgM, Low IgG/A/E |
| **Lymphoid Precursors** | Markedly Reduced in BM                                    | Normal/Reduced                        | Normal                               | Normal           | Normal              |
| **Mechanism**           | Impaired V(D)J recombination                               | Impaired TCR signaling/selection      | Impaired BCR signaling/maturation    | Impaired Class Switch | Impaired T-B help   |
| **Clinical Picture**    | Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID)                   | T-B+ SCID                             | Agammaglobulinemia (XLA)             | Hyper-IgM        | Hyper-IgM           |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The clinical presentation of absent T cells, absent B cells, and severely reduced lymphoid precursors points strongly to Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID) caused by a defect in early lymphocyte development, specifically V(D)J recombination. Remember to differentiate SCID subtypes (T-B-, T-B+, T-B+ NK-) based on lymphocyte subset analysis. Key differentials include agammaglobulinemia (normal T cells, absent B cells), hyper-IgM syndromes (normal T cells, B cells present but dysfunctional), and other forms of SCID affecting different pathways (e.g., IL-2 receptor gamma chain deficiency).

<a id='question-148'></a>

### Question 148: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 48 (normal)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Differentiating primary immunodeficiencies based on specific clinical manifestations, particularly recurrent infections with specific pathogens and associated laboratory findings, focusing on defects in T-cell function and cytokine signaling. (基于特定临床表现和实验室发现区分原发性免疫缺陷，特别是T细胞功能和细胞因子信号传导缺陷。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with recurrent episodes of severe, non-bloody diarrhea, failure to thrive, and chronic cough. He has had multiple hospitalizations for pneumonia, including one episode requiring mechanical ventilation. His infections are often caused by *Pneumocystis jirovecii*, *Staphylococcus aureus*, and various fungi like *Candida albicans*. He has never received live vaccines due to concerns about potential complications. Physical examination reveals a cachectic child with oral thrush and diffuse rhonchi. Laboratory tests show lymphopenia (absolute lymphocyte count 500/µL), markedly reduced T-cell numbers (CD3+ T cells 100/µL), and normal B-cell numbers (CD19+ B cells 800/µL). Flow cytometry reveals absent expression of CD3, CD4, and CD8 on T cells, but normal expression of CD19 on B cells. Genetic testing confirms a homozygous mutation in the *JAK3* gene.

Which of the following immunological defects is most likely responsible for this patient's clinical presentation?

(A) Defective T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling due to mutations in the *ZAP70* gene.
(B) Impaired B-cell development and antibody production due to mutations in the *BTK* gene.
(C) Defective cytokine receptor signaling affecting multiple hematopoietic lineages due to mutations in the *JAK3* gene.
(D) Defective immunoglobulin class switching and somatic hypermutation due to mutations in the *AID* gene.
(E) Impaired T-cell maturation and selection due to mutations in the *RAG1* or *RAG2* gene.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 3-year-old boy. Early onset suggests a primary immunodeficiency.
- **Clinical Manifestations:** Recurrent severe diarrhea, failure to thrive, chronic cough, recurrent pneumonia (including *Pneumocystis jirovecii*), oral thrush (*Candida*). These point towards a severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) or a severe T-cell defect, as these pathogens are typically controlled by cell-mediated immunity.
- **Immunological Status:** Never received live vaccines (contraindicated in severe T-cell defects).
- **Laboratory Findings:** Lymphopenia, markedly reduced T-cell numbers (CD3+, CD4+, CD8+), normal B-cell numbers (CD19+). This pattern strongly suggests a T-cell defect, potentially SCID.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Absent CD3, CD4, CD8 expression on T cells, normal CD19 on B cells. Confirms severe T-cell deficiency.
- **Genetic Testing:** Homozygous mutation in the *JAK3* gene. This is the definitive clue linking the clinical and immunological findings to a specific molecular defect.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **JAK3 Function:** Janus kinase 3 (JAK3) is a tyrosine kinase crucial for signal transduction downstream of several cytokine receptors, including the common gamma chain (γc), which is shared by receptors for IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, and IL-21.
- **IL-7 Receptor Signaling:** IL-7 is essential for T-cell development and survival. The IL-7 receptor uses the γc. Defective JAK3 signaling disrupts IL-7 receptor signaling, leading to impaired T-cell development in the thymus and subsequent severe T-cell lymphopenia.
- **IL-15 Receptor Signaling:** IL-15 is crucial for the survival and homeostasis of memory T cells and NK cells. The IL-15 receptor also uses the γc. Defective JAK3 signaling impairs IL-15 receptor signaling, contributing to T-cell lymphopenia and potentially affecting NK cell function (though NK cells are often relatively preserved in JAK3 deficiency compared to other SCID forms).
- **X-linked SCID (X-SCID):** Mutations in the *JAK3* gene cause X-linked SCID, the most common form of SCID. The γc is encoded on the X chromosome.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's presentation (recurrent opportunistic infections like *Pneumocystis*, *Candida*, *Staphylococcus*, failure to thrive) and laboratory findings (severe T-cell lymphopenia, normal B-cell numbers) are classic for X-linked SCID due to *JAK3* mutations. The absence of T-cell surface markers (CD3, CD4, CD8) reflects the failure of T-cell development. B-cells are present but non-functional due to lack of T-cell help.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling due to mutations in the *ZAP70* gene:** *ZAP70* is a tyrosine kinase involved in TCR signaling. Mutations cause T-cell lymphopenia and SCID, but typically *do not* affect B-cell development or NK cell function. The patient has normal B-cell numbers, making this less likely than JAK3 deficiency, which affects multiple cytokine pathways. Also, the genetic test specifically identified a *JAK3* mutation.
- (B) **Impaired B-cell development and antibody production due to mutations in the *BTK* gene:** *BTK* (Bruton's tyrosine kinase) is essential for B-cell development. Mutations cause X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA), characterized by absent B cells and immunoglobulins. This patient has normal B-cell numbers, ruling out XLA.
- (D) **Defective immunoglobulin class switching and somatic hypermutation due to mutations in the *AID* gene:** *AID* (Activation-Induced Cytidine Deaminase) is required for class switch recombination and somatic hypermutation in B cells. Mutations cause Hyper-IgM syndrome, characterized by normal or elevated IgM levels but low levels of other isotypes (IgG, IgA, IgE). T-cell function is typically normal. This patient has severe T-cell deficiency, ruling out AID deficiency.
- (E) **Impaired T-cell maturation and selection due to mutations in the *RAG1* or *RAG2* gene:** *RAG1* and *RAG2* are essential for V(D)J recombination, the process that generates T-cell receptors (TCRs) and B-cell receptors (BCRs). Mutations cause SCID characterized by a lack of both mature T cells and B cells (T-B-NK+ SCID). This patient has normal B-cell numbers, ruling out RAG deficiency.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | JAK3 Deficiency (X-SCID) | ZAP70 Deficiency (SCID) | BTK Deficiency (XLA) | AID Deficiency (Hyper-IgM) | RAG1/RAG2 Deficiency (SCID) |
|-------------------------|--------------------------|-------------------------|----------------------|---------------------------|-----------------------------|
| **Primary Defect**      | JAK3 Kinase              | ZAP70 Kinase            | BTK Kinase           | AID Enzyme                | RAG1/RAG2 Enzymes           |
| **Affected Cells**      | T cells, NK cells (mild)  | T cells                 | B cells              | B cells                  | T cells, B cells            |
| **T Cells**             | Absent/Severe ↓          | Absent/Severe ↓         | Normal               | Normal                    | Absent/Severe ↓             |
| **B Cells**             | Normal                   | Normal                  | Absent               | Normal                    | Absent/Severe ↓             |
| **NK Cells**            | Normal/Mild ↓            | Normal                  | Normal               | Normal                    | Normal                      |
| **Immunoglobulins**     | Low all isotypes         | Low all isotypes        | Low all isotypes     | High IgM, Low IgG/IgA/IgE  | Low all isotypes            |
| **Key Cytokine Pathway**| IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, IL-21 | TCR Signaling          | B-cell Signaling     | Class Switch/Somatic Hypermutation | V(D)J Recombination         |
| **Genetic Inheritance** | X-linked                 | Autosomal Recessive      | X-linked             | Autosomal Recessive        | Autosomal Recessive          |
| **Typical Infections**  | *Pneumocystis*, Viruses, Fungi | *Pneumocystis*, Viruses | Encapsulated Bacteria | Opportunistic Infections | Opportunistic Infections |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The clinical presentation of severe recurrent infections, particularly opportunistic ones like *Pneumocystis jirovecii*, combined with profound T-cell lymphopenia but normal B-cell numbers, points towards a severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) primarily affecting T-cell development. Mutations in *JAK3* cause X-linked SCID by disrupting signaling downstream of multiple cytokine receptors crucial for T-cell (IL-7, IL-15) and NK cell development. Key differentials include other SCID forms (ZAP70, RAG1/2, ADA, Artemis) which may have different cell lineage involvement, and non-SCID T-cell defects like DiGeorge syndrome (thymic hypoplasia). Remember that JAK3 deficiency specifically affects cytokine signaling pathways involving the common gamma chain.

<a id='question-149'></a>

### Question 149: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 49 (elevation)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the molecular basis and clinical manifestations of Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome (WAS), a primary immunodeficiency characterized by eczema, thrombocytopenia (small platelets), recurrent infections, and an increased risk of autoimmunity and malignancy, due to mutations in the *WAS* gene encoding the WAS protein (WASP), which is crucial for actin cytoskeleton rearrangement in hematopoietic cells. (本题考察Wiskott-Aldrich综合征（WAS）的分子基础和临床表现，这是一种原发性免疫缺陷，其特征是湿疹、血小板减少症（小血小板）、复发性感染以及自身免疫和恶性肿瘤的风险增加，这是由于*WAS*基因突变引起的，该基因编码WASP蛋白，WASP蛋白对于造血细胞中肌动蛋白细胞骨架的重排至关重要。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with a history of recurrent severe eczema since infancy, frequent bacterial infections (otitis media, pneumonia), and easy bruising. His mother reports that he has had multiple hospitalizations for bleeding episodes. Physical examination reveals widespread eczematous lesions, petechiae, and purpura. Laboratory studies show thrombocytopenia with characteristically small platelets (mean platelet volume < 7 fL). Immunological testing reveals low IgM levels, normal IgG levels, and normal T-cell numbers but impaired T-cell function, specifically defective responses to polysaccharide antigens. A bone marrow biopsy shows normal cellularity but decreased megakaryocytes. Genetic testing confirms a mutation in the *WAS* gene located on the X chromosome. Which of the following molecular defects is most directly responsible for the impaired T-cell function and thrombocytopenia observed in this patient?

(A) Defective CD40 ligand (CD40L) expression on T cells, leading to impaired B-cell isotype switching and T-cell interaction with antigen-presenting cells.
(B) Defective WAS protein (WASP) expression, leading to impaired actin polymerization and cytoskeletal rearrangement in hematopoietic cells.
(C) Defective Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK) expression, leading to impaired B-cell development and maturation.
(D) Defective Janus kinase 3 (JAK3) expression, leading to impaired signaling downstream of cytokine receptors, particularly the common gamma chain (γc).
(E) Defective adenosine deaminase (ADA) expression, leading to the accumulation of toxic metabolites (deoxyadenosine) that impair lymphocyte development.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **3-year-old boy:** Age is consistent with the typical onset of Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome (WAS).
- **Recurrent severe eczema:** A classic triad feature of WAS.
- **Frequent bacterial infections:** Due to impaired humoral and cellular immunity.
- **Easy bruising, petechiae, purpura:** Suggests a platelet disorder.
- **Thrombocytopenia with small platelets:** A hallmark feature of WAS, distinguishing it from other thrombocytopenias.
- **Low IgM, normal IgG:** Indicates impaired antibody response, particularly to polysaccharide antigens, consistent with defective T-cell help for B cells.
- **Normal T-cell numbers, impaired T-cell function:** Suggests a functional defect rather than a numerical deficiency.
- **Mutation in the *WAS* gene:** Confirms the diagnosis of WAS.
- **Question asks for the molecular defect:** Requires linking the clinical/lab findings to the underlying molecular pathology of WAS.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Wiskott-Aldrich Syndrome (WAS):** An X-linked recessive primary immunodeficiency caused by mutations in the *WAS* gene.
- ***WAS* gene:** Encodes the WAS protein (WASP), a cytoplasmic protein expressed in hematopoietic cells.
- **WASP function:** WASP is crucial for actin cytoskeleton organization and signaling pathways downstream of various receptors, including Fc receptors, T-cell receptor (TCR), B-cell receptor (BCR), and cytokine receptors. It interacts with WAVE/Scar proteins and Arp2/3 complex to regulate actin polymerization.
- **Pathophysiology:** Defective WASP leads to impaired actin-based motility and signaling in lymphocytes (T cells, B cells, NK cells), platelets, and other hematopoietic cells.
    - **T cells:** Impaired T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling, defective migration to sites of inflammation, impaired synapse formation, and reduced proliferation/cytokine production, particularly affecting responses to polysaccharide antigens (T-cell independent B cell activation).
    - **B cells:** Impaired B-cell receptor (BCR) signaling and T-cell help, leading to low IgM and impaired antibody responses.
    - **Platelets:** Defective actin cytoskeleton organization impairs platelet formation (leading to small platelets) and function (leading to thrombocytopenia and bleeding diathesis).
- **Clinical Triad:** Eczema, thrombocytopenia (with small platelets), and recurrent infections.
- **Other features:** Autoimmunity (e.g., autoimmune hemolytic anemia, nephrotic syndrome), increased risk of malignancy (lymphoma, leukemia).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective CD40 ligand (CD40L) expression:** This causes X-linked Hyper-IgM syndrome. Patients have normal T-cell numbers but impaired T-cell help to B cells, leading to low IgG, IgA, IgE, and normal or high IgM. They also have increased susceptibility to opportunistic infections (e.g., *Pneumocystis jirovecii*). This does not explain the thrombocytopenia or small platelets.
- (C) **Defective Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK) expression:** This causes X-linked agammaglobulinemia (XLA). Patients have absent B cells and very low levels of all immunoglobulin isotypes. T-cell function is normal. This does not explain the thrombocytopenia, eczema, or T-cell dysfunction.
- (D) **Defective Janus kinase 3 (JAK3) expression:** This causes X-linked severe combined immunodeficiency (X-SCID). JAK3 is essential for signaling downstream of the common gamma chain (γc) receptors used by IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, and IL-21. Patients have severely impaired T-cell and NK-cell development and function, leading to profound susceptibility to infections early in life. B-cell numbers may be normal but function is impaired due to lack of T-cell help. This does not explain the thrombocytopenia or small platelets.
- (E) **Defective adenosine deaminase (ADA) expression:** This causes autosomal recessive severe combined immunodeficiency (ADA-SCID). ADA deficiency leads to the accumulation of toxic metabolites (deoxyadenosine and deoxyATP) that are particularly toxic to lymphocytes, causing severe lymphopenia (T, B, and NK cells) and profound immunodeficiency. This does not explain the thrombocytopenia or small platelets.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Wiskott-Aldrich Syndrome (WAS) | X-linked Hyper-IgM (CD40L def.) | XLA (BTK def.) | X-SCID (JAK3 def.) | ADA-SCID |
|----------------------|---------------------------------|-----------------------------------|-----------------|----------------------|----------|
| Inheritance          | X-linked Recessive              | X-linked Recessive                | X-linked Recessive | X-linked Recessive  | Autosomal Recessive |
| Gene Defect          | *WAS* (WASP)                    | *CD40LG* (CD40L)                  | *BTK*           | *JAK3*               | *ADA*    |
| Primary Cell Type    | T cells, B cells, Platelets     | T cells, B cells                  | B cells         | T cells, NK cells    | Lymphocytes (T, B, NK) |
| Immunoglobulins      | Low IgM, Normal IgG             | Normal/High IgM, Low IgG/IgA/IgE  | Very Low All    | Low All (due to lack of T help) | Very Low All |
| T Cell Function      | Impaired (esp. polysac response) | Impaired Help                     | Normal          | Severely Impaired    | Severely Impaired |
| B Cell Numbers       | Normal                          | Normal                            | Absent          | Normal               | Low      |
| Platelets            | Thrombocytopenia, Small         | Normal                            | Normal          | Normal               | Normal   |
| Eczema               | Yes                             | No                                | No              | No                   | No       |
| Key Clinical Feature | Thrombocytopenia (small plts)   | Opportunistic Infections          | Recurrent Bacterial Infections | Severe Infections Early | Severe Infections Early |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome is characterized by the triad of eczema, thrombocytopenia (with small platelets), and recurrent infections due to mutations in the *WAS* gene, leading to defective WASP protein and impaired actin cytoskeleton function in hematopoietic cells. Differentiating WAS from other primary immunodeficiencies like X-linked Hyper-IgM, XLA, X-SCID, and ADA-SCID requires careful attention to the specific immunoglobulin patterns, cell lineage involvement, and unique clinical features like small platelets and eczema.

<a id='question-150'></a>

### Question 150: Primary Immunodeficiencies: Part 3 Focus 50 (Repeated)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the molecular defect and resulting immunological phenotype in Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome (WAS), a primary immunodeficiency characterized by eczema, thrombocytopenia, and recurrent infections, due to mutations in the *WAS* gene encoding the WAS protein (WASP), which is crucial for actin cytoskeleton rearrangement in hematopoietic cells. (本题考察Wiskott-Aldrich综合征（WAS）的分子缺陷及其免疫学表型，这是一种原发性免疫缺陷病，特征为湿疹、血小板减少症和复发性感染，这是由于 *WAS* 基因突变引起的，该基因编码WASP蛋白，WASP蛋白对于造血细胞中肌动蛋白细胞骨架的重排至关重要。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents with a history of severe, persistent eczema since infancy, frequent bloody diarrhea, and recurrent otitis media and pneumonia. He has a petechial rash and easy bruising. His mother reports that he has had difficulty gaining weight despite a good appetite. Laboratory findings show thrombocytopenia (platelet count 20,000/µL) with small platelets, low serum IgM levels, and normal or elevated IgA and IgG levels. Lymphocyte proliferation assays in response to mitogens (e.g., PHA, ConA) are markedly reduced. A bone marrow biopsy reveals normal cellularity but shows impaired T-cell and platelet morphology. Genetic testing confirms a mutation in the *WAS* gene on the X chromosome.

Which of the following cellular processes is most directly impaired in this patient's T lymphocytes, leading to the observed immunological defects?

(A) CD3-mediated T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling complex formation.
(B) Actin polymerization and cytoskeletal rearrangement required for immunological synapse formation and cell migration.
(C) V(D)J recombination of immunoglobulin heavy chain genes.
(D) Complement receptor-mediated phagocytosis of opsonized bacteria.
(E) MHC class II antigen processing and presentation by dendritic cells.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 3-year-old boy. X-linked inheritance pattern is suggested by the patient's sex and the condition's known genetics.
- **Clinical Triad:** Eczema (severe, persistent), Thrombocytopenia (small platelets, petechiae, bruising, bloody diarrhea), Recurrent Infections (otitis media, pneumonia). This triad is classic for Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome (WAS).
- **Laboratory Findings:** Thrombocytopenia with small platelets (microthrombocytopenia), low IgM, normal/high IgA/IgG. This specific immunoglobulin profile is characteristic of WAS.
- **Lymphocyte Function:** Reduced proliferation to mitogens indicates impaired T-cell function.
- **Bone Marrow:** Normal cellularity but impaired T-cell/platelet morphology suggests a functional defect rather than a production problem.
- **Genetic Confirmation:** Mutation in the *WAS* gene confirms the diagnosis.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *cellular process* impaired in T lymphocytes due to the *WAS* gene mutation.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Wiskott-Aldrich Syndrome (WAS):** An X-linked recessive primary immunodeficiency caused by mutations in the *WAS* gene.
- **WAS Protein (WASP):** A cytoplasmic protein expressed in hematopoietic cells (T cells, B cells, platelets, dendritic cells, etc.). It acts as a crucial adaptor protein and regulator of the actin cytoskeleton.
- **Function of WASP:** WASP interacts with various signaling molecules and cytoskeletal components (like actin, Arp2/3 complex, N-WASP) to regulate actin polymerization, cell motility, cell adhesion, and signal transduction.
- **Role in T Cells:** In T cells, WASP is essential for:
    - **Immunological Synapse Formation:** Proper actin rearrangement is required for the T cell receptor (TCR) complex to cluster at the immunological synapse upon antigen recognition, facilitating signal transduction.
    - **Cell Migration:** WASP is needed for T cells to migrate to sites of infection or inflammation.
    - **Cytokine Production:** WASP influences cytokine production by T cells.
- **Role in Platelets:** WASP is critical for platelet formation and function, including adhesion, aggregation, and pseudopod formation. Defects lead to microthrombocytopenia (small platelets) and bleeding diathesis.
- **Immunological Consequences of WASP Deficiency:**
    - **Impaired T-cell Function:** Reduced T-cell proliferation, impaired cytokine production (especially IL-2), defective cytotoxic T lymphocyte (CTL) function, and impaired T-cell migration. This leads to susceptibility to opportunistic infections (viral, bacterial, fungal).
    - **Abnormal B-cell Function:** Defective T-cell help leads to impaired class switching and antibody production, particularly affecting IgM production (low IgM) while IgG and IgA/IgE may be normal or elevated.
    - **Increased Risk of Autoimmunity and Malignancy:** Dysregulated immune responses can lead to autoimmune phenomena (e.g., autoimmune hemolytic anemia, autoimmune thrombocytopenia) and an increased risk of lymphoma.
- **Molecular Mechanism:** The *WAS* gene mutation leads to a non-functional or absent WASP protein. This disrupts the normal regulation of the actin cytoskeleton in hematopoietic cells. Specifically, WASP is required for the activation of the Arp2/3 complex, which nucleates actin polymerization, leading to branching of the actin network. This process is essential for membrane ruffling, cell motility, and the formation of microvilli and podosomes, all critical for immune cell function.

##### 3. Linking WASP Deficiency to the Options:
- The core defect in WAS is the absence or dysfunction of WASP, which directly impacts actin cytoskeleton dynamics.
- Option (B) directly addresses this core defect: "Actin polymerization and cytoskeletal rearrangement required for immunological synapse formation and cell migration." This is the fundamental process disrupted by the lack of WASP in T cells, explaining the impaired proliferation, migration, and synapse formation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) CD3-mediated TCR signaling complex formation: While TCR signaling is ultimately impaired in WAS due to defective synapse formation, the primary defect isn't in the initial assembly of the CD3 complex itself. The problem lies downstream in the cytoskeletal rearrangements needed for efficient signaling and synapse stabilization. WASP interacts with CD3ζ but its main role is cytoskeletal regulation.
- (C) V(D)J recombination of immunoglobulin heavy chain genes: This process occurs in developing B cells in the bone marrow and is responsible for generating antibody diversity. It is not directly affected by WASP deficiency, which primarily impacts mature hematopoietic cells' function and migration. Defects in V(D)J recombination cause other primary immunodeficiencies like X-linked agammaglobulinemia (BTK deficiency) or RAG deficiency.
- (D) Complement receptor-mediated phagocytosis of opsonized bacteria: This is a function primarily carried out by phagocytes (macrophages, neutrophils) using complement receptors (e.g., CR1, CR3). While T cells can interact with phagocytes, phagocytosis itself is not a primary function of T cells, and WASP deficiency doesn't directly impair complement receptors or the phagocytic machinery.
- (E) MHC class II antigen processing and presentation by dendritic cells: This process is crucial for initiating T-cell responses. While dendritic cells also express WASP and their function can be affected, the question specifically asks about the process impaired in *T lymphocytes*. Furthermore, the primary defect in WAS is not in antigen processing or MHC presentation itself, but rather in the T cell's ability to respond to the presented antigen due to cytoskeletal defects.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Wiskott-Aldrich Syndrome (WAS) | X-linked Agammaglobulinemia (XLA) | Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID) | Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD) |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------- |
| **Gene Defect**         | *WAS* (WASP)                   | *BTK*                             | Various (e.g., *IL2RG*, *RAG1/2*, *ADA*) | *CYBB* (gp91phox) or other NADPH oxidase subunits |
| **Primary Cell Affected** | T cells, B cells, Platelets     | B cells                           | T cells, B cells, NK cells               | Phagocytes (Neutrophils, Macrophages) |
| **Key Immunological Defect** | Impaired actin cytoskeleton, low IgM, defective T cell synapse | Absent B cells, absent antibodies       | Absent T cells (variable B/NK)          | Defective oxidative burst             |
| **Clinical Triad/Features** | Eczema, Thrombocytopenia (small platelets), Recurrent Infections | Recurrent bacterial infections (encapsulated organisms) | Severe recurrent infections (opportunistic) | Recurrent bacterial/fungal infections, granulomas |
| **Actin Cytoskeleton Role** | Critical                       | Not directly involved             | Not directly involved                   | Not directly involved                |
| **Ig Levels**           | Low IgM, Normal/High IgA/IgG    | Very low IgG, IgA, IgM             | Very low or absent IgG, IgA, IgM         | Normal                               |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome is caused by mutations in the *WAS* gene, leading to defective WASP protein. WASP is crucial for actin cytoskeleton regulation in hematopoietic cells. In T cells, this defect impairs immunological synapse formation, cell migration, and proliferation, leading to immunodeficiency. Clinically, it presents with the triad of eczema, thrombocytopenia (with small platelets), and recurrent infections. Remember to differentiate WAS from XLA (B cell defect, no eczema/thrombocytopenia) and SCID (more severe, often presents earlier with opportunistic infections). The key molecular defect in WAS is cytoskeletal rearrangement.
